#5926. Op-ed: Plug-in solar expands access to renewables, but needs a strong safety framework

By Michael Becker, Global Business Director, Energy Storage/Energy & Power, CSA | October 29, 2026

Access to plug-in solar is becoming increasingly available across North America. As more consumers explore technologies such as balcony solar and plug-in photovoltaic (PIPV) systems, these emerging solutions have the potential to make renewable energy more accessible and support broader participation in the energy transition. Solar remains one of the most accessible and promising renewable energy tools being added to the grid, and plug-in solar products may help expand that access to consumers who have traditionally faced economic barriers to participation.

As adoption grows, however, products designed to be convenient and accessible must still be treated as electrical products that interact with a building’s infrastructure. Product design, connectors, wiring, system integration and installation practices all play an important role in safety and performance. Expanding access and maintaining confidence in emerging technologies are complementary goals, and achieving both will require continued attention to the frameworks that support responsible deployment.

Clear safety standards, code-compliant installation practices and appropriate regulatory requirements will be essential to helping ensure that innovation can reach more consumers safely and responsibly. Solar-related issues are often associated not with the panels themselves, but with connectors, wiring and improper installation practices. A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet. Responsible deployment of PIPV systems depends on several complementary elements working together: products evaluated to appropriate safety standards, compliance with the National Electrical Code (NEC), qualified installation practices, and applicable state and local requirements.

No single element alone can provide the framework needed to support broad and responsible adoption.

An important emerging component of that framework is UL 3700, which is helping establish a product-safety benchmark specifically for plug-in photovoltaic systems. UL 3700 can provide manufacturers with a clearer benchmark for product development and evaluation, while giving regulators greater confidence as they consider how plug-in PV should be addressed within electrical codes and regulatory frameworks. Continued industry involvement in the development of UL 3700 will also be important as the standard evolves toward broader consensus and adoption. At the same time, UL 3700 is only one part of the overall approach. It does not replace NEC compliance, qualified installation practices or applicable state and local requirements that govern the deployment of electrical systems.

Emerging safety benchmarks are already moving from concept to practice. CSA has certified a microinverter to UL 3700, providing an early example of how the standard can be applied to products entering the market. The certification demonstrates how a consistent benchmark can be applied as new technologies enter the market, while helping support confidence among regulators, manufacturers and consumers as plug-in solar continues to develop.

The regulatory landscape for PIPV continues to evolve, with proposed changes to the NEC, state laws and local codes and ordinances aimed at facilitating the safe installation of these systems. As the industry works to establish appropriate requirements, keeping standards, codes and regulatory frameworks aligned with technological advancement will be critical to supporting the growth of PIPV products. Well-aligned requirements can help create a pathway for innovation while maintaining consistency in how emerging technologies are evaluated and deployed.

Technology itself continues to advance rapidly. Improvements in inverter technology are helping make solar more affordable while enabling greater integration of renewable energy into the grid. As inverters become more energy-dense and cost-effective, they can help lower the overall cost of PV systems and support broader adoption. New inverter capabilities can also provide valuable grid-support functions that help integrate more renewable resources and contribute to a more flexible and resilient electric grid. As these technologies evolve, standards, codes and regulatory requirements must continue evolving alongside them so that innovation and deployment can advance together.

Ultimately, greater accessibility brings greater responsibility. As plug-in solar technologies become easier for consumers to adopt, the safety frameworks that support them must keep pace with innovation. By developing appropriate product standards, maintaining NEC-compliant installation practices, utilizing qualified installers and advancing state and local requirements alongside technological progress, the industry can help expand access to renewable energy while maintaining the confidence of consumers, manufacturers, regulators installers and other stakeholders.

The statement “A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet.” is, in and of itself a misconception. If the plugin systems are safely designed, that is exactly what they are meant to prov ide the consumer–a solar panel with a plug they can connect to a household outlet. The fact that several millions of such systems have already been plugged in around the world without incident amply demonstrates their safety. All the hand-wringing about safety seems to be originating with existing vested interests that stand to loose significant revenues built on the existing over-complicated and over-regulated systems.

Helpful safety framing. One practical reader check is to separate product evaluation from deployment permission: UL 3700 does not replace NEC compliance, installation instructions, or state/local and utility rules, and effective dates vary by state. PluginForSolar.com maintains a U.S. state-by-state guide plus exact-system readiness checks so readers can verify those layers before buying.

#5921. How North Carolina Democrats flipped several House seats in historically GOP districts

Supporters watch Democratic senatorial candidate Roy Cooper speak during an election night event in Raleigh, N.C. on November 3, 2026. A.M STEWART/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

The Associated Press has called five U.S. House races in North Carolina for Democrats, all of them in districts drawn to favor Republican candidates.

Of those districts, four are flipping from Republican control.

As of Thursday, the AP had called nine of the state's 14 House seats for Democrats and none for Republicans. It's a startling result for a delegation that went into Tuesday with 10 Republicans and four Democrats.

Races called Thursday that flipped from Republican to Democratic control included:

North Carolina's Congressional map was redrawn in 2023 to significantly favor Republican candidates, and these four districts performed that way in 2024. The 14th District was the closest in that election, with Moore winning by about 16%, while the 6th District was the least competitive, with McDowell winning by more than 38%.

One of the candidates vying to replace Hudson as NRCC chair is U.S. Rep. Brian Jack, a Georgia Republican.

He wrote in a letter to fellow House Republicans that fellow GOP lawmakers may have contributed to the challenging environment. Former Gov. Roy Cooper, a Democrat, won the race for North Carolina's open U.S. Senate seat by nearly 20%.

"When Senate Republicans withdrew support from North Carolina weeks ago, they created a cascade that likely swept away many of our colleagues from that state," Jack wrote.

The AP also called northeastern North Carolina's 1st Congressional District for incumbent Democrat Rep. Don Davis on Thursday.

That's a district legislative Republicans redrew last year in an effort to make it easier for GOP candidates to win there, pulling Republican-leaning counties from the state's 3rd Congressional District to do so.

Yet as of Thursday, Davis was leading Republican challenger Laurie Buckhout by more than 10,000 votes. That's a larger margin than he won by in 2024.

North Carolina's 3rd Congressional District is one of five in the state that remain uncalled, with incumbent Republican Greg Murphy leading Democrat Raymond Smith by just 233 votes in a district where Democrats didn't even run a candidate in 2024. That race is likely headed for a recount.

In the 5th Congressional District, Democratic challenger Chuck Hubbard is leading 11-term Republican incumbent Virginia Foxx by 99 votes — that's another race that may go to a recount.

If all of the remaining results hold, the state's congressional delegation would flip from 10 Republicans and four Democrats to 10 Democrats and four Republicans.

#5917. DTE utility program funded a 132-MW Michigan solar project

DTE Energy’s 132-MW Fish Creek Solar Park near Sheridan, Michigan, is now operational. The $268 million development was fully funded by customers who voluntarily participate in DTE’s MIGreenPower program. The solar park can generate enough energy to power approximately 30,000 homes.

“Our customers expect us to keep the lights on and keep bills as affordable as possible, and bringing new generation like Fish Creek online is part of how we deliver on that commitment,” said Matt Paul, president and COO of DTE Electric. “Fish Creek adds more Michigan-made energy to our system while supporting local jobs, generating new tax revenue and delivering lasting benefits for the communities that helped make this project possible.”

Fish Creek will generate tax revenue, supporting regional infrastructure, such as schools, first responders, road maintenance and veteran and senior citizen services. The solar park also created hundreds of local jobs during construction and will provide participating landowners with supplemental income.

In addition, Bushnell and Evergreen townships each received $330,000 through Michigan’s Renewables Ready Communities Award, a grant available to local governments that host large-scale renewable energy projects. The townships plan to invest the funding in town hall and fire station renovations, along with other community improvements.

Fish Creek is part of DTE’s broader plan to meet Michigan’s growing energy needs with a mix of resources. DTE currently has 20 wind parks and 37 solar parks across Michigan, which together generate enough clean energy to power nearly 1 million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan includes an additional 15 GW of renewable energy by 2042.

#5913. Melania Trump touts rare agreement she brokered between Russia and Ukraine

WASHINGTON, DC - NOVEMBER 05: First lady Melania Trump gives hand written letters to Russian special envoy and head of the Russian Direct Investment Fund Kirill Dmitrievon (L) and Ukrainian human rights Ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets (C) to deliver to their presidents during an event on a temporary Russia-Ukraine ceasefire at the White House on November 5, 2026 in Washington, DC. Samuel Corum/Getty Images hide caption

First lady Melania Trump hosted a rare meeting between Russian and Ukrainian officials at the White House to recognize a temporary ceasefire in southeastern Ukraine.

The six-day pause from the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky comes as the administration ramps up efforts to secure an agreement to end the war in Ukraine.

"We have chosen humanity over war," the first lady said. "Undeniably, the lives of innocent people must transcend the divisions of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace."

Trump has accomplished something that has so far eluded her husband - helping broker a temporary ceasefire and bringing together representatives of the two sides in acknowledgement of the human costs of the war.

The White House sees it as a diplomatic breakthrough, but some European analysts worry the efforts can backfire.

The temporary reprieve, which expired on Sunday, enabled humanitarian aid to reach the city while allowing hundreds of residents to be evacuated.

Kirill Dmitriev, Russian President Vladimir Putin's special envoy, thanked the first lady. He said Putin personally gave the instructions to ensure the successful implementation of the ceasefire.

"On behalf of President Putin, on behalf of the people of the Russian Federation, we would like to thank first lady Melania Trump for her distinguished humanitarian role in resolving a key situation and resolving this humanitarian issue," he said.

Ukrainian human rights ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets presented Melania Trump with an award for her humanitarian work. The Order of Princess Olha, 1st Class, is Ukraine's highest award for women.

"I have only one request from all Ukrainian nation," Lubinets said, "please continue doing your wonderful work and support people."

The event comes just days before the U.S. leads a new round of peace talks between Russia and Ukraine in Abu Dhabi next week.

President Trump also met with Dmitriev at the White House.

Trump told reporters that the sides were close to reaching a deal.

"It's going to be an agreement. It's going to be a big agreement, ‌and ⁠it's going to involve more than Ukraine," he said. "I could even say it might involve all of Europe. It's ⁠going to be a big, beautiful agreement, and everyone's going to be thrilled."

Heather Conley, who worked on European issues in President George W. Bush's State Department, supports all efforts to help suffering Ukrainians, but she also said the U.S. must be careful not to normalize Russia's bad behavior.

"In this White House ceremony, we were honoring the Russians that were the cause of both the occupation of Oleshky, the starvation of Oleshky, and now we're honoring them for allowing aid to come into Oleshky," said Conley, who is now a senior fellow at the American Enterprise Institute. "The more we create this equivalency where the Russians and the Ukrainians are equal in this crisis, it is putting this thing upside down."

#5911. These are the races in the House and Senate that haven't been called yet

Democrats are poised to have a strong majority in the upcoming Congress. The final numbers are still being determined and several races are still outstanding.

A few in California are being contested by two candidates in the same party. So it's clear which party will hold the seat, even if it's not clear who will win. Others are too close to call or there aren't enough ballots counted to know which candidate will prevail. In many places in the country, it takes a while to count votes. That's normal and expected.

Here's where things stand in the races that are waiting to be called in the House by The Associated Press:

In the Senate, there are two races outstanding, Alaska and Mississippi. Because Alaska uses ranked choice voting and no candidate in the four-person race has achieved a 50% majority in the count, it may proceed to another round of counting later this month. Mississippi uses a more traditional electoral system but a large number of provisional ballots — called affidavit ballots in the state — are taking time to count. Less than 2,000 votes separate the top two candidates in that race.

#5909. RE+ will host another clean energy job fair this year

Job seekers hoping to launch or advance a career in clean energy can connect with employers and explore new opportunities at the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair on Nov. 18, at the Las Vegas Convention Center. Hosted by GRID Alternatives, the Women of Renewable Industries and Sustainable Energy (WRISE) and Green Jobs Board, the fair is free for job seekers, and everyone who registers will receive a complimentary pass to the RE+ Expo, North America’s largest clean energy industry event.

The job fair will run from 11 a.m. to 2 p.m. and attendees can connect with hiring companies. The fair is open to people of all skill levels.

“The future of clean energy is about building sustainable careers. When we connect job seekers with employers, we bridge the gap between opportunity and talent, strengthen the workforce, and ensure that the people who need access to these careers have a seat at the table.” said Lyric Darru, grants manager of workforce development at GRID Alternatives. “By connecting job seekers directly with employers and opening the door to the broader RE+ expo at no cost, we’re helping more people explore pathways into clean energy careers and ensuring the benefits of this growing industry reach more communities. We thank our partners and sponsors for their support in bringing this event to Las Vegas.”

In 2025, more than 37,000 people attended RE+, an event with more than 1,300 exhibitors from businesses in solar, energy storage, electric vehicles, grid technologies and other sectors. This year’s conference will take place November 16–19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center.

“WRISE is excited to co-host this important event with GRID again this year,” said Lauren Friedman, senior manager of programs and events at WRISE. “The continued success of the job fair year over year speaks to the lasting strength and enthusiasm for our industry. We’re glad to make these opportunities available to the WRISE membership and our larger clean energy community.”

Register for the RE+ Clean Energy Job Fair here.

#5908. Trump's arch gets a late-night green light. Will a judge stop construction?

Demonstrators stand at Memorial Circle to protest President Trump's proposed arch on Thursday night as a federal panel heard hours of testimony from concerned citizens. It voted to approve the structure. Tom Brenner/Getty Images hide caption

A federal panel packed with administration allies has given final approval to President Trump's proposed arch, after hearing nearly eight hours of impassioned pleas from veterans, lawmakers, residents and concerned citizens opposed to the structure.

The National Capital Planning Commission voted 8-4 to greenlight the arch, which would stand 250 feet tall on Memorial Circle, a traffic island on the bridge linking the Lincoln Memorial to Arlington National Cemetery. The Trump administration has said it could begin excavation work that same day, despite lacking authorization from Congress and an ongoing legal challenge.

A group of Vietnam War veterans and an architectural historian sued the government earlier this year, arguing that an arch is unlawful without Congressional approval, which the administration has not sought. The Democratic governments of D.C. and Virginia jointly filed a similar lawsuit last weekend.

D.C. District Court Judge Tanya Chutkan consolidated the lawsuits and asked the government to notify the court within an hour of commission approval. That suggests she could issue an 11th-hour preliminary injunction to block construction, as plaintiffs have requested.

The day started with a protest on Memorial Circle, where dozens of people spoke against the arch. The grass underfoot was already marked with white paint. Lawyers for the government told the court in a midday filing that the lines were painted on Saturday by its designated construction firm, seemingly without authorization from the National Park Service.

The commission meeting began at 1 p.m. It was held virtually because of the high volume of public testimony: 131 people were signed up to speak.

Just minutes into public testimony, the livestream crashed, lengthening the meeting by about an hour until it was restored. Over the course of nearly nine hours, commissioners heard from dozens of veterans and their relatives, Democratic lawmakers, historic preservation experts and concerned citizens from D.C. and beyond.

Among their many concerns, critics said the structure would obstruct an important view, insult service members buried on the hallowed ground of nearby Arlington National Cemetery and add a layer of risk to the notoriously complicated D.C. airspace. They have questions about its legality and its cost, which the administration has not confirmed.

Many see the arch as part of Trump's unilateral effort to memorialize himself in the nation's capital — and described Democrats' midterm sweep as a referendum against it.

"Leaving aside all of the legitimate concerns and challenges about the required approvals, funding, land use, single-source bidding, and peer review, the American people spoke this past Tuesday at the ballot box," said former D.C. resident Emi Yamauchi. "They said enough vanity projects, enough corruption, enough Reflecting Pool travesties."

Only a handful of speakers offered support for the arch, including Republican Reps. Andrew Clyde of Georgia and Brian Mast of Florida, both of whom are veterans.

Much of the night's testimony was heartfelt and personal: Many speakers had family members buried — or plans for their own future burials — at Arlington National Cemetery. Several implored the commissioners not to ruin their reputations by recording their support for the arch.

"[These are] warnings about the threat your own behavior poses to your political power, your moral authority, the legacy you leave to your descendants, and your eternal souls," said Stephen Eubank, some seven hours into the public testimony.

As the night wore on, a few speakers even questioned whether the commissioners — who were not shown on screen — were even listening. Many suggested the approval was a foregone conclusion, noting the political makeup of the commission and the fact that Trump-appointed Chair Mark Paoletta and several other members had expressed their support for the arch at the start of the meeting.

The commission started deliberating just before 10 p.m. The discussion lasted less than an hour.

The commissioners who spoke first were Trump allies and appointees. They thanked the speakers for taking the time to share their views, but — in brief comments — expressed their support for moving forward. They continued to cast the arch as a tribute to the nation and those who served, despite the broad pushback from individual veterans and veterans' groups.

The Trump allies dismissed concerns that the arch would violate D.C.'s restrictions on building heights as well as federal environmental and historic preservation laws. They maintained that Congress authorized the project when it approved a pair of 166-foot columns on Memorial Circle in 1925, which were never built.

And they pointed to recent claims by Trump and Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth that the monument will enhance national security — even though officials at the meeting confirmed that no such analysis or details were included in the final submission. In fact, Interior Department official Greg Wischer said "specific national security functions have been filed under seal" in court but that the National Park Service "has not been privy to any national defense preparations."

The most vocal criticism of the arch came from D.C. Council Chair Phil Mendelson, a Democrat who sits on the council. He asked questions about the arch's purpose — military and otherwise — earlier in the meeting and said, during deliberations, that he was dissatisfied with the government's contradictory answers.

"We cannot approve final site and building plans until we have the final site and building plan," Mendelson said. He introduced a motion to postpone the vote until those plans could be submitted, or until the commission's next meeting in early December. The motion failed by a vote of 4 to 8.

Vice Chair Stuart Levenbach, a Trump appointee, expressed concerns about delaying the vote because the "project proponent is under a time constraint and wants the decision today," suggesting the government wants to "commence building very quickly."

Within minutes, the commission had given the arch its green light. The Trump administration notified the court within half an hour.

Arch critics look to the courts and Congress for relief

Historic preservation groups wasted no time slamming both the process and the outcome.

Brent Leggs, the president and CEO of the National Trust for Historic Preservation, called the night's vote a "sham."

"It undermines the democratic process, violates federal statute, and insults our nation's veterans who have made the ultimate sacrifice," he said in a statement. "From the start, this Arch project has been driven by an artificial sense of urgency, while public opposition has been brushed aside."

Jimi Shaughnessy, veterans program manager for the National Parks Conservation Association, said in a statement that the organization is disappointed but not surprised. He vowed to "bring this to members of Congress, to ensure this arch is never built. Not here. Not ever."

Many Democratic lawmakers have vowed to stop — and investigate — the arch after they regain a Congressional majority in January.

Robert Weissman, the co-president of Public Citizen — the consumer rights nonprofit representing the veterans in their lawsuit — told NPR on Thursday that Congress doesn't need to act to stop the arch, since it never actively approved it. That decision, he said, will be up to the courts.

But, he added: "It would be fine if Congress takes additional measures to block construction, since we've seen this lawless administration circumvent the law and court orders so frequently."

Virginia Rep. Don Beyer has introduced a bill that would prohibit and defund the arch, and said Thursday he expects the number of co-sponsors to grow from 80 to at least 200 in the new year.

And Rep. Jared Huffman, D-Calif., the ranking Democrat on the House Natural Resources Committee, warned commissioners that companies and "officials working to advance a project not authorized by Congress will put themselves at legal risk."

He argued that the project violates federal environmental and historic preservation laws, which fall within the jurisdiction of the House committee he expects to soon lead.

"I urge all officials and firms involved with this illegal project to consider the legal hazards they may be facing by advancing it, because, in case you hadn't heard, we'll soon have a new Congress that takes the rule of law very seriously," Huffman said.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5888. Jackery Launches Explorer 100D Portable Power Station. Maximum Capability For Carryon Compatibility

Jackery just launched the Explorer 100D portable power station. This is Jackery’s smallest battery, giving a new level of versatility to power many of the devices people rely on most. Meanwhile, it integrates  the durability and safety people expect from Jackery’s larger models into a compact case.

The Explorer 100D has a capacity of 96 Wh (30,000mAh) which puts it just below the carryon limit for domestic and international flights. Meanwhile, up to 158 W of output is more than enough to charge a laptop. The 100D is compatible with advanced protocols including AVS, PD 3.1 and PPS, providing precise charging, particularly for Apple products.

The Explorer 100D measures 4.6” X 3.4” X 3.4”(11.6 X  8.3 cm X  8.5 cm), or about the size of a softball. It weighs 1.87 lbs. (850 g). This is a bit larger than the typical power bank, but much smaller than the typical solar generator. It is also smaller and lighter than the Explorer 100 Plus that it replaces, while also increasing in power output.

On the test sample model, I found the form factor very convenient. It is not small enough to fit in your pants pocket, but the rectangular case with rounded corners was easy to fit in a backpack or carryon bag. A cloth handle makes it easy to hold, and a shoulder strap is available. I had a credentials lanyard from a previous media event on hand, and it worked fine as a strap for the lightweight battery.

On the front of the battery are two 140 W USB-C ports and an 18 W USB-A port. The left USB-C port can both charge the battery and discharge to another device. The battery can also be discharged through the other two ports while it charges. The battery does not come with a charger, although a USB-C cable is provided. I was able to charge the Explorer 100D on a wide range of chargers, although lower powered chargers take a while to charge the large capacity battery.

The front display default shows battery charge percentage as well as input and output power. However, you can switch the display to also show power output to each port or battery health, cycles and temperature.

Unlike some of Jackery’s larger batteries, the 100D does not have cooling fans or vents in the case. The LFP battery is rated for 3000 full charge cycles, good for 8 years of daily use. This is far more durable than many Li-ion power banks, which commonly only offer a few hundred charge cycles. It is also a 50% improvement over the previous Explorer 100 Plus model that it replaces. Of note, while this model does comply with China’s strict CCC (China Compulsory Certification), the logo was not printed on the test model I received, although that may change in the future. Without the logo present, it could be taken away if travelling in China. However, the battery did have FCC and SGS certification printed on it, which are more relevant to most US customers.

The portable battery can function as a solar generator by accepting direct solar input up to 100 W. This makes it a bit different than many power banks. Solar panels connect through the USB-C charge port. which may require the purchase of an adaptor, depending on the panel used. Jackery’s lightweight, folding SolarSaga 100 Air could charge the battery in less than two hours. The battery can also take 12 V or 24V input from a car, boat or RV. Using a traditional USB-C charger, the Explorer 100D can accept up to 140 W of input power.

The Explorer 100D  test model came with 30% charge and reached a full charge in about an hour. While the battery was charging, I was able to charge my phone and another power bank off the output ports while the battery was charging. I initially charged the battery off the HomePower 1000 Plus V2, which has a 140 W USB-C output. With similar styling, it looked like a baby of the larger battery when placed on top. Of course, the battery would take longer to charge on lower power. However, it could be used to accumulate charge over time and discharge more quickly for a device like a laptop that needs more power than what may be available from a charge jack at a hotel room or airport.

While the battery will often be used to charge phones, the Explorer 100D ran my T-Mobile Wi-Fi router all day, with capacity left to charge my phone and tablet. When used with a laptop with a USB-C charge port, the small battery could power a remote office.

I was also able to run a small personal fan off of the Explorer 100D. With peak power draw at 5 watts, it didn’t pose much of a challenge for the battery. This would come in handy at an outdoor event or while fishing on a hot day.

On Halloween, I used the battery to power an old Bluetooth speaker for hours of handing out treats, while keeping our phones charged. The black and orange design fits well with the holiday decorations. By the end of the night, the battery still had over 90% capacity.

I also used the battery on a trip to an apple harvest festival. While charging ports were in short supply in the car, the Explorer 100D kept phone and camera batteries topped up. The rubber feet kept the battery from shifting around when placed on the center console.

The Jackery Explorer 100D launches as a two-unit bundle for $179.99 at select Costco warehouse locations from the start of November 2026. Individual units will then become available at Walmart for $99 on November 15th, just in time for holiday travel.

Frost & Sullivan recently named Jackery the World’s No.1 Solar Generator Brand. Jackery leads in solar generator sales volume globally and has pioneered multiple innovations in portable power. The Explorer 100D is the latest example. The company has a focus on providing solutions for customer needs with durable products that have exceeded my expectations in testing. After trying the Explorer 100D, I also find it to be more useful than I originally expected, using it in a wide range of applications. While the Explorer 100D clearly is not going to solve all of the world’s energy problems, it provides another power option that meets the needs of many customers while integrating well with Jackery’s expanding range of solar and energy storage products. As Steven Wang, CEO of Jackery Americas puts it:

Power support is often most effective when it’s highly portable and backed by comprehensive compatibility and a long lifespan. Developed for peak productivity, the Explorer 100D represents years of honed design and feature implementation, while maintaining focus on the top-quality engineering and unmatched consistency that make Jackery portable power stations must-haves for work and travel. Thanks to that collection of refined expertise, it’s among the longest-lasting ultra-portable batteries ever.

I look forward to putting the Explorer 100D to the test during some upcoming travel over the next few months. I also look forward to seeing what Jackery comes up with next in versatile, portable power.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5886. Germany’s Hydrogen Pipeline Reached Marl. Its Market Still Needs to Add Up.

Germany’s hydrogen backbone achieved a genuine milestone on August 4, 2026. RWE and its partners announced that renewable hydrogen produced during commissioning at Lingen had reached Marl Chemical Park, roughly 120 kilometres away. Electrolysers, converted natural gas pipelines and industrial receiving infrastructure were working together.

The GET H2 consortium deserves credit for getting the equipment operating. But completing a hydrogen pipeline and establishing an economically competitive market for the hydrogen moving through it are different achievements.

The announcement did not disclose the quantity received at Marl, the price paid by an industrial customer or which existing hydrogen production was displaced. The English release describes hydrogen being available for industrial use, while the German release says it was used. Neither provides the industrial mass balance needed to determine the actual emissions benefit. The network also contained hydrogen from earlier commissioning activities, which makes tracing individual molecules through the pipeline less useful than measuring what changed at the receiving industrial facilities.

Marl is hardly a new hydrogen customer. The chemical park has used hydrogen for decades in integrated production processes. Providing another source may be useful, especially where renewable hydrogen replaces fossil-derived supply, but existing demand does not automatically become commercially viable demand for a considerably more expensive feedstock.

The larger infrastructure ambition makes that distinction important. Germany’s approved hydrogen core network covers 9,040 kilometres and has expected investment costs of €18.9 billion. Much of the network will use converted natural gas pipelines. These are approved plans and anticipated investments, not evidence that the entire network has been built or that future customers have committed to using it.

There are genuine industrial commitments. RWE has a 15-year agreement with TotalEnergies to supply approximately 30,000 tonnes of renewable hydrogen annually to the Leuna refinery from 2030. That provides a named customer, volume and contract period. The price is undisclosed, and European fuel regulations help create demand for renewable hydrogen in refining.

That is a much stronger commercial signal than commissioning a pipeline. The question is how many comparable customers exist, what they can afford and whether their businesses will continue using hydrogen over the decades required to recover infrastructure investments.

The wider European outlook has become less optimistic. ENTSOG’s October 2026 draft assessment reduced its 2030 hydrogen demand assumption from 620 TWh to 335 TWh, a 46% reduction from its 2024 assessment. Its 2040 assumption fell 33%, from 1,929 TWh to 1,296 TWh. These are changes to European planning scenarios, not measurements of declining actual hydrogen consumption, but they matter when infrastructure economics depend on demand growth.

My central model of hydrogen supplied through the Lingen system produces an illustrative delivered cost of about €9.06/kg after assumed capital support, compared with a €3/kg conventional hydrogen benchmark. Those are scenario values, not RWE’s actual costs or the undisclosed price paid at Marl. The gap illustrates the challenge facing industrial manufacturers selling chemicals into competitive international markets.

Hydrogen remains an important industrial feedstock. Well-located connections between producers and customers with durable demand may be worthwhile. Germany needs evidence that the customers and products can support the investment, rather than assuming that building enough pipelines will bring the market into existence.

#5882. PJM’s Emergency Order Should Be a Wake-Up Call for Michigan: Build More Power Before the Next Crisis

By Terence Parker, Michigan resident and Solar Mounts LLC team member

The U.S. Department of Energy’s October 12, 2026, emergency order directing PJM Interconnection to use additional generating resources should be a wake-up call for Michigan and the entire PJM region. Effective October 13–16, the order authorizes specified power plants to operate as needed for reliability and allows backup generators at large facilities to help reduce grid demand during emergencies.

This does not mean blackouts are inevitable. However, it highlights the serious challenges facing our electricity system and the need to add capacity before emergency measures become routine.

As a Michigan-based company, Solar Mounts LLC, a designer and manufacturer of commercial and utility-scale solar mounting systems, recognizes the potential implications for electricity prices, air quality, local businesses, and families across the state.

Rising Costs and the Consequences of Delayed Generation

PJM’s capacity auctions have exposed the difficulty of securing enough electricity for future demand. Prices reached $329.17 per megawatt-day for the 2026–2027 delivery year and $333.44 for 2027–2028, with the latter auction falling short of PJM’s stated reliability requirement.

Source: Vicinity Energy’s Analysis of PJM Capacity Auctions

Capacity costs are only one component of electricity bills, but rising prices signal growing pressure on consumers. We need more power before shortages force increasingly expensive emergency responses.

The October 2026 order permits designated generating units to exceed specified operating limits when necessary for reliability. It also authorizes PJM to direct backup generation at data centers and other large facilities, potentially including diesel generators as a last resort.

Running fossil-fuel plants more frequently can increase emissions, depending on the units involved. Operating diesel generators will worsen local noise pollution and expose operators to high diesel prices. If these measures become more common, we should ask why cleaner generation and storage are not coming online faster.

Weather Uncertainty Makes Planning Urgent

NOAA’s October 15, 2026, winter outlook anticipates warmer-than-average conditions across much of the northern United States, including the Midwest, influenced by a strong El Niño.

Source: NOAA’s 2026–2027 U.S. Winter Outlook

Seasonal forecasts cannot predict every period of extreme demand. Aging infrastructure, maintenance, changing weather, and growing electricity consumption reinforce the need for advance planning. Temporary authority to run additional fossil-fuel resources cannot replace long-term investment in generation.

Solar, Wind, and Batteries Can Help Close the Gap

Utility-scale solar, wind, and battery storage can help meet demand on timelines that many conventional generation projects cannot match. Suitable solar projects may be built within 18 months, depending on permitting, equipment, and interconnection. New nuclear facilities generally require substantially longer development periods.

Solar and wind will reduce emissions when they displace electricity from fossil-fuel generators. Batteries store surplus electricity for periods of higher demand. Note: actual emissions benefits depend on which generators are displaced and when electricity is delivered.

Faster interconnection reviews are also essential. Qualified solar projects shouldn’t remain in queues for years because of avoidable administrative delays. Better coordination among PJM, utilities, regulators, developers, and state governments, alongside necessary transmission upgrades, will bring capacity online sooner.

Solar Development and Agriculture Can Coexist

At Solar Mounts LLC, we witness concerns about installing solar on productive farmland here in rural Calhoun County (home to Solar Mounts LLC). However, well-designed projects can include restoration plans that allow agricultural use to resume when leases end. Solar leases can also provide farmers with supplemental income when crop prices fail to keep pace with production costs.

Agrivoltaic systems combine electricity generation with compatible crops or livestock. Thoughtful design and community engagement can balance renewable energy development with agricultural productivity, rural traditions, and farmland preservation.

Opportunities extend beyond prime farmland. Parking lots, commercial properties, brownfields, landfills, and other underutilized sites can accommodate solar generation. Solar carports provide electricity, shade, and weather protection, while community solar expands access for people unable or unwilling to install their own panels.

Solar Mounts LLC supports practical, site-appropriate solutions that expand generation while respecting farmers, landowners, and rural communities.

Hyperscale Data Centers Must Help Provide Their Own Power

Growing data center demand, particularly in Virginia and other parts of PJM’s territory, is adding pressure to an already constrained grid. Consequently, developers of new hyperscale facilities should demonstrate how they will secure adequate electricity and pay their fair share of supporting infrastructure. Dedicated generation, renewable energy contracts, batteries, flexible demand, and grid upgrades can all contribute.

Requiring every facility to build a gas-fired peaker plant is not necessarily the best answer. Gas turbines face equipment and supply-chain constraints, while additional fossil-fuel generation carries emissions and operating costs. A combination of solutions tailored to each location is more practical.

Existing facilities should also reduce peak demand where possible. Energy efficiency remains one of the most immediate ways businesses can limit electricity consumption.

Solar Mounts LLC sees the opportunity to expand commercial and utility-scale solar infrastructure across Michigan and the country. We encourage this expansion by supporting faster approvals, better grid planning, and a serious commitment to adding power generation and energy storage before shortages worsen.

Reliable electricity underpins our economy, jobs, and daily lives. Repeated emergency orders, rising capacity costs, and greater reliance on high-emission generation are not sound long-term strategies.

We shouldn’t wait for the next crisis to build the power system we already know we need.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5881. Swarm Of Electric Trucks Coming To US (Battery Swapping Included?)

Tesla CEO Elon Musk famously ditched the idea of EV battery swapping back in 2015 after a brief experiment. That pretty much laid the idea to rest here in the US, but other EV stakeholders soldiered on. The latest example is the Australian firm Janus Electric, which is bringing its Class 8 heavy duty electric truck conversions to North America. Janus offers a battery-as-a-service model that includes both charging and swapping…so, is battery swapping finally coming here to the US?.

Battery swapping for two-wheelers and larger EVs is commonplace in some overseas markets, with industry leader Nio being one example. So far, though, swapping stations for e-bikes are the only sign of emerging activity in the US.

Musk’s opinion aside, a modern battery swapping station can change a spent electric car battery for a fresh one in just a few minutes, or less time than it takes to drive through an automated car wash. On the other hand, considering today’s EV fast charging technology, battery swapping is somewhat less relevant to passenger cars and other light duty vehicles. Access to home charging is another factor that can limit the appeal of driving to a battery swapping station for a refresh.

Electric trucks, though, are another kettle of fish. Every minute counts in the fast paced transportation industry. Waiting in line for an EV charging station to free up is a non-starter, and then there’s the charging time. Even the much-heralded Tesla Semi Class 8 truck can take hours to reach a full charge overnight, though the truck is reported to achieve 60-70% charge in as little as 30-45 minutes at fast charging stations.

The time factor explains why battery swapping for trucks is appearing in overseas markets. The seeds of change have also begun to sprout up in the US, where the the diesel-to-electric conversion field is beginning to offer some opportunities. Last summer, for example, the US startup Revoy (formerly SixWheels) announced a swappable battery retrofit for Class 8 diesel trucks. The retrofit consists of a battery unit inserted between the cab and the trailer, which can be swapped out in a matter of minutes. The unit does not replace the engine, but it reportedly cuts diesel emissions by up to 85%, with a 95% fuel savings aided by regenerative braking.

The US Army is also dipping a toe in the conversion field with “idling kits,” which are batteries powerful enough to run on-board equipment for military vehicles in near silence, without the giveaway noise and heat signature involved in idling the vehicle’s engine. No word yet on whether or not swap-outs will be part of the picture, but the technology pieces are in place.

So much for the current state of affairs. Last week a full diesel-to-electric conversion opportunity suddenly appeared when the Canadian lithium-ion battery specialist Electrovaya announced a new $26 million diesel-to-electric supply agreement with the Janus that leverages Electrovaya’s proprietary “Infinity Battery Technology, a durable system compatible with daily swapping cycles for Class 8 trucks.

The $26 million agreement represents just the initial phase of orders for the US and Canadian markets. “Electrovaya has also agreed to invest approximately $1.4 million in Janus for up to 4.2 million shares, together with up to 6.3 million warrants,” Electrovaya noted, indicating a long term partnership allowing plenty of room for scaleup.

“Janus’s approach converts existing diesel prime movers to battery-electric operation and pairs them with swappable battery packs and Janus Charge & Change Stations, allowing fleets to exchange batteries in minutes rather than wait hours to recharge, and to electrify without the capital cost of purchasing new trucks,” Electrovaya explained (see additional coverage on CleanTechnica  here and here).

Janus’s footprint in North American is still tiny, at 118 truck conversion orders and commitments in the US and Canada combined. However, the firm’s business with Electrovaya should help ramp up the activity. Janus has already begun deliveries to Electrovaya, and Electrovaya has announced a major expansion of its North American manufacturing footprint. The showpiece is a production facility in Jamestown, New York covering lithium-ion battery cells, modules, and packs.  Start of operations is schedule for sometime next year.

With US President Donald Trump’s war in Iran continuing to elevate diesel costs, both Janus Electric and Electrovaya smell an end to the partisan war on EVs that rocked the US market after Trump took office in January of 2025. “Electrovaya’s expanding North American manufacturing capabilities will be important as we scale,” said Janus CEO and Managing Director Ben Hutt in a press statement.

“The investment is a strong vote of confidence in our technology and approach to electrifying heavy transport,” Hutt added.

The CEO of Electrovaya, Dr. Raj Das Gupta, also underscored the potential for rapid scaleup. “Janus has developed a practical solution to one of the major challenges facing heavy transport, enabling diesel-powered trucks to be converted to electric without compromising their commercial operations,” Gupta emphasized.

“Our strategic investment, together with the warrants, aligns us closely with Janus’s long-term growth,” Gupta also explained, with warrants referring to up to 6.3 million in contracts for future stock transactions.

If you’re wondering how much traction Electrovaya and Janus will gain in the US electric truck conversion space, that’s a good question considering Trump’s on-again, off-again trade wars with the upstairs neighbors.

The factory in New York State should help push matters along. As if on cue, over the weekend the White House also signaled that Trump is suddenly willing to call off his tariff dogs, potentially leading to a trade deal with Canda that could help accelerate activity in the US electric truck conversion field, among other areas.

On the other hand, the negotiations are described as achieving an early stage “concept” of a deal, which makes it seem more like a weak effort to gin up voter support for the Republican ticket ahead of Election Day on November 3. The concept of a deal could just as easily de-materialize by the morning of November 4.

Deal or no deal, Trump is scheduled to leave office in January of 2029 at the latest — peacefully this time, one hopes — opening up space for a new era of stability and mutual benefit in US-Canada trade relations.

In the meantime, keep an eye on Electrovaya for more activity in the electrification field, with or without help from the US market. In addition to supplying batteries for electric truck conversion, the company anticipates business in the fields of material handling, robotics, defense, and AI data center infrastructure.

Photo: The Australian firm Janus Electric has hooked up with Canada’s Electrovaya to accelerate the electric truck conversion field in North America, regardless of the sharp U-turn in US electrification policy (cropped, courtesy of Janus).

#5878. As Diesel Costs Surge for Farmers & Rural Businesses, Solar Offers a Lifeline

The cost of diesel fuel rose nearly 33% from 2025 to 2026, with some countries experiencing even higher price spikes. For many of the world’s rural farmers, processors, and small businesses, solar-powered equipment offers an attractive alternative.

On a dry hillside in Chamwino, central Tanzania, mango farmers used to watch their trees struggle through the long months without rain. Irrigation was technically possible: A diesel pump could lift water from a nearby borehole. But in practice, fuel was too expensive and too unpredictable for smallholders to run the system often enough. When diesel prices rose, farmers irrigated less, production dropped, and most households stayed locked into low-yield farming and razor-thin margins.

In 2021, a solar-powered pump and drip irrigation system replaced the diesel setup as part of a pilot project. Within one season, farmers reported that their mango production and average income from mango sales roughly doubled, as farmers could irrigate beyond the rainy season and manage their orchards more intensively. The technology change was simple—solar instead of diesel. But for farmers, it meant shifting from a fuel prone to wild price swings to an energy source they could rely on and afford.

Chamwino is not an isolated case. Across Africa and other regions, farmers, processors, and rural businesses are among the most exposed to diesel price spikes, like those happening right now. Because diesel powers irrigation pumps, generators, mills, and refrigerators, every price jump shows up as higher operating costs, thinner margins, and greater risk.

Distributed clean energy is emerging as one of the most practical ways to break that dependence.

While access to electricity has expanded significantly over the last 10 years, the reality is that diesel still powers much of rural life in places like sub-Saharan Africa. Only 33% of the rural population can access electricity. Even among those with power connections, frequent outages and poor quality have led farmers, processors, retailers, mines and telecommunications companies to rely on diesel generators for backup or primary power. An Energy for Growth Hub study estimated that 150–190 gigawatts (GW) of distributed diesel generator capacity was operating across Africa in 2024, compared with 268 GW of grid-connected capacity. Removing South Africa from the equation, Sub-Saharan Africa had nearly 1.5 times as much diesel generator capacity as grid-connected capacity. And diesel capacity exceeded grid capacity in at least 26 African countries.

The war on Iran has exposed the vulnerability created by this “shadow power system.” The average global retail diesel price exceeded $1.50 per liter in the second quarter of 2026, 20% higher than the previous three months and 32.9% higher than a year earlier. In Africa, retail diesel prices rose by 25% on average across 42 countries between mid-January and mid-June 2026.

The regional average masks sharp differences between countries. In some, subsidies, tax cuts, and administered fuel prices have cushioned consumers from rising international prices, but at a cost to government budgets, especially in fuel-importing economies with limited fiscal space. Elsewhere, consumers have faced much larger increases at the pump. Diesel prices increased by 86% in Nigeria and more than 50% in Tanzania and Ethiopia from January to June 2026.

The effects extend beyond the cost of running generators. Diesel powers agricultural machinery, irrigation pumps, mills and refrigerators. Diesel-fueled road transport connects farms with suppliers and consumer markets. The impacts of the Iran conflict are already rippling through interconnected energy, fertilizer, trade and logistics networks, raising the costs of agricultural production and food distribution across Africa and elsewhere. Fertilizer prices have increased by 30-45% in several African markets since the conflict began, increasing the risk that farmers will reduce application rates, switch to other crops or miss their planting windows.

The effects are felt most acutely at the local level. For example, members of a women’s groundnut processing cooperative in Nigeria reported that rising fuel and fertilizer costs increased the price of raw groundnuts and groundnut oil by 33%. In Myanmar, which imports more than 90% of its fuel and relies predominantly on road transport, higher diesel prices have increased the cost of moving food and agricultural inputs, and have led to rising rice prices for consumers.

We already have better alternatives. Many locations are struggling with diesel-based irrigation, cooling and backup power, but solar and other distributed renewables can provide more affordable and reliable electricity than diesel-dependent systems.

The shift is already gaining momentum. In Africa, 17 GW of solar capacity will be installed in 2026 — a 45% increase from 2025, equivalent to approximately 100,000 panels installed each day. Much of the recent growth is happening outside large power plants: Roughly three-quarters of Africa’s solar growth between 2023 and 2025 came from distributed systems, as households and businesses sought alternatives to unreliable supply and diesel generation.

The benefits are already visible for some who have made the switch.

Ethiopia’s Berbere district, or woreda, is surrounded by four rivers, yet for years farmers could not use that water for irrigation. Limited access to electricity and the high cost and unreliability of petrol and diesel made pumping water from rivers to fields impractical, keeping production closely tied to rain-fed maize and teff. In 2025, WRI and partners installed two solar-powered water pumps to lift river water to irrigated plots. In the first production season, households involved in the project earned an average $709 from onion cultivation alone, compared with around $207 annually from maize and teff before the solar-powered pumps were installed. Farmers also reported being able to harvest more than once a year, plant surplus crops such as onions and chilies to increase their incomes, and make new investments in livestock and home improvements.

Similarly, when Kenya experienced its longest nationwide power blackout in 2023, one Nairobi hospital maintained critical services thanks to its solar-plus-battery storage system. For rural clinics and county hospitals, similar systems can ensure power even when the grid fails, without tying budgets to volatile fuel prices and expensive back-up diesel generators.

There’s huge potential for distributed clean energy to further break diesel dependence and improve livelihoods in places like rural Africa and elsewhere. Energy Access Explorer, an open online geospatial platform hosted by the WRI Polsky Energy Center, lets planners examine solar potential alongside grid networks, health facilities, schools, and farming communities. Bringing these layers together can help governments and practitioners identify where clean power could cost-effectively serve livelihoods and services that currently depend on diesel.

For example, Energy Access Explorer is already informing electrification planning in Ethiopia. The Ministry of Water and Energy, working with the World Bank, has used the platform to prioritize off-grid electrification through a results-based financing program. Our latest analysis reveals that about 12.6 million people in Ethiopia live in areas with high agricultural activity and high solar potential, but little access to grid-connected electricity. These areas could be assessed for solar-powered irrigation, processing, cooling and other services that could benefit from distributed clean power.

A Uganda analysis applies the same approach to health services. The map below shows 243 health facilities and approximately 4.6 million people living in areas with limited or no connection to electricity. Yet these areas do receive abundant sunlight, making them suitable for solar power. Used alongside data on facility electricity demand, existing supply reliability, and roof or land availability, the maps can help prioritize clean power investments for rural health services.

As diesel prices continue to swing wildly with every conflict and crisis, farmers, processors, and rural businesses pay the price, alongside governments that try to keep costs low through subsidies. It’s time for energy ministers, donors and financiers to look closely at where distributed clean power can viably replace diesel and direct their investments there. The latest Tracking SDG 7 report estimates that $21 billion is needed by 2030 to provide off-grid solar to the 398 million people for whom it is the least-cost electricity option—approximately 6 times the sector’s recent investment level!

Moving from energy crisis to energy security is ultimately about knowing where clean energy truly changes livelihoods. For many of the world’s rural farmers, processors, and small businesses, that starts with swapping diesel for solar.

By Dimitris Mentiss, Lead, Energy Access Explorer

This article is informed by research and insights from the WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition. The Center harnesses analytical power, convening ability, and global expertise to help orchestrate a transition to a clean, abundant, affordable, and reliable energy future by overcoming critical barriers.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5874. The ETS Investment Engine: Scaling Europe’s Strategic E-fuel Market for Shipping & Aviation

Making the ETS a strategic investment tool for aviation and shipping

The EU Emissions Trading System (ETS) review proposal published in July 2026 is intended to make the EU’s most vital climate policy mechanism fit for its 2040 climate targets. It also carries the ambition to turn the ETS into an ‘’engine for innovation and investment”.

This policy brief looks at the investment dimension of the ETS proposal with respect to the aviation and maritime sectors. They are major contributors to the ETS revenue generation. We argue, however, that the proposed framework is not fit for purpose to adequately support clean fuel investments in these hard-to-abate sectors.

The ETS Review is an opportunity to set up a tailor-made investment tool to de-risk and boost the production and uptake of e-fuels, using the ETS funds from aviation and shipping. Early financial support is crucial for the development of a domestic e-fuels value chain, Europe’s geostrategic autonomy and the EU’s industrial position in the global competition for clean technologies. RFNBOs are the most scalable fuels needed to reach the targets under FuelEU Maritime and ReFuelEU Aviation.

#5871. More of the Same Expensive Dependence on Fossil Fuels in TEP and APS 2026 Energy Plans

PHOENIX — Arizona utilities Tucson Electric Power Company (TEP) and Arizona Public Service (APS) filed their 2026 Integrated Resource Plans (IRPs) on Friday that evaluate how utilities will obtain energy for the next 15 years. While their plans include new renewable energy and storage, they continue to spend on fossil fuels, building new gas, and in the case of APS, extending coal use. Instead of facilitating a timely transition to affordable, clean energy, both utilities — and especially APS — continue an expensive dependence on fossil fuels that keep ratepayers in a dangerous cycle, paying the price for decades.

Arizonans already experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country. Yet, instead of easing energy burden and saving its customers millions of dollars, APS prolongs costly coal operations at the Four Corners coal plant, worsening climate impacts, increasing air pollution, and threatening public health.

Of all the state’s utilities, APS has the most planned fossil gas, adding over 4,000 MW of gas by 2041. APS’s gas projects include the planned Desert Sun Power Plant and the Redhawk Expansion Project, which includes constructing eight new methane gas-fired generating turbines in Maricopa County. It has also signed a coal supply contract extension to resuscitate its Four Corners Plant, abandoning APS’s previous plan to exit the plant in 2031 and instead extending coal-fired operations to 2038. In a pending case before the Arizona Corporation Commission, APS is also proposing a 14% rate hike that would increase the average residential customer’s utility bill by roughly $240 per year.

TEP’s energy plan includes coal to gas conversions of units 1 and 2 at its Springerville plant by 2031 rather than retirement of those units as TEP had previously proposed. TEP plans to exit its share of the Four Corners coal plant by 2031, but also plans to add 300 MW of new fossil gas generation. TEP had previously committed to reduce carbon emissions 80 percent by 2035 but now says in its plan that it won’t achieve that goal.

The public can submit comments on both utility energy plans. Both APS and TEP’s plans will come before the Arizona Corporation Commission to be approved or rejected.

“Arizonans across the state support a timely transition from fossil fuels to 100% clean energy by 2050, but what the utilities have presented in their energy plans further delays our move from gas and coal to solar and wind,” said Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr. “We’re seeing these utilities protect their own bottom lines, instead of making the most economical decisions for ratepayers like you and me. More than ever, we need to embrace a clean energy future to protect our health, the climate, and our wallets.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5869. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5866. They Are Trying to Silence Us

State and federal government officials are trying to harass, intimidate, and silence NRDC and other nonprofit environmental advocates.

The Attorney General of West Virginia recently initiated an investigation of NRDC and a handful of other groups on the stated ground that we may have committed consumer fraud. The theory appears to be that it’s deceptive for NRDC to say that the burning of fossil fuels contributes to climate change, which, in turn, causes harm to the planet and its people. But that’s fact, not fraud.

To pursue his investigation, the AG served a subpoena seeking a vast trove of confidential internal and external communications relating to our advocacy around climate change for the past 10 years. The demand could cover sensitive strategy memos for policy advocacy, draft comments on regulations, exchanges with coalition partners, back-and-forth among scientists, dialogues with donors, and drafts of NRDC publications; all disclosed to a hostile adversary.

Co-counseling with Democracy Forward, we responded to the subpoena by preparing a federal lawsuit asserting infringement of our First Amendment rights to speak, associate with others, and advocate for effective solutions to the climate crisis. Those solutions include the replacement of fossil fuels with renewable sources of energy. And that is what drives the AG’s attempt to punish NRDC: pure retaliation for the content of our point of view.

At 2:13 p.m. on October 22, we advised the AG that we were going to file suit early that evening. A little more than two hours later, at 4:32 p.m., the AG withdrew the subpoena.

The AG is not alone in trying to bully NRDC. In September, the Committee on Natural Resources of the U.S. House of Representatives sent a letter of inquiry to NRDC and 18 other nonprofit environmental groups, informing us they are investigating the groups’ litigation practices. Why? According to the Committee, the targeted groups “build powerful and profitable litigation machines funded by taxpayer dollars through the exploitation of statutory fee-shifting provisions.” On this purported basis, the Committee demanded that NRDC disclose an exhaustive array of intrusive information and documents that could take months of concentrated effort to produce.

The premise of the Committee’s inquiry is that NRDC “effectively function[s] as [a] law firm[] funded by taxpayer dollars.” It calls NRDC “one of the most active litigating environmental nonprofit organizations driving the exploitation of statutory fee–shifting provisions.”

While NRDC is indeed an active litigant in federal court, it rarely receives attorney fees from the federal government. During our past five fiscal years, NRDC’s income from such fees in cases involving agencies or statutes identified in the Committee’s letter constituted 0.112 percent of our operating budget. In light of this numerical fact, contending that NRDC “build[s] a business model focused on siphoning tax dollars by weaponizing litigation awards” is unmoored from reality. With minimal effort, Committee members or staff could have discovered this from documents in the public record.

Courts have repeatedly held that the citizen-based enforcement litigation NRDC conducts, which holds government agencies accountable to the law, is a public service, and that Congress meant to encourage such suits by providing the opportunity to recover fees when a court determines an award is appropriate. This is the paradox: The Committee, which is part of Congress, accuses NRDC of stealing taxpayer money, when NRDC is doing precisely what Congress itself has empowered and encouraged NRDC to do.

It is not the only paradox. The Committee claims that its purpose is “to root out waste, fraud, and abuse of taxpayer dollars.” If so, is it investigating the nearly $4 billion in taxpayer funds the U.S. Department of the Interior has agreed to pay companies to abandon offshore wind projects that would produce economical, renewable energy? The government bought back the wind leases without any statutory authority to do so, drawing funds from the same pot of federal money that often covers attorney fee awards. The amount of taxpayer money expended on the buybacks during the past six months is more than 3,000 times the amount of attorney fees paid to NRDC over the past five years. To our knowledge, the Committee is not investigating the lease buybacks.

In response to the Committee majority’s attempt to intimidate NRDC, we sent this letter, asserting our First Amendment right to litigate to enforce the law. Thus far, the Committee has not sought to compel us to respond to its demand for documents.

The Natural Resources Committee has plenty of company within the House. The effort to harass environmental nonprofits and their funders is part of a multipronged attack that includes the House Committees on the Judiciary; Science, Space, and Technology; and Oversight and Government Reform. While NRDC is not yet a specific, named target of any of these additional House inquiries, other environmental nonprofits are.

These investigations and inquiries are designed to chill dissent, to punish those who disagree with the policies of those in power. The method of harassment is the same pernicious ploy we see repeatedly from the White House: Start with a lie (e.g., NRDC spreads deception, not facts, about climate change; NRDC feasts on federal attorney fees), then parlay the lie into a malicious attempt to deflect attention, divert resources, silence perceived enemies, and trample constitutional rights. It’s retaliation for protected speech. The West Virginia AG doesn’t want to debate the relative merits of renewable energy and fossil fuels—he wants to punish us because we disagree with him.

The recent moves by the West Virginia AG and the House Committees erode the rule of law. Not to speak out against them is to cede the narrative—to be complicit in a dangerous form of attempted repression. In the face of hostile questioning, the upright prosecutor Jack Smith recently told Congress that, while fear is contagious, so is courage. He’s right. Everyone who acquiesces in these abuses of government authority makes it easier for everyone else to do the same. Everyone who speaks out encourages others to resist.

It is not enough to survive this moment, silent and unscathed. As an organization rooted in the law, NRDC is compelled to fight for freedoms central to our democracy. The climate crisis is the existential issue of our day. But climate is not the only thing at risk.

We will persist without fear to address the ravaging effects of climate change and enforce the law against polluters. Our millions of members and supporters expect and deserve that from us. We did not succumb to intimidation, and we will not succumb to it if it comes our way again. This is the sole silver lining in the misguided attempt to bully NRDC into submission and silence: It will not work.
