#5834. Stratos Data Center Will Emit Air Pollution That’s Linked to Premature Deaths and Health Economic Damages for Utah & States Downwind

A new report models emissions from different on-site methane generation power scenarios to show potential impacts on the people of Utah and residents of multiple downwind states.

Canadian businessman and television personality “Mr. Wonderful” (aka Kevin O’Leary) and his businesses have proposed the Stratos data center in Box Elder County, Utah, near Salt Lake City, that  will be one of the largest data centers in the world, requiring as much as 9 gigawatts (GW) of on-site power from burning methane gas. The Box Elder County Commission approved the project in May—under highly contentious circumstances and under a highly unusual arrangement within a project area created by Utah’s Military Installation Development Authority.

A new report prepared by EmPower Analytics Group for NRDC estimates that the air pollution, health harms, and economic damages from the proposed Stratos data center would be substantial: “[a]t the potential 9 GW full build-out, air pollution from Stratos’s methane-gas power generation is estimated to cause approximately 5–45 additional premature deaths and $75–$690 million in health-related economic damages every year.” The report also discusses additional health harms that extend beyond premature deaths and include new asthma cases, nonfatal heart attacks, hospital visits, and lost work and school days.

These ranges reflect scenarios that model the health harms and economic damage caused by air pollution from two possible methane-gas primary power generating technologies: modern combined-cycle combustion turbines and methane gas–fired reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE). The primary harmful air pollution consists of tiny soot particles—particulate matter of 2.5 microns or less (PM2.5)—about 30 times smaller than the width of an average human hair.

The report further finds that over a 30-year period, at the potential 9 GW full build-out, the health and economic harms from this PM2.5 air pollution “could accumulate to approximately 856–1,350 premature deaths and $13.5–$20.7 billion under gas-fired RICE, compared with 145–253 premature deaths and $2.3–$3.8 billion under combined-cycle gas.”

Of the two methane-gas primary power generation technologies evaluated in the report, one uses a smaller number of large gas combustion turbines, while the RICE scenario reflected electricity generated by many individual engines operating in parallel. Indeed, the RICE scenario is patterned on a methane gas–powered data center with 69 RICE generators that the Utah Division of Air Quality permitted in Millard County in early 2026. RICE generators produce higher emissions than combined-cycle gas at the same generation capacity. Accordingly, the report finds that “[t]he technology used to power Stratos could make a five- to sixfold difference in its estimated health harms.”

Importantly, the modeled combined-cycle gas scenario does not estimate the emissions from the use of on-site diesel generators for backup power because those plans by Stratos remain unknown. (The report modeled diesel backup generators for the RICE scenario, scaled from the Millard County permit example.) Diesel generators are significantly dirtier than comparably sized methane gas generators, emitting 95 percent more smog-forming nitrogen oxides than an equivalent gas generator as well as 98 percent more non-methane hydrocarbons and 59 percent more carbon monoxide. Harmful PM2.5 emissions will be even higher from any data center employing diesel generators (the scale of that additional pollution would depend on whether they were truly used for “emergency conditions” versus being run more frequently; state permitting parameters for backup diesels is a critical issue for all data center developments).

The highest concentrations of harmful air pollution from the Stratos data center will occur near the facility in Box Elder County, neighboring areas of northern Utah (including Salt Lake City), and southern Idaho. But the health harms and economic damage from Stratos would extend well beyond these areas: The report finds that more than 80 percent of health harms and health-related economic damages occur outside the state, reaching downwind populations in Colorado, Wyoming, and as far away as Illinois. The report appendix identifies the full list of potentially impacted states.

The Stratos developers have maintained from the start that the massive data center will be powered by methane gas. “That’s never going to happen,” Utah Governor Spencer Cox told the Salt Lake Tribune in May. “The very first phase will be natural gas, but the other phases should not be. They should be nuclear, and they should be geothermal, and solar and other technology.” But the most recent filing by the Stratos developers with the state still show plans for the full build-out of the data center to be powered by methane gas. This filing shows that the developers now say that they plan a 7.5 GW data center using the dirtier and more harmful RICE generators that burn methane gas. None of these filings commit the developers to doing or not doing something different from what they are saying now, a reality that has characterized this data center project—and many other proposed data center projects nationally.

Data centers in Utah or anywhere else, of course, need not be powered by highly polluting, harmful fossil fuels like methane gas. Other data center developers have been doing better jobs showing how, in stark contrast to the Stratos proposal, it is possible to power these facilities with cleaner energy—such as a robust portfolio of wind, solar, geothermal, and batteries—and maxing out demand side resources.

NRDC commissioned this important EmPower Analytics report to raise awareness and concerns about the harmful health impacts and associated economic damages from dangerous air pollution caused by data centers like the proposed Stratos Project in Utah and by the proliferation of data centers across the United States. These harms arise especially from hyperscaler data centers powered by fossil fuels, like Stratos. While the dangers and costs of excessive water use by data centers are more well known, the deadly toll of air pollution—a silent killer—deserves greater attention and concern.

Harmful air pollution should be met with the best air pollution control technologies available. Data centers should be powered by new, additional, deliverable clean energy that does not release air pollution, along with batteries, storage, and other smart solutions. If someone nicknamed Mr. Wonderful wants to earn that name, he should live up to it, starting with the Stratos data center.

By John Walke, Director, Federal Clean Air & Senior Attorney, Environmental Health, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5831. China Truck Sales Now Majority Electric. What’s Next?

EV heavy trucks hit 50.3% market share in China for September, with sales up 34% from a year ago. Long-haul big rig market share hit 64.5%. China is on track to have over 40% EV market share for the entire year, reaching their 2030 goal in a matter of months. This is not the first time China’s heavy truck market share topped 50%, but that previous blip in December last year was prior to subsidies ending, followed by a dip in January. Current sales rates are not bolstered by the subsidy and are now on a much more stable upward trajectory.

The speed of EV adoption for heavy trucks in China has recently grown much faster than for cars, having risen from just 13.6% in 2024. When I visited Guangzhou in November of last year and again in April of this year, I noticed a lot more green plates on trucks, indicating that they were electric. It seems that my perception is backed by sales data.

With the addition of LNG trucks, diesel’s share of heavy trucks has dropped to roughly 40%. While LNG sales have also declined with the rate of EV adoption, diesel trucks could soon make up less than 1/3 of sales and decline further from there. China could feasibly double its 2030 goal and hit 80% EV penetration, by which point many diesel models are likely to be discontinued.

With these levels of market penetration, in addition to retirements, China’s diesel demand is on track to fall by about 230,000 barrels per day this year, despite increased freight movement. Every additional electric truck that replaces a diesel truck then builds on that reduction. Due to their duty cycle, each electric truck sold has a much greater impact on fuel consumption than each car sold. And the price of fuel combined with the duty cycle has a much greater impact on overall truck economics.

To be clear, these improving results are not due to subsidies, as they appear in the year after subsidies were cut. They are also not due to mandates, as the 2030 goals were met before the ink was dry on their announcement. EV trucks are now more affordable to own and operate than diesel trucks in China. The increase in diesel prices has only increased the cost gap.

Last year, roughly 90% of all electric truck sales globally were in China. That percentage is set to expand this year, despite rising sales in some other countries. Those sales are simply not rising fast enough outside of China to keep pace with China. In comparison, the EU is looking at 2.5% EV market share this year. China is on pace to average roughly as many electric trucks sold per week as Europe sells per year. The US EV truck market looks like a rounding error in comparison at less than one percent.

In addition, China has rapidly been building out heavy truck charging infrastructure as part of the 28 million charging facilities planned to be in operation by the end of 2027, up from 21 million last March. This not only focuses on the major cities but also extends into more rural areas that have been slower to adopt electrification. In addition, several companies are building out battery swap stations. While we have yet to see if battery swap or MW+ charging wins in the truck market, expect the competition between the two approaches to be fierce.

Within Tier 1 cities, I could see a potential phase out of diesel trucks in the near future. Hainan province is already planning to end ICE passenger car sales by 2030, and I expect that to extend to diesel trucks as well. Although rural areas have been slower to adopt, they also tend to be less wealthy and thus more impacted by the changing economics. If diesel prices stay elevated while charging becomes convenient, many rural businesses will not be able to afford to buy new diesel trucks. With the end of diesel truck sales, strict emissions standards and a potential return of scrappage incentives could take remaining diesel vehicles off the road at an accelerated rate.

With fewer diesel trucks on the road in the largest market globally, diesel consumption is set to decline. In addition, the supply chain for diesel parts will also start to decline. As protectionism has targeted electric vehicles, rather than internal combustion  engine (ICE) vehicles, a decline in Chinese (ICE) parts has a potentially significant impact on global markets.

Of course, as EVs take over the majority of heavy truck sales in the fiercely competitive Chinese market, Chinese manufacturers will start looking outside of the country for growth.

The traditional thought would be to look at wealthy Western countries that have substantial buying power. However, many Chinese companies may be a bit reluctant to focus on those markets after the protectionist efforts against electric passenger vehicles. Many European markets, like Germany, also have very high electricity prices, which makes the financial proposition less attractive for commercial vehicles that are heavily motivated by costs. Chinese companies may start focusing elsewhere.

The Middle Corridor could become a focus. This essentially rebuilds the Silk Road land route from China, across the Caspian Sea, on to Turkey. Many of the countries along this route  once had prosperous trade, only to fall into decline when ocean trade routes were established. However, land transport is much easier to electrify than long ocean voyages. China is investing heavily with countries like Kazakhstan along the route with renewable electricity infrastructure. Electrified rail is expanding by 26-fold in Turkey. Electrified trucking is the next logical step, connecting the rail lines to more local production along the route.

Other Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) countries could also benefit. In countries like Ethiopia, which has expensive diesel but electricity prices to businesses of just $0.017/kWh, electric trucks could make perfect sense to transport between new factories and new ports. Trading partners like Vietnam and Thailand could also see an influx.

All this is not to say that local companies will not benefit. Unlike passenger cars, heavy equipment tends to be more modular. Due to the bulk and mass of the vehicles, final assembly is more likely to be localized, with greater use of local suppliers.

Overall, we could see much of the “Rest of the World” (ROW) leapfrog legacy economies in EV truck adoption. The purchase price is no longer prohibitive in China, and the operating costs are much lower. With infrastructure buildout and increasing renewable installed capacity, electric trucking is poised to take over rapidly.

That is not to say that the West will be completely left out. Countries with less prohibitive trade policies toward EVs will likely see increased adoption. While EVs have taken over passenger car sales in Norway, their trucking industry still has a way to go at just 14.4%. And the geographically most eastern country typically lumped in with the “West,” Australia, is likely to see EV truck sales follow a similar pattern to EV car sales. If Canada continues to open up, they could also benefit. Of course, as the majority of the economic benefit from electric trucks comes from their use, inflated trucking costs hurt every company that moves products by truck, and those inflated costs then lead to inflation throughout the economy. That impacts overall affordability. As such, there is a chance that protectionists do not put as much effort into undermining electric truck imports as they put into passenger EVs.

And the EV heavy truck global supply chain will become increasingly well established. This could mean more parts availability for brands outside of China to build competitive vehicles, if those companies have access to global supply chains. Competitive markets will also drive innovation, moving the technology forward at an accelerating rate. Meanwhile the supply chain for diesel trucks will shrink.

While the US still seems to be figuring out how to make a safe EV Semi, battery-powered heavy trucks are becoming increasingly mature products elsewhere. As electric trucks move from majority share to complete market dominance in the largest market, other markets will follow, and the transition will become inevitable. Hopefully, the largest oil producing country in the world will not be left too far behind.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5828. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. Reaching permission to operate on a site like this reflects decades of hands-on experience, and it’s exactly the kind of project where that experience matters most.”

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.

#5826. How Does Oahu Get to an Economically Resilient, 100% Clean Energy Future?

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5825. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5824. XPENG Sales Soar!

XPENG had a fantabulous sales month in October. We can give you the numbers, but as you can see well enough in the chart above and below, the company’s sales soared to a whole new level last month.

Reaching 50,237 vehicle deliveries, that meant 22% sales growth month over month and 20% sales growth year over year. There were 41,256 XPENG vehicles delivered in September 2026 and 42,013 delivered in October 2025, the company’s previous monthly record. Will it be another year until XPENG beats its monthly sales record again, or is the October 2026 result just the beginning of more record sales in coming months?

Well, there are some reasons to expect even more growth. “In October 2026, the XPENG L05 was officially launched in the Chinese mainland, with deliveries set to begin on November 1, 2026,” XPENG shares. “On October 26, 2026, XPENG obtained a demonstration operation license to conduct Level 4 Robotaxi services in Guangzhou. The approval enables XPENG Robotaxi to advance from technology validation to real-world operational and business model validation, marking a key milestone toward the commercialization of XPENG’s Physical AI technologies.”

A big portion of this sales growth came from tremendous growth in sales outside of XPENG’s home market of China. “Overseas deliveries also reached an all-time monthly high of 13,326 units, up 166% year-over-year,” the company shares.

Overall, things are looking very bright for XPENG. And those EV sales are helping the planet and humanity as well. “XPENG’s electric vehicles delivered from January to October 2026 are expected to reduce life-cycle greenhouse gas emissions by more than 5.77 million tons compared to internal combustion engine vehicles, equivalent to the carbon absorbed by 95.43 million young trees over 10 years,” XPENG wrote in closing, and I’ll close with that highlight as well.

Oh, wait, I’ll also add that XPENG’s big spike in sales in October helped the company to shoot high above similar (but different) rival NIO in their long-term battle as the best selling smart EV startup.

NIO didn’t have its best month, but maybe it will surge again in November and/or December.

#5823. Caterpillar expands production of engines and turbines to meet data center demand

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The heavy machinery and power equipment maker saw its order backlog reach $78 billion during the third quarter, almost double what it was a year ago.

Caterpillar delivered one of its strongest third quarters on record, powered by data center buildouts, as orders across power generation, construction and mining continued to grow, the company said last week.

“Our third quarter results reflect the durability of our end markets, with our highest sales quarter ever, strong order rates and a record backlog,” Caterpillar CEO Joseph Creed said in a Q3 earnings call Thursday morning.

The heavy machinery maker saw its backlog reach $78 billion, nearly double what it was a year ago. Caterpillar has taken steps to expand production of its large reciprocating engines and turbines, while increasing capacity, to meet surging demand for data center customers, Creed said.

“The team is effectively managing the operational challenges that come with this rapid growth,” he said.

Caterpillar’s power and energy division, which manufactures gas-powered engines and generators, led the way for the company during Q3. Sales totaled $8.8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year.

Most of the growth came from power generation sales, which increased 30% year over year and were fueled by data center customers, according to the report. Oil and gas sales also saw double-digit growth, driven by reciprocating engine aftermarket parts and engines used in gas compression applications.

The vast majority of external sales and revenue, approximately 82%, came from North America and the Europe-Middle East-Africa region. Sales declined in the Latin America and Asia-Pacific regions.

The segment turned a profit of $2.1 billion, up 31% from a year ago.

Caterpillar’s construction industries division, which manufactures bulldozers, excavators and other heavy machinery, saw higher sales across all of its geographic market regions during Q3.

Sales totaled nearly $8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year. North America led the way, comprising more than half of the company’s external sales, followed by the EMEA, Asia-Pacific and Latin America regions.

Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing costs, including the impact of higher freight and capacity expansion. Caterpillar last month said it will invest $1 billion to build a facility in Sanford, North Carolina, focused on compact construction equipment for small contractors and growing businesses.

The segment posted a profit of $1.8 billion, up 33% over last year.

Caterpillar’s resource industries division came in below expectations during Q3, in part due to mining shipment and commissioning delays, as well as lower locomotive deliveries, Creed said on the call.

Sales totaled $4.6 billion, up 15% from a year ago. However, Creed said sales to end users or retail customers increased just 4%. The vast majority of Caterpillar’s sales come from dealers and original equipment manufacturers.

The segment turned a profit of $622 million, up 14% over last year. Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing and material costs, including tariffs.

Caterpillar recognized $600 million in tariff costs during Q3, excluding International Emergency Economic Powers Act refunds that it claimed.

This was in line with last year but $200 million lower than what Caterpillar recorded in Q4 2025, CFO Kyle Epley said on the earnings call. Construction industries comprise about 50% of the tariffs total, while power and energy and resource industries each account for 25%.

Caterpillar expects “moderate sales growth” in Q4 over last year, as well as higher operating profit margins, according to Caterpillar’s earnings presentation. For the full year, the company forecasted “mid-to-high teens growth” versus 2025. It also expects full-year tariff costs to be about $2.2 billion, excluding IEEPA refunds, Epley said.

“We delivered another very strong quarter, with year-to-date financial results tracking toward another record year for Caterpillar,” he said. “Our end markets remain healthy with strong order rates and a growing backlog.”

#5822. The Senate permitting bill holds promise but needs amendments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Targeted changes can minimize the most negative consequences of the permitting bill’s overreaching provisions, writes former FERC Chairman Mark Christie.

Mark Christie is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and former chairman of the Virginia State Corporation Commission, and is the director of the William & Mary Law School Center for Energy Law and Policy.

Every true compromise in a polarized legislature contains candy for each side, but also requires each side to swallow some hairballs. In the Senate permitting reform bill (SB 5653), the true permitting reforms hold a welcome potential to get needed infrastructure built faster. The transmission provisions, however — which are not permitting reforms at all — contain some provisions that are indigestible if you care about rising consumer costs and respect for historic state authority over utilities.

The bill’s best transmission provisions seek to ensure that large-load customers pay the full costs they impose on the grid. The worst transmission provisions, however, can be fixed with the amendments described below. Before considering these amendments, it is important to understand that the Federal Power Act for nearly a century has recognized a clear and logical division between state and federal authority over the power grid: The states have authority over the siting and permitting of all physical assets within their borders, including generation and transmission. The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission has authority over the rates for interstate transmission services and wholesale power sales.

The current Senate bill blows up this century-old balance of authority by giving FERC the sweeping power to order a transmission line built in any state, without regard to the state’s own decision on the project or the opposition of the state’s residents who will be directly affected. Congress has enacted so-called “backstop siting authority” before, most recently in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act in 2021, but Congress strictly limited the geographic reach of FERC’s backstop authority to narrow geographic corridors established by the U.S. Department of Energy and only after DOE made a finding that the national interest required the establishment of each corridor.

The Senate bill contains no geographic limits on FERC’s permitting power. It expands FERC’s authority to override the states to every square inch of the country, a breathtaking expansion of federal power at the expense of a core state function: the permitting and construction of utility assets within their own borders, projects that could have huge impacts on the lives of their residents.

Fortunately, targeted amendments can minimize the most negative consequences of the bill’s overreaching provisions. Here are three recommended amendments:

The bill allows FERC to order the construction of transmission lines based on a nebulous “public interest” standard. Such a standard is a blank check for FERC to approve trillions of dollars’ worth of transmission lines for whatever reason a presidential administration finds is in the “public interest,” as long as FERC adds general findings of some reliability improvements or consumer benefits, which can be claimed for every transmission line ever proposed. In the short term, FERC could use the bill’s grant of new authority to order transmission lines built to serve hyperscale data center development, a Trump administration priority. A future FERC could find that the public interest requires trillions of dollars of new transmission to meet “Net Zero” climate goals.

So my first recommended amendment is to remove the “public interest” and other general criteria, and instead require that FERC must find that the proposed line is the optimal solution to meet a proven reliability need at just and reasonable rates, compared to alternative solutions.

My second amendment addresses the critical role of states in cost allocation. FERC Order 1920 guaranteed to states in a multi-state region the right to reach an agreement on cost allocation for regional transmission projects and have that agreement submitted to FERC. The Senate bill should add an explicit guarantee that for regional transmission projects the states in each region have the right to agree on cost allocation and FERC shall presume such state agreement is just and reasonable.

My third amendment concerns one of the most onerous and frankly insulting provisions in the Senate bill. When we at FERC implemented the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act’s backstop siting authority, we gave each affected state one full year to conduct its permitting proceeding before FERC would start its own backstop proceeding. That allowed the state to develop a factual record and issue a decision within a reasonable period of one year.

The Senate bill allows FERC to start its backstop proceeding simultaneously with the state proceeding. That effectively renders the state proceeding meaningless, and states will perceive it exactly that way. Why should a state spend its time and limited resources on its proceeding when FERC is already doing its own? Any evidentiary record developed in the state proceeding may not even be available to FERC before it overrides the state. This provision in the Senate bill is nakedly punitive and overreaching and should be amended to give the states one full year to act before FERC can begin its own backstop proceeding.

Even if you don’t care about respecting state authority, you should care about the bill’s impact on consumers already struggling to pay their power bills. Over the past decade, transmission has been one of the fastest-growing drivers of power bill increases. The Senate bill as drafted could result in literally trillions of dollars of new transmission being ordered by FERC to serve whatever policy goals a president wants. These costs will flow right into consumers’ monthly bills.

Every compromise offers candy and hairballs. With these amendments, the bill can be made a lot sweeter.

#5821. Electric utilities join industries urging renewal of cybersecurity information-sharing law

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CISA 2015, as the law is often called, provides liability protections for companies sharing cyber threat indicators amongst themselves and with federal agencies. It is set to expire next month.

Three dozen business groups, including organizations representing electric utilities and critical infrastructure sectors, are pressing Congress to renew a major cybersecurity information-sharing law before its expiration next month.

The 2015 Cybersecurity Information Sharing Act “has become a foundational component of the nation’s cybersecurity,” the groups wrote on Sunday to the chairs and ranking members of the congressional armed-services committees. Signatories included the Edison Electric Institute, which represents investor-owned utilities, and the National Rural Electric Cooperative Association.

CISA 2015, as the law is often called, provides liability protections for companies sharing cyber threat indicators amongst themselves and with federal agencies. Those sharing alliances underpin the vast constellation of public-private collaboration groups that have sprung up over the past decade as the federal government has streamlined its approach to cyber defense.

After the law’s initial 10-year operating period expired in 2025, lawmakers passed several short-term extensions, with the latest one set to lapse on Dec. 11. Cybersecurity experts and industry leaders have repeatedly urged lawmakers to renew the law, calling it a critical tool for fighting hackers. Businesses now want Congress to include a new 10-year extension in the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) for fiscal year 2027.

“These authorities support the timely exchange of actionable threat intelligence and are central to the collective defense of public- and private-sector networks, particularly across critical infrastructure sectors,” the industry coalition wrote to lawmakers.

The signers include major trade associations representing almost every sector of critical infrastructure, including the Aerospace Industries Association, American Bankers Association, American Chemistry Council, American Petroleum Institute, Healthcare Leadership Council, National Association of Manufacturers, National Retail Federation, National Rural Water Association, U.S. Chamber of Commerce and USTelecom.

Cybersecurity-focused groups, including the Alliance for Critical Infrastructure and the Cybersecurity Coalition, also signed the letter.

Cybersecurity experts say the need for machine-speed information sharing has only grown as AI has sped up the pace of vulnerability discovery and cyberattack orchestration. In their letter, the business groups pointed to recent vulnerability crises that have led to global patching scrambles.

“In this environment, the speed and breadth with which defenders share information determines whether they keep pace with adversaries,” the industry coalition wrote. “That speed depends on the legal certainty CISA 2015 provides.”

The House’s draft version of the 2027 NDAA includes a provision reauthorizing and renaming the cybersecurity information sharing law. That provision would add AI to the list of topics about which companies can share information. The Senate’s draft NDAA does not include a CISA 2015 reauthorization.

Lawmakers will begin working to reconcile the two draft bills after they return to Washington on Nov. 9, following the midterm elections.

#5820. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5818. Trump ban on foreign grid components sparks industry concern over cost, practicality

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Stakeholders applauded the order’s intent to secure the grid but said restrictions based on geography, rather than function and capability, miss the point and threaten investment.

President Donald Trump’s recent executive order seeking to protect the bulk power system from foreign threats correctly identifies the risk of U.S. reliance on outside suppliers, but mitigating those risks without stalling the grid’s buildout or saddling ratepayers with even more costs will be tricky, stakeholders told Utility Dive.

Executive Order 14421, issued Aug. 26, gives Secretary of Energy Chris Wright the power to block the installation of foreign-produced equipment – “or any critical component, software, firmware, digital service, maintenance service, or remote-access capability associated with such equipment” – that poses an “undue risk” to national security, either through intentional sabotage or supply chain disruption.

It also gave the secretary the authority to order the removal and replacement of previously installed equipment.

The Department of Energy has until Dec. 24 to publish rules and regulations implementing the order. In September, DOE asked industry and other stakeholders to weigh in.

Their responses to DOE’s request show broad support for the intent of the order, but concern over how it will be implemented. A number pointed out that security standards, especially for software that would enable remote access, can be more important than country of origin since flaws in any system can be exploited by anyone, anywhere.

The vast majority of stakeholder responses that took a position on the “function over origin” question favored DOE emphasizing functional controls as opposed to a software product’s origin, according to data compiled by industry analyst Brendon Shank.

Many respondents also said they wanted DOE to take a phased approach to introducing new compliance rules and allow some vendors to “prequalify” as trustworthy. Utilities are projected to spend more than a trillion dollars on grid upgrades through 2030 and are racing to secure contracts for equipment amid rising manufacturer backlogs.

“Supply chains are already approaching a breaking point for many categories of electrical industry products,” said the National Electrical Manufacturers Association, which represents more than 300 electrical equipment manufacturers.

“[W]e strongly encourage the DOE to consider the time, money and resources it takes to evaluate, redesign, develop and produce equipment that is made for the electrical system,” it said. “At a time of high demand and longer lead times than during periods of more stable electricity growth, that is only further complicated by balancing timeliness with retooling or restructuring of production lines to meet new changes.”

Until DOE releases its determinations, the industry faces a lot of uncertainty and big questions, said Alice Wu, a manager of federal policy at the clean energy nonprofit RMI.

Even though the order instructs DOE to focus on transmission-related equipment and components of 69 kV or higher — not on the distribution system — it also puts distribution system components linked to the bulk system with questionably sourced software under scrutiny, she said.

“It is not feasible for generation, transmission and distribution providers to rip-and-replace every piece of equipment and for DOE to check it,” Wu told Utility Dive in an interview. “What is the secure proxy for that? … And what will the potential cost impacts to ratepayers be?”

The U.S. has imported more than $22 billion in bulk-power equipment since 2025, particularly from China, which would likely be the country most impacted by the order, according to a recent Wood Mackenzie analysis.

Transformers and substations are already in an estimated market shortage of 15% and 8%, respectively, in 2026, and “restrictions on the use of Chinese built units is going to further exacerbate the supply chain challenges,” WoodMac said.

The market for 100 MVA power transformers is particularly exposed, it said, adding that the market for power transformers greater than 10 MVA has expanded nearly 300% since 2020.

A different analysis by RMI and BloombergNEF said the value of grid imports in 2025 was more than $30 billion. Together, Mexico, Southeast Asia and the European Union made up more than half of that, while China accounted for $3.5 billion and “other” countries for $7.4 billion.

NEMA and other stakeholders and experts applauded the order’s emphasis on software, maintenance support and other services that introduce remote-access capabilities. They also pointed to existing security standards DOE could apply from the North American Electric Reliability Corp., the National Institute of Standards and Technology, the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers and others.

“Overall, this executive order focuses on the right security areas because it makes those who deploy critical infrastructure accountable for their software,” said Aaron Rose, a security architect manager with Check Point Software Technologies, which assesses security for utilities and other companies.

Check Point sees an average of about 3,000 cyberattacks per large organization per week, he said in an interview with Utility Dive.

DOE software determinations will be complicated, because “almost everything on the power system is software defined, controlled and driven,” he added.

DOE could ask for a software’s source, who maintains it, who has remote access to it, and where updates originate, Rose said. But updates for most firmware, cloud control systems, substation systems and customer-owned devices are from open-source libraries or code already written for other uses, making their countries of origin difficult to identify, and eliminating all software of uncertain origin is impractical, he said.

Power system software may also be sub-contracted to a third-party, Rose continued. Hardware manufacturers may or may not have documentation on that software’s developer or on who has remote access for updates, or be able to trace them, even with AI tools.

To avoid a complicated decompiling by a security team and still ensure critical needs are met, DOE could require future updates and remote access to be from secure sources, he added.

Rose said it is unclear whether the costs for such measures will fall on software vendors, infrastructure owners or their consumers.

An analysis of the order by the Rainey Center, a policy think tank, also questioned the logic of restrictions based on geography rather than capability and function.

“The risks the order names travel through code, connections, and control, and they work the same whether a device is built abroad or at home,” it said. “A ban governs geography; a standard governs the risk, on every device.”

The report recommended DOE define risk by “a verifiable, technology-neutral standard” built on existing controls, as well as a system for vendors to prequalify through independent U.S. testing.

The order’s flexibilities, like phased compliance, can allow determinations that avoid negative economic impacts, said Danielle Russo, executive director of  SAFE, a nonprofit advocacy group focused on U.S. national and economic security.

DOE can provide power system manufacturers and developers the clarity they need with lists of the specific supply chain equipment and components that are secure, Russo said. That will avoid slowing manufacturing investment and project development needed to meet load growth, she added.

In today’s complex investment environment, uncertainties from tariffs to changing political leadership can threaten investors with stranded assets, Russo said, adding: “It is hard to blame them for being challenged by that.”

DOE’s security restrictions should be balanced with legislated incentives that provide long-term certainty, Russo said.

#5817. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5815. APS expects sales to double by 2041, plans extending coal use to 2038

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Peak demand will grow about 3.4% annually over the next 15 years, driven by industry and data centers, Arizona Public Service said in an integrated resource plan filed Friday with regulators.

APS projects peak demand of more than 14.5 GW in 2041, almost 60% higher than its current record peak of about 9.2 GW, set in August. And the utility expects annual retail sales to grow to about 80,600 GWh in 2041, up from about 34,200 GWh in 2025.

There is some uncertainty behind APS’ load projections, but the utility said it has taken steps to protect customers.

Despite refinements to APS’ large-load interconnection process, the “sustained record levels of interest” from large customers poses “a risk in increased potential for unrealized loads,” APS said in its resource plan, which it files every three years with the Arizona Corporation Commission. To mitigate the risk, APS said it adopted strategies such as having customers commit to “minimum demand and energy-based contract terms, providing collateral in case of default, and instituting early exit fees.”

As a result of population growth and higher levels of economic activity, APS said it expects to add up to 29,000 residential customers annually in the near term, and across the planning period the utility anticipates annual customer growth to be in the range of 1.5% to 2.5% through 2030.

The utility’s preferred plan includes continuing to operate the Four Corners coal plant beyond a planned 2031 retirement. APS said the extension could save customers close to $1 billion.

“The company will continue to evaluate the appropriate time to cease operations between 2031 and 2038, with the decision depending on customer needs, economics and other factors evaluated through future planning processes,” APS said in a statement.

“Reliability is nonnegotiable,” Mike Eugenis, APS director of resource planning, said in a statement.

APS’ planned natural gas resource additions include repowering the Cholla Power Plant as a 387-MW facility by 2030, adding 1,107 MW through new combustion turbines at existing APS sites and uprates to tolling units during 2026-2030, according to the IRP. Under the plan, APS will also add the 1,238 MW Desert Sun combined-cycle facility in 2032 to serve large-customer subscription load.

Customer and clean energy advocates were critical of the APS plan.

APS’ proposal “further delays our move from gas and coal to solar and wind,” Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr said in a statement. “We’re seeing these utilities protect their own bottom lines, instead of making the most economical decisions for ratepayers.”

The utility “should be investing in proven community-based energy solutions, like local solar and battery storage,” Vote Solar West Senior Regulatory Director Kate Bowman said in a statement.

APS “failed to consider how alternative technologies like rooftop solar, battery storage, and other distributed resources could help meet energy needs,” Bowman said. As the ACC considers the plan, regulators will have “an opportunity to push for a more comprehensive approach that puts affordability, reliability, and the interests of customers at the center of long-term planning,” she added.

The utility said it is adding nearly 2 GW of renewable utility-scale solar and 2.5 GW of battery storage in the next two years, “diversifying our resources to keep energy reliable and affordable.” By 2030, APS said it expects to have approximately 6 GW of battery storage on its system.

The utility also expects continued growth in customer-sited resources. APS said behind-the-meter storage adoption is expected to grow every year to 1.2 GW by 2045 — up from 69 MW today. For 2032, the storage forecast is approximately 95% higher compared to the last storage forecast developed in 2023, APS said.

Behind-the-meter solar adoption is expected to slow relative to the 2020-2023 period but “will still experience some modest growth,” reaching 3.7 GW by 2045 — up from 2.1 GW today.

Electric vehicles in APS service territory are expected to grow from 65,000 vehicles today to 1.1 million in 2045, resulting in 5.2 TWh of EV charging load, APS said. In 2042, this forecast is approximately 18% lower compared to the last EV forecast developed in 2023, it noted.

#5813. Exelon, Invenergy complaints at FERC seek capacity market changes

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Exelon asked the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission to order PJM to adopt a “prompt” capacity auction; Invenergy wants the Midcontinent grid operator to let HVDC lines receive capacity payments.

Exelon and Invenergy Transmission this week filed separate complaints at the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission seeking changes to the capacity markets run by the PJM Interconnection and the Midcontinent Independent System Operator.

Exelon asked FERC to order PJM to shift to a “prompt” auction from its current forward capacity auction schedule, according to a Tuesday filing at the agency. Currently, PJM holds annual base capacity auctions to secure power supplies three years in advance.

Under Exelon's proposal, PJM would hold its capacity auctions two months before the delivery year begins, starting with the 2030/31 delivery year. If FERC approves the proposal, PJM would hold its next capacity auction in December for the 2029/30 delivery year as planned, and then it would hold its next auction in early 2030.

Pushing out the date of the 2030/31 auction would give PJM time to overhaul its capacity market, which Exelon contends is no longer working properly, according to the complaint.

Adopting a prompt auction would help ensure that auctions are based on more accurate load forecasts, resource availability data and capacity accreditation levels, Exelon said. It would also facilitate more efficient resource entry and exit through improved market signals, the Chicago-based utility company said. Exelon’s utilities have about 10.7 million customers in PJM’s Mid-Atlantic and Midwest footprint.

Exelon asked FERC to make a decision on the complaint by Jan. 31, the last day before PJM is set to release the planning parameters for the 2030/31 delivery year base auction, which is set to be held in May.

PJM is reviewing the complaint and will reply at “the appropriate time,” Jeffrey Shields, a PJM spokesman, said.

The grid operator is accepting proposals from stakeholders on how it could reform its capacity market through its Power Reliability Through Market Design initiative, Shields noted. PJM has received more than 30 responses to its request for papers on the issue. They will be discussed at a Nov. 13 workshop.

When it kicked off the effort in May, PJM proposed three broad reform pathways: stabilizing its existing capacity market, rationing grid reliability among customer classes or regions and shifting to a market focused on energy and ancillary services and away from the capacity market.

Meanwhile, Invenergy Transmission on Wednesday told FERC that MISO improperly bars high-voltage, direct-current merchant transmission lines from its capacity market.

MISO counts the capacity value of HVDC lines when it decides how much capacity it needs to buy in its capacity auctions, a move that reduces the amount of capacity it acquires, according to the complaint.

But, MISO’s rules prevent those lines from being accredited as capacity resources or their owners otherwise being paid for their capacity value, allowing the grid operator to benefit from the lines for free, Invenergy said.

“MISO cannot rely on that capacity, to the benefit of other customers, while refusing to provide any avenue for the entity that provides that capacity to receive commensurate compensation,” the Chicago-based company said.

Instead of recovering their costs through FERC-approved cost-of-service rates, the owners of merchant lines recover their costs via rates negotiated with their customers, Invenergy noted.

“Interregional merchant HVDC transmission lines only receive compensation if there is a product or service — such as capacity — for which they can transact with potential customers,” Invenergy said.

Without capacity payments from MISO, “there is no product or service that can be the basis of a merchant-based transaction in MISO,” Invenergy said.

Using a “direct loss of load” accreditation methodology, Invenergy’s Grain Belt Express HVDC transmission line could provide more than 1,800 MW of accredited capacity, according to the complaint.

The 5-GW, 600-kV Grain Belt project is slated to run about 800 miles from Kansas to Indiana. Invenergy plans to build the roughly $11 billion project in two phases, with the first phase running from southwest Kansas to central Missouri.

#5812. Entergy sees progress on Arkansas data center deal, strong Q3 sales

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The New Orleans-based company separately inked firm pipeline contracts for enough natural gas to support 10 new combined-cycle power plants in Louisiana, CEO Drew Marsh said Monday.

Entergy’s amended application to serve a controversial Google data center will save its Arkansas ratepayers at least $500 million more than the $1.6 billion previously agreed, CEO Drew Marsh told investors Monday during the company’s third-quarter earnings call.

The New Orleans-based utility holding company saw earnings increase more than 20% over the year-ago period helped by warmer-than-average weather across its four-state U.S. territory in the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s southern zones, he said. Total electricity sales rose 5% year over year.

Entergy made progress on two key regulatory priorities in Louisiana, which accounts for nearly half of the holding company’s retail sales.

State regulators in October approved Entergy Louisiana’s $1.2 billion, 500-kV Babel to Webre transmission project, Marsh said. And Louisiana Public Service Commission staff separately reached a settlement with the utility that Entergy said “resolves all outstanding issues” between the company, LPSC staff and one intervenor in a contested proceeding related to several gigawatts of generation and related infrastructure to serve Meta’s planned Hyperion data center campus.

The LPSC is set to vote on the matter next month, Entergy said.

Meanwhile, Entergy’s Mississippi utility asked state regulators last month for state clearance to issue bonds that would offset approximately $237.8 million of costs related to Winter Storm Fern, which Entergy said caused “severe damage” to its infrastructure.

And in Texas, Entergy said it has energized the Orange County Advanced Power Station, a 1,215-MW combined-cycle gas plant built to support load growth in the state’s southeastern corner.

Entergy filed the amended Google service contract with Arkansas regulators last month. If approved, Entergy would add 350 MW of battery energy storage at two sites and procure 440 MW of solar from Pattern Energy’s nearby Big Island Solar project, the company said in a required securities filing this week.

The Arkansas Public Service Commission is set to hear the matter in January, Entergy said.

The amended contract would expand power demand from Google’s West Memphis data center, but the exact amount and other key details are redacted, the Arkansas Democrat-Gazette reported on Oct 20.

On Monday, Marsh said Google would pay $600 million toward the cost of the battery systems and suggested the tech giant would avoid passing costs to other Entergy ratepayers.

“This agreement ensures that Google will cover its fair share, which includes the cost of the new investments as well as their share of system costs,” Marsh said.

Entergy Arkansas is one of five utility companies owned by Entergy, and earned about 48% of its sales from industrial customers in 2025. The parent company’s four-state territory has been a popular destination for large-scale data centers like Google’s Arkansas project and Meta’s even larger Louisiana campus.

Together, data center and traditional industrial customers account for a large share of Entergy’s expected future sales growth, much of which is not yet reflected in the company’s near-term capital plan, Marsh said.

“We have a strong pipeline of projects in varying stages that are not in our base plan,” he said. “Data center interest remains strong and in the range of 7 [GW] to 12 GW. Our traditional industrial segments continue with discussions for 3 [GW] to 5 GW.”

Those large-load pipeline figures are largely unchanged from recent quarters’ projections. But the company’s projected four-year capital spending has risen significantly over the past year, to about $55 billion as of Sept. 30.

Marsh said the Google contract allows Entergy to keep base rates flat for Arkansas customers. However, that’s not the case in Louisiana, where Meta “is helping [to] offset some [infrastructure] costs, but not necessarily to make it go back to flat like we were in Arkansas,” he said.

To serve the Meta data center, Entergy plans to spend about $12.9 billion to build seven combined-cycle gas-fired power plants with 5,278 MW of combined capacity, plus a $1.4 billion transmission line and 200 MW of battery storage. It has also asked Louisiana regulators to approve a corporate sustainability agreement that would see Meta — doing business as Evest LLC — cover the cost of 2,500 MW of new renewables and energy storage resources.

To support the new gas generation, Entergy inked firm transportation agreements with “large midstream and pipeline companies” for 1.325 billion cubic feet/day of natural gas, Marsh said.

“That's enough for Entergy to supply fuel to 10 new combined cycles in Louisiana … and also helps build new pipeline capacity that benefits our industrial customers' growth,” he said.

#5810. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/
