#5808. Eaton CEO: AC to DC migration for data centers ‘is real’

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The electrical equipment giant says it’s poised to capitalize on the trend by investing in solid-state transformers. But the “timing of that transition is not under our control,” said CEO Paul Ruiz.

The CEO of one of the world’s biggest suppliers of electrical equipment sees the AI-driven global construction boom continuing for years — and driving a shift from traditional low-voltage systems to 800-volt direct-current architecture for data centers.

In August 2025, Eaton Corp. paid $86 million for Resilient Power Systems, a solid-state transformer developer.

Using magnets and semiconductors rather than specialized steel alloys and vast amounts of copper wiring, solid-state transformers, or SSTs, are more compact and efficient than traditional low- and medium-voltage transformers. Data centers built on the 800-volt direct-current architecture that Eaton expects will soon become the industry standard could reduce non-IT capital expenditures by up to 18%, according to a recent McKinsey analysis.

Earlier this year, the Electric Power Research Institute, New York Power Authority and North Carolina State University tested a 1-MW SST under “real-world” conditions and cut power losses in half compared to a traditional transformer configuration.

Paulo Ruiz, CEO of Eaton, told investors on Tuesday that the company’s 15-kV, 2-MW SST became, “to our knowledge,” the first multi-megawatt unit to pass all tests under a new UL Solutions standard for the technology.

However, it may be some time before Eaton SSTs are widely deployed, he cautioned.

“The good thing for us to keep in mind is that this direction of travel, in terms of migrating from AC to DC, is real because of the large power savings that the customers see in this new technology,” he said. “But the timing of that transition is not under our control.”

He added that SSTs are only one of several “key building blocks” needed, along with DC breaker, power quality and advanced cooling technologies.

“The AC architectures are not bad, and we are fine either way,” Ruiz said. “We win in either path.”

Ruiz said he sees no letup in the global demand for power equipment from data centers and utilities.

Eaton, which is headquartered in Ireland but is one of the largest suppliers in the U.S. market, makes and markets low- and medium-voltage electrical products such as single- and three-phase power quality systems, utility-grade power distribution equipment, circuit protection and power reliability equipment. It reported a record $8.9 billion in third-quarter sales, up 28% year over year.

“We track the [worldwide] projects that are announced, and now they amount to 366 GW,”  Ruiz said, of data centers. “Contrast that with the installed capacity of 50 GW. It's an enormous build-out. In my opinion, this will last for many years to come.”

Eaton predicts end market growth of about 11% in 2027. In 2025, data centers and distributed IT accounted for 21% of sales, while utilities accounted for 11%.

A little less than half the total of third-quarter sales came from Eaton’s Electrical Americas segment. That unit benefits from broad-based demand from data centers and other electrification “mega-projects,” helping to widen profit margins, Ruiz said.

The Electrical Americas segment saw its backlog rise to $16 billion – up 33% year over year –  while orders increased 39% on a rolling 12-month basis.

Ruiz emphasized that data centers were not Eaton’s only high-growth customer segment. Utility investment and other large-load industrial customers are propelling business, too, he said.

“Demand continues to run ahead of what we can convert,” Ruiz said. “This is a business that continues to benefit from powerful secular trends, utility investment, grid modernization, industrial reshoring, AI-driven data center expansion and broader electrification.”

In the past two years, Eaton has acquired multiple companies with data center exposure.

The most recent was a roughly $920 million deal for COL Group, an Italian manufacturer of medium-voltage electrical distribution equipment, grid automation technologies and modular power systems that Eaton said would boost its sales to data center and utility customers in Europe.

In March, Eaton paid about $9.6 billion to acquire Boyd Thermal, “a U.S.-based global leader in thermal components, systems, and ruggedized solutions for data center, aerospace and other end-markets,” the company said in a securities filing this week.

The Boyd pickup will help Eaton develop integrated power and cooling architectures for data center customers and “deliver differentiated grid-to-chip solutions while increasing our exposure to some of the fastest-growing segments of the data center market,” Ruiz said.

PJM had planned to accept offers for backstop power supplies from Sept. 30 through Oct. 21 after falling short of its reserve margin targets. But FERC only partially approved its plan, zeroing in on cost allocation concerns.

#5807. “War” Secretary Pete Hegseth Endorses 15 Minute City Model

You just can’t make this stuff up. While conservatives around the world are waging holy war against the “15 Minute City” concept, US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has gone all-in on the idea. His newly announced “Fortress America” military resilience plan includes a new vision for military bases that embraces walkability and quality of life. If that sounds like the 15 Minute City plan in action, it is. Oh, lordy…

Walkable communities are as old as the hills. That’s what people did before automobiles hit the scene and cities were organized around cars. Credit for applying the walkability model to 21st century urban planning goes to the Franco-Colombian scientist Carlos Moreno, who formalized the 15 Minute City concept in 2016. The idea got a further boost of attention when the Mayor of Paris, Anne Hidalgo, pitched walkable, bike-able neighborhoods as part of her successful re-election campaign in 2020.

The COVID-19 lockdown of 2020 was also a pivotal moment, with a surge in work-from-home households drawing attention to the need for local resources as a matter of survival and resiliency in case of a widespread emergency.

The 15 Minute City model covers a lot of ground, but the basic idea is that people should be able to do their shopping, recreation, socializing, and other essential routines within walking or biking distance from their homes, making cars largely optional within the neighborhood. Walkable access to schools and health care is also part of the plan.

The argument for 15-minute cities (also called 15-minute neighborhoods or 15-minute communities) is also pretty straightforward. Walking is healthier than sitting in a car. Avoiding injury or death from car accidents is another plus. Walking also reduces carbon emissions, and it saves money on gas.

In addition, walkability levels the playing field, so to speak. Everyone in the 15-minute zone has walkable access to basic resources, regardless of whether or not they can afford a car. In other words, there is a significant equity and inclusion element to the concept…

…and, that’s where the trouble kicks in. In 2022 the World Economic Forum posted an article by Lisa Chamberlain, the founder of the urban communications and business firm Strategies for City Builders. Chamberlain describes a 15 Minute City as characterized by diversified, mixed-use neighborhoods built to withstand shocks like COVID-19 and climate change.

“As climate change and global conflict cause shocks and stresses at faster intervals and increasing severity, the 15-minute city will become even more critical,” Chamberlain emphasized, with a further emphasis on equity and inclusion:

“Specifically, communities that foster and maintain social and economic relationships don’t have to be wealthy, but they do need to be walkable and safe, with both residential and commercial buildings intact. And, I would add, for 15-Minute Cities to thrive, not just survive crises—and this cannot be stressed enough—they must also have plenty of mixed-income and equitable housing, as well as digital access.”

It didn’t take long for the conspiracy machine to kick into action around the globe. In February of 2023, for example, the Australian news organization ABC radio summed up the 15 Minute City conspiracy theory thusly:

“To some, it’s an urban planning concept that promotes sustainable and healthy living.

“To a small group of others, it’s a plot by ‘tyrannical bureaucrats’ to take our cars and control our lives, which could lead to a real-life Hunger Games scenario.”

“Limiting cars and promoting pedestrian or bike access is framed as a slippery slope to government-run, open-air prisons,” ABC continued.

Yikes! ABC also cites the Canadian psychologist Jordan Peterson. who railed against “idiot tyrannical bureaucrats” who “decide by fiat where you’re ‘allowed’ to drive…and, make no mistake, it’s part of a well-documented plan” (emphasis added).

Ohfergawdsakes, of course there is a well documented plan, and it’s called urban planning. And, that’s where the US Department of Defense comes in.

On September, in a move that sailed under the CleanTechnica radar, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced the new “FORTRESS America” plan, with FORTRESS representing “Federal Operations for Resilience, Troop Resources, Energy, Supply, and Survivability.”

Stripping away Secretary Hegseth’s testosterone-fueled purple prose in the September 30 announcement, FORTRESS AMERICA applies the 15 Minute City urban planning model to major Defense Department bases in the US, ensuring they can operate independently of civilian infrastructure for a sustained period of time. That includes everything needed to support community life in addition to the supplies needed to sustain military operations.

FORTRESS America even goes beyond the initial 15 Minute City concept. As envisioned under the “Next Great American Base” element of the program, a central feature of the plan consists of access to a reliable source of on-site energy.

Don’t just take my word for it. “The program aligns basing and infrastructure projects to ensure they are collectively advancing toward greater independence,” Hegseth stated in the official order, with independence referring to the capability to sustain operations within a facility that is cut off from civilian resources. In other words, sustainable urban planning for military facilities.

Hegseth tasked the Under Secretary of War for Acquisition and Sustainment to carry out the new order. The base design part of the order was passed along to Dale Marks, the Assistant Secretary for Energy, Installations, and Environment.

Marks did not let the 15 Minute City grass grow under his feet. In a public presentation on October 29, he emphasized the importance of building quality of life into a sustainable model for military facilities in the US.

“Let us build installations worthy of the warriors who call them home,” Marks emphasized. “The nation shouldn’t expect warfighters to focus on high stakes, multidomain operations when they come home to base housing with black mold, failing plumbing and leaky roofs.”

“Substandard living conditions are not minor inconveniences. They are an active drain on morale, combat readiness and our retention of top-tier talent,” Marks elaborated.

It’s not just a matter of rehabbing military housing. In the presentation, Marks made the case for a full blown 15 Minute City model. “The Next Great American Base will include a compact, walkable concept with premier schools, advanced healthcare and town centers that foster community,” Marks continued, neatly summing up the fundamentals of 15 Minute Cities in a nutshell.

Marks anticipates that state governors and other stakeholders will compete to see where the first “Next Great American Base” will be constructed. Once complete, the new facility, and its 15 Minute City, will serve as a model for future projects.

As for the on-site power generation angle, nuclear energy gets the pole position — for now. As Marks and Hegseth both emphasize, though, speed is of the essence. Results are demanded within months, if not weeks. That leaves plenty of room for solar-enabled microgrids to throw their hat into the FORTRESS America ring. After all, if speed-to-power is the name, solar and batteries are the game…

Image: The 15 Minute City model calls for walkable, bike-able access to basic services and resources, a concept that has been embraced by none other than the US Department of Defense (courtesy of Rutgers University).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.

#5806. SpaceX Starship Will Need To Re-Fuel 22 Times In Space To Make It To The Moon

As NASA plans to send astronauts back to the surface of the Moon in 2028, there's still the small matter of figuring out how to get them there. While the Artemis II mission proved that we can send people around the Moon and bring them safely home, the space agency needs a way to take astronauts out of the Orion capsule and down to the actual ground. The original plan was for a SpaceX Starship to fulfill this duty, and that could still be the case. But delays in the development of that ambitious rocket caused NASA to re-open the contract, which allowed Blue Origin to toss its hat in the ring. Still, SpaceX insists it will fend off this challenge from a rival billionaire-owned space company and win the contract all over again. The only thing standing in its way: the minor fact that it has to refuel the Starship in-orbit, something never done before and that SpaceX hasn't even attempted yet. Oh, and it has to do it many, many times. How many? Great question.

The NASA Aerospace Safety Advisory Panel recently announced that, by their calculations, a Starship would require fully 22 in-orbit refuelings to get that one single rocket to the Moon's surface, per Ars Technica. Again, the total number of times that any rocket has ever done this is zero. Specifically, the plan from SpaceX is to launch one Starship into orbit to act as a fuel depot; then launch many other Starships to load up that first one with fuel; then launch a final Starship to take on that fuel from the depot and head out to the Moon.

If 22 sounds like an insane number to you, you're not alone: SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said on the social media platform X, which SpaceX owns, "Actual number will be more like 6 to 8." That makes for quite a broad range of estimates. The reason this is so fuzzy is because the fuel we're talking about here isn't gasoline, but rather a mixture of liquid oxygen and methane. This actually boils off in the vacuum of space, so how much fuel can be transferred depends on the efficiency of the technology involved. Technology that has never actually been tested in orbit.

Currently, SpaceX plans to perform its first test of orbital refueling in early 2027, just a little over a year before it will theoretically do it for the Moon landing. If that doesn't work, well, SpaceX's lunar ambitions are in trouble. Even if it does go well, and there are absolutely no issues whatsoever with this unprecedented capability, there's still the small matter of actually launching all of these rockets. Right now, the still-in-development Starship only launches once every few months. Musk claims it will be launching weekly by early 2027, just months away. And then he further claims it will launch twice a week by the end of 2027. Let's just say that would be, ah, truly remarkable if it happened.

For once, let us be optimistic, and believe in words. If these words be true, and Starship truly is launching twice-weekly, then launching 22 refueling Starships (plus launching the initial "depot" Starship) would take... three months. And that is assuming no failures, either of the rockets or of the fuel transfer process. That's reduced to three weeks if you also believe Musk's figure of six launches. At least Musk has never once lied about his companies capabilities! If for some reason you don't believe in that launch cadence, then total refueling will take potentially years. Either way, that would amount to the most complicated endeavor NASA, or any space entity, has ever attempted.

By contrast, Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin has a much simpler plan to get to the Moon. It's still complicated, let's be clear, but less so than SpaceX. The company's proposal calls for a mere four launches of its New Glenn rocket, assuming it doesn't explode and destroy its entire launchpad, again. The first three would park a booster stage in orbit; the fourth would put a Blue Moon lunar lander into orbit, where it will collect all the booster stages, stack them together, and then blast off towards the Moon. That is a wild dance all its own, but it doesn't require figuring out how to transfer any fuel.

If you're wondering where the actual astronauts are, don't you worry: they're on a completely different rocket altogether. The Space Launch System (SLS) rocket carries the Orion capsule into orbit with all the astronauts on board. The capsule then continues on from Earth orbit to lunar orbit on its own, as the Artemis II mission demonstrated. Somewhere along that path — whether in high-Earth orbit, high-lunar orbit, or low-lunar orbit (this part still isn't nailed down) — the Orion capsule will have to dock with either the Starship or the Blue Moon, depending on who wins the contract. The lander will then land, the astronauts will enjoy a nice walk around another world, and then the lander will return to lunar orbit. Then it's back aboard the Orion capsule and then back to Earth.

Sounds easy! Basically, a lot has to go right for NASA to make that 2028 landing date. How it shakes out in practice depends on how well a lot of untested equipment and procedures work. Then again, if you were to go back to the 1960s, the Apollo program wasn't exactly any more precedented, or safer. In that decade, though, NASA did have a lot more testing demonstrations before going for the Moon. This time around, the agency wants to get there faster.

#5805. Nabrawind’s Crane-Free Wind Towers Face a Supplier Test

Spain’s Nabrawind has developed a way to install exceptionally tall wind turbine towers without bringing an enormous crawler crane to the site. Its Nabralift system uses a tubular upper tower supported by a three-legged steel structure. Hydraulic equipment raises the assembled turbine while modular sections are inserted below it near ground level. The lifting frame can then be removed and used on another installation. This addresses genuine logistical and construction problems, particularly at remote sites where specialist cranes are expensive to transport and where wind can interrupt conventional lifting operations.

The company has progressed beyond demonstration-scale hardware. In September 2026, UL Solutions issued type certification for Envision’s 7.8 MW turbine on Nabrawind’s 188-meter tower. Fortescue is using 17 of those tower configurations at the 133 MW Nullagine wind project in Western Australia’s Pilbara region, where construction is underway. Nabrawind previously erected a 160-meter prototype in Spain in under two weeks and has supplied smaller commercial towers in Morocco and France.

The engineering benefits are credible. Nabrawind reports using roughly 20% less tower steel than comparable conventional tubular designs and reducing foundation concrete by as much as 80%, depending on the design and site. Those are supplier claims rather than independently established savings across all wind farms. The potential to avoid large cranes, simplify transport and raise turbines in windier conditions could also matter substantially to total installed cost and construction schedules. High hub heights only earn their keep when those costs are justified by the electricity generated over the project’s life.

The current deployment has an unusual commercial structure. Fortescue acquired Nabrawind in September 2025 and is building Nullagine to serve its own mining operations. Fortescue has the capital, internal electricity demand and industrial incentive to support a technology it owns. Successful installation in that context would be important evidence of construction performance, but it would not establish that an unrelated wind developer can finance and maintain the same system under ordinary third-party commercial arrangements.

Nabralift’s proprietary construction approach creates a procurement issue. Conventional tubular towers can be sourced from several qualified manufacturers, and experienced contractors can compete for much of their construction work. A developer selecting Nabralift currently depends on one supplier for the core tower system, specialist lifting knowledge and associated support. Wind turbine original-equipment manufacturers also create supplier dependencies, but concentrating another critical part of the project in a proprietary installation system requires its own contract and risk assessment.

A type certificate provides valuable technical assurance about the certified turbine-and-tower configuration. A lender or insurer still needs enforceable delivery schedules, long-term warranties, repair and maintenance provisions, clarity on replacement parts, and a workable remedy if the supplier’s capacity or commercial priorities change. These questions become more important when a wind farm must operate for 25 years or longer and there is no qualified alternative provider of the same system.

Nabrawind’s next important proof point is an independently developed and financed large wind farm that accepts those risks on commercial terms. Qualified installation partners, credible long-term service arrangements and greater competitive capacity could make its engineering advantages easier for developers to buy. The 188-meter tower has earned technical attention; its broader market depends on financing and procurement confidence catching up with the machinery.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Nabrawind’s self-erecting design, Fortescue’s Nullagine deployment and the supplier concentration that independent wind developers would have to manage. It follows my earlier assessment of 300-meter wind towers, where construction economics were the central challenge.

#5804. 1976 Pontiac Trans Am For Sale With 7 Miles After Restoration Pairs 4-Speed Stick And 455 V8

For those looking to channel your inner Burt Reynolds from "Smokey and the Bandit," WeBe Autos (via Hemmings) has this lovely black-and-gold Trans Am for sale that's nearly the perfect match. Except, it's a 1976, not a 1978, so it doesn't feature the quad-headlight front-end that the movie car had. If that's what you want, however, this 1978 example or this 1979 one that's done roughly 22,000 miles should be of interest. Neither of them, mind you, is in black, nor do they feature a 455-cubic-inch V8 or offer the three-pedal experience like the '76 example we have here.

Unlike the movie car, though, this Trans Am is a hardtop coupé. But it has undergone extensive restoration and has only done seven miles since. And yes, it has the Screaming Chicken hood decal, or the "Rising Phoenix" if you're part of that camp. So what's the asking price for this 1970s muscle-car goodness? That'd be $72,495. Although it may not be as sophisticated as the latest BMW iX3 (also a $70,000 ballpark proposition), the 455 V8 and four-speed combo has enough personality and aura to challenge whatever these modern-day "computers on wheels" can offer.

Character assessment aside, what you get for your money is a well-finished exterior, a beautifully detailed engine compartment, an exceptionally clean underbody, and a beautifully restored all-black interior. While the Endura-style bumpers and Honeycomb wheels stand out as exterior highlights, the classic Trans Am instrumentation, custom Autosound audio system, and factory-style dashboard come across as some of the notable attractions on the inside. Sure, it might not be a T-top nor feature the movie car's front fascia, but then again, Bandit's companion was actually a 1976 Trans Am with a '77 front end.

Classic Industries and Hemmings reckon there were four cars used in total, while Autoweek counts five, but the important thing is that all Trans Ams used in the movie started out as '76 Special Edition 50th Anniversary cars with the updated '77 front end. Pontiac had to get creative because the movie's production began before 1977 models were actually available. And the engines you saw (or rather heard) on screen weren't part of the 1977 lineup at all. While Classic Industries reported the stunt cars retaining the 455 V8, in reality, you could not have optioned the motor in a 1977 car — it was retired after the 1976 model year.

Setting aside this bit of "Smokey and the Bandit" trivia, you can probably already tell how the Trans Am listing we have ties in with all this. It's a 1976 car in the right paint scheme, with the 455 and a four-speed manual thrown in for good measure. More importantly, there's no deception here, and it looks nothing like a 50-year-old hunk of metal.

Is the near-$73,000 asking price too much? That depends on whom you ask. It's a lot of money for something that old. Despite the restoration, that 455 was part of the Malaise Era lineup, which famously neutered a lot of high-performance V8s. Given that the listing mentions "emissions equipment present," it's unlikely that things are any different here. Still, for someone who likes '70s Americana, is an ardent fan of movies, or, above all, a true-blue Pontiac enthusiast, this Trans Am should hold plenty of appeal. Assuming, of course, they have the means to afford it.

#5803. Solar carport project at California medical plaza solves tricky energy billing

Watthub Renewables and Sunrock Distributed Generation completed a 322-kW solar photovoltaic system at Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza, the three-story Class A medical office building in Irvine, California. Solar modules were installed to carports covering approximately 108 parking spots, turning bare asphalt into shaded, covered parking and into the building’s primary source of electricity.

Watthub Renewables, Sunrock Distributed Generation and SunRenu Solar completed a 322-kW carport solar project at Irvine Heritage Medical plaza in Irvine, California. Credit: Watthub Renewables

The system is expected to generate approximately 515,690 kWh in its first year, covering the majority of the plaza’s annual electricity consumption. It was delivered under a 25-year power purchase agreement requiring no upfront capital from Irvine Heritage’s ownership. Watthub and Sunrock DG financed, engineered and contracted by SunRenu Solar. Sunrock DG now owns and maintains the array, while the property buys the power it produces at a rate below Southern California Edison’s.

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza was built in 2008 on just over two acres, with approximately 40,000 sq ft of medical suite.

“Medical office is one of the few property types where the parking lot is part of the patient experience,” said Aaron Moston, director at Watthub Renewables. “Somebody’s coming out of a medical or dental procedure at two in the afternoon in August. Whether their car is 140 degrees or 95 degrees is not a small thing. Carports were the right structure here before we ever ran the production model — the fact that they also happen to be the best south-facing real estate on the property is what made the project work.”

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza is master metered, meaning the entire building draws electricity through a single Southern California Edison (SCE) account on the GS-3-TOU commercial rate, and that cost flows through to tenants inside their charges rather than through individual utility bills. For years, that meant every practice in the building carried a share of an electric bill it could not see or control in a utiliyt territory with rate increases and, during the summer, on-peak energy.

Since solar generation is netted at the master meter, the savings are not captured by one tenant — they reduce the building’s total electricity cost.

“A master meter is usually described as a disadvantage — tenants have no visibility into their usage and the owner absorbs every swing in the energy bill,” said Wilson Chang, CEO at Sunrock DG. “For a solar project it’s the opposite. One meter, one interconnection, one bill to offset and every suite in the building benefits automatically. It’s the simplest way to bring solar to a multi-tenant property.”

Earlier this year, developed another 300-kW solar carport project for the Foothill Medical Association in Foothill, California. The system was also commissioned with a PPA.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5802. Ferrari Luce Sold Out Through 2027 In Another Win For The Liberal Agenda

November 3 was a historic night for the liberal agenda as Democrats won just about every single major election across the country in a massively convincing fashion, leading many to declare that woke is indeed back. Perhaps then, it is no surprise that sales of the Ferrari Luce, the wokest vehicle Maranello has ever produced, are off to a roaring start with the order books now full through the end of 2027. As it turns out, internet haters who cannot afford a Ferrari aren't the tastemakers one would think.

Ferrari made the announcement that its first all-electric vehicle was sold out through '27 during its earnings call earlier this week, according to InsideEVs. The news comes after a July announcement from the automaker that the $560,800 EV had already hit its 2026 sales target after being available for purchase for just a couple of months. People want this thing, and people want pronouns in email signatures again. It's morning again in America.

In any case, it's time to jump in the Luce, crank up "Fight Song" and blast down the highway, because we're libbing out in this bih.

Washington, DC — As Democrats regain control of the US House of Representatives and the US Senate, the Sierra Club today released a new legislative policy blueprint for the incoming Congress that will deliver for the American people by addressing the affordability crisis, holding polluters accountable, preserving our public lands, and protecting public health and safety.

#5800. Scandal-Ridden Cadillac F1 Owner Putting Team Up For Sale After Just One Year

The long-awaited entry of an American factory team was viewed as a coup for Formula 1. Billionaire financier Mark Walter is now looking to sell the Cadillac F1 Team after a single F1 season. While the team has failed to score a point in its maiden campaign so far, the sale would help him dodge scrutiny amid federal investigations into alleged self-dealing. Walter's TWG Group is in negotiations to sell a significant team stake at a $3 billion valuation.

Walter amassed a sports empire over the past 15 years. His investment firm Guggenheim Partners purchased the Los Angeles Dodgers in 2012 for $2.15 billion. Walter personally owns 27% of the MLB franchise. Since the acquisition, the Dodgers have alarmingly amassed over $1 billion in deferred payroll obligations on their way to three World Series titles. TWG would expand into racing in 2024, purchasing Andretti Global and its F1 entry bid. The company spent over $1 billion on the Cadillac F1 Team before its debut race in Australia earlier this year, nearly half that total was an anti-dilution fee.

How could Walter afford to do all of this? According to The Wall Street Journal, the U.S. Department of Justice and the Securities and Exchange Commission are investigating how TWG-owned insurance companies lent billions of dollars to Walter's other enterprises. IndyCar fans are familiar with these insurance companies, seeing Delaware Life and Gainbridge logos plastered on Andretti's cars. An internal review found that $20 billion in loans weren't disclosed to financial regulators. Walter presumably needs to repay said $20 billion as soon as possible.

The TWG firesale began in August. Walter sold the Los Angeles Lakers for $12.5 billion to Josh Kushner's Thrive Capital. The deal was negotiated over a weekend. He added the NBA team to his sports empire just last year for $10 billion. After the Lakers deal, TWG denied that the Cadillac F1 Team was for sale. Despite the denial, Fenway Sports Group is the favorite among several bidders to buy the F1 team. The company currently owns the Boston Red Sox, Liverpool F.C. and RFK Racing in the NASCAR Cup Series.

The Cadillac F1 Team's sudden sale could be viewed as an opportunity for another automaker to enter Grand Prix racing, but General Motors also owns a stake in the team. With that constraint, FSG is the most logical choice to keep the team on its current course as GM develops its in-house F1 power unit program. #5798. Bay Area Air District Moves Forward with Nation’s Strongest Water Heater Standards

Health advocates, environmental groups, heat pump installers, local electeds celebrate decision as.

SAN FRANCISCO — The Bay Area Air District voted today to move forward with the strongest clean appliance standards in the nation, putting millions of Bay Area homes on a path to switch from gas water heaters to zero-emission alternatives. Beginning in 2028, households will be required to install water heater appliances that meet clean air standards as old gas units burn out, with exemptions approved today for low-income households and complicated installations.

Gas appliances in Bay Area homes and buildings generate more smog-forming nitrogen oxide pollution than all of the region’s passenger cars combined. The Air District estimates its clean appliance standards for water heaters and furnaces could prevent up to 85 premature deaths and 15,000 asthma attacks each year.

The switch also saves families money. New research from RMI finds $360 in annual savings for Bay Area residents from switching from gas water heater to a heat pump. Analysis from SPUR projects $315 million in rebates for home energy efficiency will be available in the next three years to support the transition.

The vote follows thousands of public comments in support of the standards, despite an industry-backed opposition campaign linked to oil interests.

Supporters of the standards released the following statements:

“The research makes it clear that climate policies and affordability go hand-in-hand and today the Bay Area is taking the historic step of making clean home appliances the standard for a better future,” said Melissa Yu, Campaign Strategist for Clean Heat, Sierra Club. “A transition toward zero-emission gas heaters will reduce a key source of smog pollution, launch programs to help get more people access better technological solutions and lower energy costs. Today’s vote is a victory for cleaner air and for energy affordability.”

“Today’s vote will supercharge the job growth we’re already seeing at heat pump businesses like mine, opening new pathways for early-career Bay Area workers looking for family-sustaining wages and meaningful work. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership in approving these standards. We’re proud to be on the front lines of equipping homes with energy-efficient technologies that cut energy bills, clean the air, and make homes more comfortable,” said Owen Krebs Grimsich, CEO and owner of 1-888 Heat Pumps.

“Today, the Bay Area chose clean air over fossil fuel pollution. Under these standards, when a household’s gas water heater wears out, it will be replaced with cleaner, more efficient technology like a heat pump, so millions of residents will breathe easier and save money on their energy bills. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership, and the rest of the country should follow its lead,” said Tony Sirna, Deputy Policy Director for Buildings at Evergreen Action.

“Today’s vote is a big win for clean air, lower utility bills, and climate leadership,” said Berkeley City Council member Igor Tregub. “The Bay Area is moving forward with the nation’s strongest water heater standards, and millions of families here will breathe cleaner air as a result. These standards build in the flexibility we need to make the transition workable, while growing the market for clean technologies that lower bills and create good jobs.”

“Today, the Bay Area Air District said yes to saving lives and no to gas industry attempts to slow progress to clean our air,” said Fernando Gaytan, senior attorney on Earthjustice’s Right To Zero campaign. “The Bay Area is now one step closer to making non-polluting appliances the  norm thanks to the leadership of board members and staff. This policy will show the rest of the country what’s possible.”

“Today’s vote is a step forward for cleaner air, healthier homes, and a more affordable energy future. By advancing these standards, the Air District shows we can move away from polluting gas appliances without leaving families behind,” said Sam Fishman, sustainability and resilience policy manager, SPUR. “Bay Area cities, utilities, and regional programs are taking the important steps to invest more than $120 million annually in home electrification and energy-efficiency incentives. With these tools, we can make our homes safer for our planet and our health, too.”

“As regional leaders in building electrification, Acterra sees this vote as a vital win for local communities and public health,” said Lauren Weston, Executive Director at Acterra: Action for a Healthy Planet. “Transitioning to zero-emission heat pump water heaters clears toxic nitrogen oxide pollution from our neighborhoods while lowering utility bills and accelerating equitable access to clean, energy-efficient technology for all Bay Area households.”

“The Air District Board’s vote today to support the implementation of Rule 9-6 is a watershed victory for healthy and safe communities, moving from our dependence on fossil fuels to a cleaner, greener source of energy,” said Leslie Alden, Executive Director of Act Now Bay Area. “The Air District staff, the experts, the working groups, and so many dedicated members representing the Bay Area worked tirelessly from 2023 show once again that the Bay Area leads the way.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. Solar contractors install the first solar modules on the first solar project in Faropoint’s 16-project portfolio in New Jersey. Credit: Faropoint

The other 15 projects, totaling approximately 8.5 MW, have been accepted into New Jersey’s Community Solar Energy Program. Nine of them have received a notice to proceed, allowing construction to begin.

#5795. Is It Over for DAPT After Left Atrial Appendage Closure?

— Antithrombotic monotherapy worked at least as well at 6 months

Antithrombotic monotherapy was similar to dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) for 6-month outcomes after left atrial appendage (LAA) closure, the SIMPLAAFY trial showed.

The incidence rate of combined all-cause death, stroke, systemic embolism, or major bleeding at 6 months was 5.1% with reduced-dose non-vitamin K oral anticoagulants (NOACs), 3.3% with aspirin alone, and 6.9% with DAPT, reported Saibal Kar, MD, of Los Robles Hospital and Medical Center in Thousand Oaks, California, at the Transcatheter Cardiovascular Therapeutics annual conference hosted by the Cardiovascular Research Foundation.

Statistically, the strategy of reduced-dose NOACs was at least as good as DAPT (risk ratio [RR] 0.75, 95% CI 0.48-1.18), as was aspirin alone (RR 0.49, 95% CI 0.29-0.82).

"These findings support the simplification of antithrombotic management after uncomplicated LAA [closure] and may be individualized according to competing bleeding and device thrombosis risks and patient preferences," Kar said.

The study was simultaneously published in JAMA.

Among the secondary analyses in the trial, there was no excess stroke at 6 months with the aspirin or NOAC monotherapy regimens. However, bleeds alone gave both an edge over DAPT, while low-dose NOAC therapy stood out for a relatively low incidence of device-related thrombus (DRT) at 45 days (0.9% vs 3.4% with DAPT and 2.7% with aspirin).

As it stands, standard practice varies widely for antithrombotic therapy after LAA closure on account of a "lack of legitimate evidence" for guidance, said session discussant Mohamad Alkhouli, MD, of the Mayo Clinic in Rochester, Minnesota.

"Aspirin seems to emerge a winner," Alkhouli suggested, based on SIMPLAAFY, while he noted that "the jury is still out" for NOACs.

Complicating the picture is the ANDES trial, which found that after LAA closure, short-term NOACs were no better than DAPT at preventing DRT at 60 days, and were associated with even more DRTs at 1 year (5.2% vs 2.3%, P=0.154).

Kar said these data gave him pause on making a blanket recommendation for NOACs after LAA closure.

Meanwhile, 12-month results from SIMPLAAFY are still pending.

"The fact is that we really didn't know what to do with these patients," said Kar. "What we will do beyond 1 year, I don't know."

SIMPLAAFY included patients with nonvalvular atrial fibrillation who had the Watchman FLX Pro LAA occluder successfully implanted. The investigators randomized 1,857 patients 1:1:1 to aspirin 81 to 100 mg alone, initial DAPT for 6 months (followed by aspirin thereafter), or initial reduced-dose NOACs -- 2.5-mg apixaban (Eliquis) twice daily or 10-mg rivaroxaban (Xarelto) daily -- for 3 months (followed by aspirin thereafter).

Across the three study groups, mean age was 76 years, 43% were women, mean CHA2DS2-VASc score was 4.2, and mean HAS-BLED bleeding risk score was 2.6.

Acute events through 7 days were rare in general. Rates of clinically relevant nonmajor bleeding were 1.0% with aspirin alone, 1.6% with DAPT, and 1.5% with low-dose NOACs; major bleeding rates were 0.2%, 0.5%, and 0.2%, respectively.

The proportion of participants adhering to their assigned medications (for 80% or more days) was 87.9% with aspirin alone, 76.8% with DAPT, and 85.3% with reduced-dose NOACs. "Both monotherapy treatments were better tolerated than DAPT," Kar noted.

A subgroup analysis indicated that people ages 75 and older had disproportionately better outcomes on aspirin (RR 0.34, 95% CI 0.18-0.64) or reduced-dose NOACs (RR 0.53, 95% CI 0.31-0.93).

LAA imaging at 45 days suggested no differences in the Watchman device seal across groups.

Kar stressed that only uncomplicated LAA occluder implants were included in the trial, limiting the generalizability of the findings. Some high-risk populations were also excluded (e.g., those with severe kidney disease, recent stroke, myocardial infarction, or major bleeding).

Of note, there was no prespecified comparison of the two monotherapy arms.

#5794. Who Controls the Battery in Your Driveway?

Tesla brought home power backup to America. In Australia, an update is switching it off. North American owners just got Powershare with Powerwall. Australian owners of existing Teslas got a software update that appears to block the third-party gear they use to power their homes. It is a test case for who really controls the battery in your driveway.

Mark Purcell is an electrical engineer and energy advocate based in Noosa Heads, Queensland, Australia. He owns two Teslas and a bidirectional DC charger, and has been a member of the Australian Electric Vehicle Association since 2022. He has contributed content to CleanTechnica previously.

On 12 October, my Model Y ran my house. Through a Sigenergy bidirectional DC charger in my garage on Australia’s Sunshine Coast, it supplied about 25 kW to my home. The battery in my driveway holds several times the energy of a typical home battery, and I have already paid for it.

That may not last. Owners in several countries report that Tesla software from the 2026.38 branch, rolling out since October, cuts third-party discharge within minutes. On the Tesla Motors Club forum, owners describe power stopping after about three minutes with a battery management alert, or after 15 to 20 minutes whatever the load. The old Camp Mode and Service Mode workarounds no longer help. Drive Tesla Canada has covered the reports. EleJoy, Tlyard and Sigenergy users are all affected, and Tlyard now warns customers that Tesla’s 2026 software detects “simulated charging” and may cut the power link.

The release notes? “Security improvements” and minor fixes. Nothing about switching off a function owners use every day.

Here is the part that should interest readers everywhere. The same 2026.38 branch delivered Powershare with Powerwall home backup to owners in the US and Canada. That is a good thing. Tesla has finally joined Ford, GM, and Kia in letting the car back up the house.

Australia did not get it. Tesla Australia has said Powershare cannot be retrofitted to existing vehicles, and here Powershare is vehicle-to-load only. So, for every existing Tesla on Australian roads, third-party equipment is the only way to put the car to work for the home. If the software now blocks that equipment, Australian owners are left with nothing: not Tesla’s solution, and not anyone else’s.

The Tesla Australia owner’s manual says: “Do not use the Battery as a stationary power source. Doing so voids the warranty.” Tesla’s own Australian warranty page is narrower. It excludes damage caused by third-party adaptors or chargers, not the whole warranty.

Australian law is narrower again. Under the Australian Consumer Law, statutory consumer guarantees cannot be signed away by a line in a manual. Apple found this out when the Federal Court ordered A$9 million in penalties over the “Error 53” affair, after Apple told customers that third-party repairs had cost them their rights. American readers will recognise the shape of this argument from the right-to-repair fight.

Australia is a world leader in rooftop solar and is now subsidising home batteries at scale. The next step is obvious: the biggest battery most households will ever own is already parked outside. Bidirectional charging is how that capacity reaches the home and, in time, the grid.

That whole idea depends on interoperability. If one carmaker can switch off discharge overnight, through an over-the-air update the owner cannot roll back, and without saying so, then every bidirectional charger is a bet on that carmaker’s goodwill. Chargers bought in good faith become stranded. Competing suppliers lose access to the fleet. Grid operators lose flexibility they are counting on.

The ACCC, Australia’s competition and consumer regulator, has already named “denying interoperability” as conduct that can distort competition in digital platform markets. A car that decides which charger it will talk to is a platform in all but name.

I am deferring Tesla updates on both my cars, so I am also missing whatever genuine fixes they contain. I have asked Tesla in writing to hold updates until it confirms discharge will keep working. I have lodged a complaint with the ACCC under Australia’s competition and consumer laws. I have also asked the Australian Electric Vehicle Association, the national voice of EV users, to back it.

Tesla may have a real safety or engineering reason. If so, it should say what it is, and show why a less restrictive answer would not work: certified partner equipment, discharge limits, or a published interface that charger makers can build to.

Three things, and they apply well beyond Australia. Carmakers should disclose functional changes in plain language before they ship. Owners should be free to use the bidirectional charger of their choice. And regulators should set interoperability rules for vehicle discharge, as many already do for solar inverters. If we can make inverters speak a common language, we can stop cars locking out the chargers they plug into.

My car still powers my house. It does so only because I have stopped it updating. No owner, in any country, should have to choose between a current car and a working home battery.

“I am looking forward to the discussions around this topic,” David Waterworth, who helped get this article published, adds.

We publish a number of guest posts from experts in a large variety of fields. This is our contributor account for those special people, organizations, agencies, and companies.

Guest Contributor has 4698 posts and counting. See all posts by Guest Contributor

#5793. Voters Back Abortion Rights and Transgender Restrictions in Some States

— Measures provided a political undercard for a high-profile midterm election

Abortion-rights advocates notched victories in several states Tuesday, and voters in some states backed transgender restrictions as they decided 145 ballot questions in more than three dozen states.

The ballot measures provided a political undercard for a high-profile midterm election in which Democrats took control of Congress and gained power in state legislatures and gubernatorial offices.

Voters defeated a proposed amendment to Missouri's constitution that would have reimposed restrictions on most abortions. The vote Tuesday essentially reaffirmed voters' decision from 2 years ago, when they became the first in the nation to undo an existing abortion ban by approving an abortion-rights amendment.

The Republican-led Legislature then referred a new, less restrictive version of an abortion ban to this year's ballot. The measure would have allowed abortions only for medical emergencies and fetal anomalies, or in cases of rape or incest up to 12 weeks of pregnancy. It also would have amended the constitution to prohibit certain medical procedures for transgender minors.

Abortion-rights advocates, led by national progressive groups, heavily outspent abortion foes in a successful campaign to defeat the Missouri ballot measure.

In Virginia, a voter-approved measure creates a "fundamental right to reproductive freedom" in the state constitution, ensuring that abortions can occur during the first two trimesters of pregnancy. The amendment also creates a right to contraception and fertility care, among other things.

An amendment approved in Nevada creates a fundamental right to abortion until fetal viability, which is generally considered after 21 weeks of pregnancy. The amendment won initial voter approval in 2024 but needed a second vote this year to add it to the constitution.

Since the U.S. Supreme Court overturned a nationwide right to abortion in 2022, voters in more than one-fifth of the states have passed ballot measures protecting abortion rights.

But voters in Republican-leaning Idaho on Tuesday defeated a ballot measure that would have created abortion rights in state law. Current Idaho law bans abortions unless necessary to save the life of a pregnant woman, or during the first trimester of pregnancy in cases where rape or incest has been reported to law enforcement.

Abortion-rights measures failed 2 years ago in Florida and South Dakota.

Transgender Restrictions Pass in Some States

Nebraska voters approved an amendment to add existing transgender medical restrictions to the state constitution. And in Arizona, voters passed a measure that extends public school sports restrictions to private schools.

Voters in Colorado and Washington also were considering measures restricting transgender athletes or medical procedures. That comes as Trump's administration has been pushing for restrictions impacting transgender people, especially youth.

Most Republican-led states already bar transgender women and girls from female school sports teams. In the Democratic-led states of Colorado and Washington, the measures were placed on the ballot by citizen initiatives. A second ballot question in Colorado would ban some transgender medical treatments for minors.

#5792. GM Recalls Over 67,000 Chevy Blazer EVs And Cadillac Lyriqs, Vistiqs, And Even Celestiqs For Temporary Loss Of Braking

General Motors is recalling 67,380 Chevrolet Blazer EVs And Cadillac Lyriqs, Vistiqs, and even Celestiqs because under specific circumstances, a software defect can cause a temporary loss of braking ability. According to the NHTSA recall report, GM is aware of 20 minor accidents due to the fault, two of which resulted in minor injuries. Fortunately, the condition only arises at low speeds, but any loss of braking is a deeply concerning and dangerous occurrence. Two-wheel-drive Cadillac Lyriqs are not included in the recall, and the only Blazer EV trims involved are SS and Police Pursuit Vehicles.

Cadillac Lyriqs are hit hardest by the recall, which includes 474 2023 Lyriqs, 22,819 2024 Lyriqs, 11,926 2025 Lyriqs, 9,322 2026 Lyriqs, and 44 2027 Lyriqs. The recall also includes 13,627 2026 Vistiqs and 5,175 2027 Vistiqs, as well as 51 2025 Celestiqs, and 31 2026 Celestiqs. (This is one of our best glimpses yet into how many Celestiqs have been sold.) The only Chevrolets included in the recall are 562 2024 Blazer EVs, 2012 2025 Blazer EVs, 1,314 2026 Blazer EVs, and 23 2027 Blazer EVs. VINs are already searchable on NHTSA's website, and the remedy comes in the form of an over-the-air update for owners who have opted in to those, otherwise owners will have to take their vehicles to dealerships to have the software update downloaded.

The conditions that lead to the temporary loss of brake function are very specific. According to the recall report and GM's testing, "when the brakes are lightly applied at low speeds during a sharp turn and the vehicle encounters a bump or depression – such as at the end of a driveway – a momentary release and reapplication of the brakes may cause the software's slip calculation to misinterpret wheel movement. In some instances, this may result in a temporary loss of braking. The phenomenon is unique to certain vehicles equipped with performance all-wheel drive (pAWD, RPO X0E) and is resolved when the brakes are applied again."

GM already recalled 21,469 all-wheel-drive Lyriqs for a similar issue in 2024, but it found that vehicles built with the software changes following that recall and vehicles that already received the software update from that recall are still susceptible to the conditions described in the new recall, and they need the latest update. The latest software update corrects the slip calculation in the vehicle's brake system control module that causes the temporary loss of braking, which coincidentally is the same claim made on the 2024 recall. Hopefully this time it actually fixes the problem.

If you drive one of the cars included in the recall, be aware of the possibility of braking loss, and remember that if you experience braking loss, normal brake function should return on the next brake press. Dealers should already be notified of the recall, but owner notification is estimated to begin on December 14.

#5791. Tesla Allegedly Misled Thousands Of South Korean Customers About 'Full Self-Driving'

Usually, when we tell you that Tesla has gotten into hot water for misleading folks about its "Full Self-Driving" Level 2 driver assistance system, it has to do with Tesla overstating just how capable of a system it is. That's not the case in South Korea, though, where the Texas-based automaker has allegedly vastly overstated the legality of FSD usage and charged customers for it nonetheless. Now, tens of thousands of Korean Tesla owners are looking for answers.

Tesla apparently markets FSD in South Korea despite the fact that it knows the majority of its cars can't legally use the program in the country, the complaint from an advocacy group alleges. That means it can't be activated on the car. Soon, the complaint will be filed with the Korean Fair Trade Commission, a consumer protection agency, according to Reuters. Tesla will have to answer for why it marketed FSD to prospective buyers for years, even collecting the $6,726 lump-sum payment required for activation, even though it couldn't legally be used.

Currently, just 20% of Teslas registered in South Korea can legally use FSD because they're built in the U.S., according to a preliminary testimony given by Tesla Korea country manager Seo Young-deuk last month. Those specific cars meet U.S. vehicle-safety standards that South Korea recognizes as part of a free trade agreement. The thing is, most Teslas on the road in South Korea are manufactured in China, where no such agreement exists. It was only recently that Tesla started the process of obtaining FSD certification on China-made vehicles, even though it has been charging customers for the software for over three years.

Right now, it's not totally clear how many customers in South Korea bought FSD subscriptions on ineligible vehicles, but the automaker has sold over 170,000 China-made vehicles in the country since 2023, Reuters reports. The company continued to market FSD as sales of ineligible China-manufactured vehicles surged in South Korea in recent years, and last year, 99% of new Teslas sold in the country couldn't legally use FSD.

The complaint alleges that last year, Tesla promoted FSD as "coming soon" and posted a demonstration video filmed in South Korea without clearly disclosing that the tech could only be used in a tiny fraction of the vehicles it was selling, according to Reuters. The consumer advocacy group accused Tesla Korea of deceptive marketing and has asked the KFTC to issue a temporary cease-and-desist order on marketing FSD for ineligible vehicles, impose fines and refer the case to state investigators.

It may be an uphill battle, though. Just last month, a court in Seoul dismissed a lawsuit filed by Tesla owners seeking refunds for the FSD option, Reuters reports. The court rejected the claim that Tesla had failed to deliver the driver-assistance system. Instead, it put the onus of the issue on regulatory and legal restrictions that had limited the company's ability to roll out future functions.

Man, that feels like one weak ahh argument to me.

#5790. Elon Musk Threw Away $474 Million (Or More) … But May Benefit From Failing

Some people think Elon Musk has lost his mind. Others think he’s always been a charlatan and has just had an insane amount of luck. Some people think he’s a genius and that everything he touches will turn to gold. And then there’s also a decent portion of folks who think he’s an absolute idiot about some things but brilliant — or at least very clever — when it comes to others. Whatever you think of Elon Musk, though, there’s no denying he just threw away about half a billion dollars.

Musk reportedly spent at least $474 million on the midterm elections (because one individual is allowed to do that, apparently), and yet his picks — the key people he supported — got completely crushed. The politicians he spent all of that money to get elected did not get elected, and were actually not even close to getting elected. Apparently, voters in their jurisdictions thought they were horrible choices, sometimes historically so. Imagine if all of that money he spent had been spent on something good and useful instead! (Perhaps saving the lives of starving, poverty-stricken children and babies around the world? Perhaps stopping the spread of horrible diseases, since the US government has dropped such efforts on several threats? Perhaps to provide subsidies for electric vehicles, like Tesla sells, since Republicans in Congress and Donald Trump killed those? Perhaps to use solar power instead of fossil fuels to power AI data centers?)

Why did Elon Musk spend a whopping half a billion dollars (or more) on the 2026 midterm elections?

I know there are some people who don’t see this, but it’s been clear for a while that the following things are true about Elon Musk:

Musk largely targeted Texas with his political spending, and Texas had an epic, historic blue wave. Something that has been dreamt about for decades came true in dramatic fashion despite Elon Musk spending hundreds of millions of dollars to try to stop it. Ironically, Democratic politicians are the ones who support electric vehicles and solar power, key segments of Musk’s empire. It was California, led by Democrats, that birthed Tesla Motors and enabled it to grow and develop into an industrial behemoth. It was US policies under Democratic presidents and Congresses that also provided critical help for years upon years. So, despite Elon Musk’s worst instincts and biggest efforts politically, will his businesses actually do better under the newly elected Democratic leadership? Perhaps there are some corporate issues that need reigned in or need better regulation, and perhaps people Musk brazenly and often quite rudely campaigned against will squeeze down extra hard on his corporate actions, but policymakers who support cleantech over fossil fuels should, overall, mean more good news than bad news for Tesla and its broader mission, right?

Sure, Musk threw away $474 million and lost bigly, but is he going to be better off from failing in his efforts to pick the people running his current home state and representing it in the US government? What are the actual risks he and his companies face from the people he heavily campaigned against winning their elections? What are the benefits he and Tesla will get from fossil-loving, pollution-peddling Republican politicians no longer running the show in Texas and in Congress? One has to wonder.

By the way, one of the key people Musk campaigned against, James Talarico, has a message specifically for him. “Texas is not for sale,” Talarico said. Musk spent at least $150 million to try to get Talarico’s opponent elected. “The people of Texas delivered a crystal-clear message to the world’s first trillionaire: Texas is not for sale,” Talarico added. “The power in this state, in this country, belongs to the people, and we’ve got to make sure it stays that way.” What do the people want? Well, the people want clean air, clean and cheap energy, and less money in politics. Shouldn’t all of those things help Musk? On that last one, after all, if he wasn’t allowed to pour so much money into politics, he wouldn’t have thrown away half a billion dollars.

#5789. Investigating RFK Jr., Fixing Surprise Billing Law May Be on Agenda for New Congress

— Reversing or delaying Medicaid cuts could also be a possibility, experts suggest

Changes to laws on surprise billing and price transparency as well as investigations of HHS officials could be on the menu when the Democratic-controlled Congress assumes office next year, analysts say.

"We're heartened to see a number of people elected who are likely to be public health champions," Bruce Mirken, communications co-chair at Defend Public Health, said in a phone call.

"[Sen.-elect] Abdul El-Sayed, MD, talks a lot like we do, and there are a number of other people who got elected" such as Reps.-elect Richard Pan, MD, (D-Calif.) and Mitchell Berman, RN (D-Wisc.), he said. "So there will be a lot of voices in this next Congress who will speak up for public health in a way that hasn't been happening enough in the last 2 years."

Among possible actions to be taken by the new Democratic-controlled Congress, "one is a complete and thorough investigation and airing of [HHS Secretary] Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s record of malfeasance and disinformation, which we think should lead to articles of impeachment," he said. "This guy's guilty of public health malpractice and Congress needs to assert its authority ... and start the process of removing Mr. Kennedy."

Reasserting authority over health spending should be another top priority, Mirken said. "This administration and HHS played a lot of games with money Congress appropriated. Congress needs to dig in quickly and [fix the] damage done to disease monitoring and tracking, food safety, and health research."

Tom Miller, JD, senior fellow at the American Enterprise Institute, took a more cautious approach. "Tuesday's sweeping election results will trigger the usual post-mandate cross pressures for congressional Democrats: the urge to get some things done differently versus the strong temptation for factions to overstate and overreach their mandate to settle scores, address grievances, and pursue boundary-stretching changes not supported by the voters who just threw another set of rascals out," he said in an email. "All of this will be challenged further by the calendar pressures of starting the 2028 presidential campaign ... this week!"

"Although healthcare usually ranked in the top two or three issues most of concern to voters, it's far from clear what those voters really want (other than the generic wish for more care at lower prices)," Miller said. "Developing a list of priority targets and feasible reforms will remain challenging for the new leaders of Congress. In the meantime, competing demands on the calendar, and the limited attention span of both voters and officeholders, will make substantive legislating more aspirational than successful."

He did offer a few possibilities for action, however. "Expect few large reforms or rollbacks of recent Trump administration policy changes, but some adjustments at the margins. Some limited consensus may be available for adjustments to the flaws of the No Surprises Act and targeted shots at common villains of the moment in the health sector (hospitals, drugmakers, insurers), but expect more rhetoric than reality in what changes from current law."

Other health cost initiatives may also come under the new Congress's radar, said healthcare consultant Thomas Campanella, professor emeritus of health economics at Baldwin Wallace University in Cleveland, in an email. These include initiatives such as site-neutral hospital payments, "stricter vertical and horizontal anti-trust enforcement of hospital systems, addressing 340B abuses, and stricter enforcement of user-friendly price and ideally quality transparency," he said. "Also, there is bipartisan support for enhanced investment in primary care and rural healthcare."

Medicaid is another possible area of action, according to Edwin Park, JD, research professor at Georgetown University's Center for Children and Families in Washington. For example, the House and Senate could try to repeal the Medicaid and Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program benefit cuts that were included in the reconciliation bill passed by Congress last summer, "and if that's not successful they may attempt to delay some of those cuts," Park said in a phone interview. There are also provisions of the Medicaid program that are expiring next year -- such as money to pay Disproportionate Share Hospitals -- that Congress will need to deal with by year's end, he added.

Park also said he expected more scrutiny of actions taken by CMS. That includes how the agency is implementing Medicaid cuts included in the reconciliation bill -- also known as the One Big Beautiful Bill.

"There have been a number of occurrences where CMS has gone well beyond what [the bill] requires, whether in [the interim final rule] for work reporting requirements ... or guidance related to financing Medicaid waivers," he said. In such cases, "CMS is going way beyond what is required. Similarly, there is little information on how prices are obtained or what drugs are subject to rebates. There is a lot of talk about how great [these deals] are but no transparency about what they actually do."

The analysts all acknowledged that many reforms have little hope of being enacted since Democrats don't have a veto-proof majority in the Senate. However, Mirken said a veto override isn't beyond the realm of possibility in today's political climate. "It's worth trying and people need to see [that] this new Congress is trying to fix the damage."

#5788. NASA's Swift Space Telescope Re-Enters Atmosphere With Five Times More Risk Of Casualties Than Anticipated

On Wednesday, NASA's Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory, a space telescope that's been in orbit since 2004, finally re-entered Earth's atmosphere above Indonesia. However, it appears to have done so with a risk of casualties five times beyond what NASA technical standards mandate. How and why this happened isn't immediately clear; NASA Public Affairs Specialist Alise Fisher told Jalopnik, "In the leadup to Swift's Nov. 20, 2004, launch, NASA determined the risk was consistent with mission requirements as allowed by agency policy." Somewhere between launch and reentry, that risk assessment shifted. It's unlikely that anybody made a specific mistake; this is probably the result of, well, space being hard. Still, it does highlight that our main defense against the detritus of astronomical ambitions is pure luck.

Swift is only crashing to Earth because it ran out of fuel to stay in orbit or adjust its trajectory. To NASA's credit, it did throw $30 million at a private company in an unprecedented attempt to rescue Swift with another satellite that would pull it back up into safe orbit. Sadly, the rescue satellite itself failed, dooming Swift to this fate.

In formal terms, Swift suffered an "uncontrolled reentry," which basically means it had no propulsion and just fell from space. NASA's own requirements, most recently updated in 2021 but going all the way back to 2001, state: "For uncontrolled reentry, the risk of human casualty from surviving debris shall be less than 0.0001 (1:10,000)." If you're not aware, there's no actual law requiring that satellites de-orbit in some sort of safe way that won't, you know, crash into a city or kill anyone. So our shield against the skies is just statistics. This is the threshold at which NASA feels the risk is acceptable. For reference, in all of history, just about 10,000 satellites have de-orbited, so... statistically we're due. And that was before we were putting tens of thousands of satellites up every few years.

In Swift's instance, however, NASA played its cards a little close to its chest. For one thing, it did not put out a risk assessment for the reentry, which is unusual. Then, in the middle of a longer announcement, the agency mentioned that Swift's risk of human casualties was actually "1 in 2,100." That's a risk of human injury or death five times beyond what it was meant to be.

Fortunately, the odds appear to have resolved in our favor. There are no reports of anyone being struck by space debris from Swift. Most of the satellite was expected to burn up during reentry; the trouble would come from whatever survived, likely dense pieces like the telescope lens or the reaction wheels. If any of those made it to the surface, there doesn't appear to have been a person around it.

That's a roll of the dice, though. At some point, as thousands of satellites de-orbit per year in our new megaconstellation era, laws should be passed to manage this formally. That would likely mean requiring controlled reentries, when the satellite still has a little fuel left to navigate to a safe spot out in the ocean. The other option, which companies like SpaceX have been pursuing, is to design the satellite to burn up entirely on reentry. The issue there is what effect all that vaporized metal might have on the atmosphere. Scientists don't yet know the answer, for the simple reason that there was never a reason to ask that question before. Now, the health of our planet may depend on it.

As space gets busier and busier, more and more will fall to us down here from up there. Up until now, the power of statistics has kept us safe. But eventually, a 1 in 10,000 chance is going to happen. Especially if some of those chances turn out to be riskier than intended.
