#5787. Latin America EV Sales Report: With Over 200,000 Units Sold in Q3, EVs Now Stand at 12% Market Share!

Through 2026, EVs have gained significant momentum all over the world (safe for the US), and Latin America has been no exception. Sales grew by almost 140% in the third quarter, and clock in at a very healthy 120% growth through 2026, bringing the yearly total to more than half a million units. If this trend continues, it’s all but certain we will surpass 700,000 units by the end of the year, and perhaps even get to 800,000.

Another interesting bit of trivia is that sales reached 220,000 units in the third quarter, not too far behind the USA’s 266,000. In theory, this means it should be possible for LatAm to surpass total US EV sales as early as Q1 2027 … though, I would be much happier if the USA’s market starts growing strongly again and thus Latin America cannot get ahead.

Now, to be fair, the US is much more BEV-heavy (242,000 units sold, vs 127,000 in LatAm), whereas Latin America prefers PHEVs to a higher degree (93,000 units sold vs 44,000 in the US), but, still, these are impressive results for a region overall much poorer than the US, and with a vehicle market around a third of the size. For over a decade, it was the richest countries in the world that led EV adoption, but — as many of us predicted on this very site — that has now changed.

As mentioned earlier, total EV sales reached 220,000 units, surpassing for the first time the 200,000 mark and growing a healthy 140% YoY, and a strong 17% from Q2. BEVs grew faster than PHEVs (146% vs 130%), and thus now represent 57.5% of all EV sales in the region, slightly below the 58% result for Q2.

Market share grew to 12%, up from 10.7% in Q2 and 5.5% in Q3 last year, a pretty strong result. This brought market share above 10% through 2026, 5.9% BEV and 4.3% PHEV.

Latin America’s overall vehicle market is growing at rates of around 10% annually, but thanks to strong EV growth, combustion-based powertrains (HEV, MHEV and ICEV) are stagnant: they fell by 3.7% last quarter, and barely grew by 3.1% this one, meaning it’s very likely that we’re reaching “Peak Combustion” in Latin America and the market will only go down from 2027 onwards.

Sales wise, we have a pretty stable list, with Brazil being the unsurprising winner, followed by Mexico and Colombia, just as in Q2. Uruguay follows, but then we have a new rising star: Ecuador, which nearly reached 5,000 BEVs sold last quarter! Costa Rica, Chile, and Argentina close the top 8 and make up all the countries with over 1.000 quarterly BEV sales.

PHEV sales are much more varied, though. Brazil, of course, leads again, but following come Mexico and Argentina, with Colombia in 4th place and Ecuador in 5th.

Market share: again, we have a pretty constant list, with the first 8 positions unchanged. Uruguay, unsurprisingly, leads the region with a very strong 50.7% EV market share (45.7% BEV), followed by Costa Rica, Colombia, Ecuador, and Brazil. Notable mention to Paraguay, the sixth country in our region to reach 10% EV market share through a quarter, but in this case thanks to PHEVs.

This has been an eventful year due to Donald’s dubious defense decisions, but even as high oil prices have been driving massive EV adoption and/or massive ICEV crashes all over the world, I feel the situation in Latin America isn’t as clear cut.

In the US and Europe, fuel prices have gone up by around 40–50%, at least in the case of gasoline. Now, prices have gone up similarly in some countries in Latin America, including Panama, Paraguay and Perú, but in many others, like Brazil, Colombia and Mexico, it has barely moved, either because of significant subsidies — direct or indirect — to maintain prices, or because it was already expensive before the war and the government did not make any adjustments to its price.

One would think that the countries most affected by high fuel prices would be at the forefront of the EV transition, but no: the largest increases in EV sales only partially track gasoline prices. For example: Panama and Perú are still lagging behind the region despite having the highest increase in the region (>50% in both cases), whereas Colombia and Brazil are sprinting ahead despite relatively stable fuel prices. What gives?

The answer, I believe, is linked to EV prices and how they compare with their ICEV competition. Yes, I know the TCO is a very important metric, but evidence in our markets point to the sticker price being the main driver of adoption. The cases of Brazil and Colombia on one hand, and Peru and Panama on the other, exemplify this very well:

Brazil and Colombia have had pretty stable fuel prices, up around 10% or less since the war started, yet both have booming markets, up 160% and 190%, respectively, with EV market share getting close to 20%. Meanwhile, Panama and Peru have seen gasoline prices spike over 50% from their pre-war costs, yet the former only showed a meager 57% growth in September, and the latter, though clocking in at a more impressive 270% YoY, remains at a disappointing 1.3% market share.

The main difference has to do with the nature of the market. In Brazil and Colombia, combustion vehicles are relatively expensive — in no small part due to tariffs to protect local industry — and EVs have effectively reached price parity with their combustion counterparts. Meanwhile, in Panama and Per,u EVs are just as cheap if not cheaper than in Brazil or Colombia, but combustion cars are significantly cheaper, with some entry-level models at or below USD$10.,00. This makes customers hesitant to try the new technologies by the simple logic that even if the EV will save a lot of money in the long run, $6,000 will buy a lot of gas.

And in Peru, this is particularly relevant insofar as gas-mobiles from Chinese brands are very popular. Changan, Jetour, Geely, and JAC together account for 20% of the Peruvian market largely thanks to their hyper-affordable combustion cars, and since the Chinese gas-mobile market is collapsing, it’s likely these brands would rather keep it like this for a few more months so they can move some of the inventory they won’t be able to sell at home before betting it all on EVs.

In general, this tracks for most markets, where EV prices compared to traditional combustion cars are probably the most relevant factor to keep track of. And of course, fuel prices do have an influence, but it seems it’s rather limited compared to, for example, what we’re seeing in Europe.

But the lessons are clear: Want to improve EV sales? Either make them cheaper, or make gas-mobiles more expensive.

At the beginning of this year, I was hopeful we would see 10% market share in the fourth quarter, normally the highest in the year. We went past that in Q2, and at 12%, we’re sitting far beyond what I considered feasible only 10 months ago.

Yet, there’s still a long path ahead. Aside from the 5 leading markets, we’re yet to see a country getting consistent BEV market share above 5%, and several large markets — Chile, Argentina, and Mexico — are still lagging. PHEVs have raised the bar in several countries, Paraguay most important of all, but we would rather see more BEVs.

This is not to be overtly pessimistic. The results so far are great, much better than we would’ve expected even a year ago. But as things improve, the bar rises, and I’m now waiting to see the moment when we will see 20% market share (Q3 2027?) or when e-truck sales will start to ramp up (2027? 2028?).

Passionate for all things Latin American, I’ve been closely following the energy and mobility transitions since they started to become present south of the equator.

#5786. Open Your Mind And The 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe Will Blow It

Appealing to enthusiasts and buyers of performance cars is already hard enough with internal combustion engines, whether we're talking affordable hot hatchbacks or high-end supercars. As many automakers have been learning the hard way, it's orders of magnitude tougher to build and sell a good electric car to those same enthusiasts. They want their cars to feel real, with tactile sensations and character and emotion and all those other valid clichés, and few (if any) EVs have achieved that or even really gotten close.

Now, the wizards at Affalterbach have done it. The 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe has unbelievable performance figures when it comes to power and speed as well as efficiency and charging, it has advanced technology features and F1-derived aerodynamics, and months after the reveal its design still has everybody talking, to say the least. Most importantly, it is absolute dynamite to drive – a grand slam, a home run, the automotive equivalent of an EGOT, however else you want to say it – and much of that is down to how it emulates a combustion powertrain, complete with V8 noises and shifts that will stretch your definition of "real."

Head of development Oliver Wiech told me that even the most staunch anti-EV car enthusiasts, from people on Mercedes' own engineering teams to its dealers and long-time AMG customers, have been completely enamored by the electric GT once they've experienced it for themselves. Regardless of how you feel about its styling or its powertrain or its perceived gimmicks, if you go into a drive in the new Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe with an open mind, prepare to have your mind blown.

Full disclosure: Mercedes flew me to Málaga and put me up in a lovely hotel so I could spend two days driving the GT55 on the road and the GT63 on track at Ascari. I spilled a latte on my MacBook Pro on the first day of the trip, so if you see any typos in here, I blame the crappy PC I've had to write this on.

In many ways this car is the heir to the first-generation Mercedes CLS, particularly because of its sloping roofline that's the reason for the four-door coupe moniker, and thankfully the new GT is much easier to get in and out of than both the original CLS (which I own) and the first-gen GT 4-Door Coupe that this EV replaces. Even people much taller than my 5-foot-9 self won't really have to worry about smacking their head when getting in or fitting comfortably in the seat. Despite being almost two inches lower in height than the old GT 4-Door, its interior seems far more spacious, even in the back seats.

I settle into a sports car-like driving position with a low H-point and a great view out ahead; the rear window seems small at first but visibility isn't that bad through it, and the C-pillar blind spot is better than in my 20-year-old CLS. Progress! All of the cars I drive are equipped with the optional performance seats with integrated headrests, which look cool and are very supportive and comfortable, even after spending hours in the car. They have a heat function and adjustable bolsters and lumbar support, but unfortunately if you want ventilation and massage (and I do want them) you have to stick with the still-supportive base seats.

Aside from some flourishes like the center screen canted toward the driver and the surrounds of the air vents, the GT's interior feels much like any other new Mercedes EV, and that same feeling extends to how it drives – in Comfort mode, at least. This is an easy car to drive around the city, with standard six-degree rear-wheel steering giving it a tight turning radius. The cars we're driving have staggered-width 21-inch wheels wearing Continental SportContact summer tires, and even with that big rubber the optional Active Ride Control hydraulic suspension keeps the ride firm and sporty but never uncomfortable or rough. It's certainly much more comfortable than the last-gen GT, which had a horrible ride.

When still in Comfort, I try out the first of the three Race Engineer knobs on the center console, which adjusts the responsiveness of the throttle pedal. (More on the other two later.) The dial goes from 1 to 9, with 1 being the least-responsive and 9 being the most, and boy does turning it up all the way make a difference. In addition to adjusting the actual responsiveness of the motors, the physical feel of the pedal changes, giving you less resistance when you press it like when cycling through regen braking settings.

On the road we're driving GT55s, which have one electric motor on the front axle and two on the rear axle. Those motors are axial-flux units created by YASA, a British company that was acquired by Mercedes a few years ago. (YASA already has similar motors in plug-in-hybrid supercars like the Koenigsegg Regera and Ferrari 296.) The rear motors are combined together in a housing with planetary gearsets, an oil cooling system and two water-cooled silicon carbide inverters; the motors can spin to more than 13,000 rpm. The front motor, which also has an SiC inverter and a spur transmission, is activated when more power or traction are needed and can reach over 15,000 rpm. They're juiced up by an 800-volt battery pack with a usable capacity of 106 kWh, which has Formula 1-derived tech like skinny cylindrical cells and an electrically non-conductive cooling oil that flows between them all.

AMG's main goal with the GT's powertrain and the dedicated AMG.EA platform it rides on was sustained, repeatable performance in all aspects – especially important when there's this much power on tap. The GT55 has a peak output of 805 hp when using launch control or Boost mode (output is 577 hp continuous) and a massive 1,372 pound-feet of torque. Accelerating from 0 to 60 mph takes only 2.7 seconds (or 2.4 seconds with a 1-foot rollout included), and it'll reach 124 mph in 9.0 seconds (also three tenths quicker with the same rollout). It feels absurdly quick both from a stop and when accelerating at speed, even when in Comfort.

Switch the car into Race mode and the suspension hunkers down and tightens up, the front motor is permanently engaged, and special powertrain preconditioning is turned on. It's hard to think of a better point-to-point car than this, especially if your route includes spectacularly twisty mountain roads like the area around Ascari. The Active Ride suspension keeps the car extremely flat and stable in corners by using hydraulically interconnected dampers instead of mechanical anti-roll bars, and I promise you the car feels much lighter than its 5,501-pound curb weight suggests. Its steering doesn't have quite as much feedback as a Porsche Taycan, but it's sharp and direct, maybe the best-tuned setup that AMG currently offers.

The tri-motor setup allows for fully variable torque vectoring between the rear wheels, and with stability control in its regular on setting, there is an unstoppable amount of grip. Power out of a corner hard and the traction control light flashes, the car blasting ahead with zero drama. Put it into ESC Sport and the GT gets looser and more playful, the tail kicking out in easily controlled little slides without feeling like the computers are constantly reigning in the fun.

Very strong one-pedal regenerative braking can be dialed in using the steering wheel paddles; while I really enjoy driving fast EVs with just the throttle, and it's great to do so in the AMG, I prefer turning the regen to a milder setting and actually using the brake pedal. It's a hydraulic brake-by-wire system, with the GT coming with 16.5-inch carbon-ceramic brake discs up front and 14.9-inch steel discs in the rear. There's great initial bite and pedal feel, with seemingly no loss of performance after some hard driving. This setup is more than strong enough for most people, but I'd want to spec the optional ceramic high-performance compound braking system, partially just because it adds beefier carbon brakes to the rear that look a lot better than the base setup. Those stronger stoppers are part of a package that includes bronze calipers and a 186-mph top speed, instead of the normal 161 mph.

If that was all and the review ended there, I would say the AMG GT is one of the best EVs (or any sort of car) on the market. Once you try the AMGFORCE Sport+ mode with its V8 noises and shifts, though, you won't want to drive it in any other mode again. In fact, you probably won't want to ever stop driving the GT, and no other EV will scratch the same itch. It's hard to think of the right words to describe it. I definitely wouldn't call it "fake", and "simulated" doesn't feel right either. Saying the noises are "synthesized" is probably the way to go, but really it doesn't matter at all. Who cares that the sounds and shifts are not "real" when the end result is this absurdly good?

AMG recorded more than 1,600 sound files of the previous-gen GT R's twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8, capturing every situation from every possible angle, and they're mixed in real-time to match driver inputs and what the powertrain is doing. That means you're never hearing the same noises twice, and everything feels unique and organic to how you're driving the car. The same goes for how it "shifts" when you leave the car to its own devices, though you of course can and should use the paddles to shift, too. Put anyone into the GT as either a passenger or a driver without telling them what's powering the car, and they'll all think there's actually a V8 under the hood. It adds a ton of engagement and sensation to an already engaging drive, eliciting feelings of sheer joy and excitement.

First of all, it's loud, but not in a way that's obnoxious or too much. If you switch into AMGForce Sport+ when the car is stopped it'll burst to life sounding just like the real V8 on startup, but it's much funnier if you change the drive mode at speed, since the car will kick itself into a low gear with the revs up pretty high, resulting in a hilariously good sound. Keep the car in Sport+ around town and it emits wonderful burbles, while at cruising speeds it fades into the background and shifts smoothly like a V8 would. Drop a few gears and start driving more energetically, though, and it gets really gnarly. As my drive partner and friend of Jalopnik, Roberto Baldwin, points out, the placement of the speakers and the rumbling exciters in the seats make it sound like the car is mid-engined, or that there are big side-exit exhaust right underneath us. It roars under acceleration, utters pops and bangs on "overrun," barks when I shift. Nothing about the noises sound artificial, unnatural or repetitive.

It's just freakin' awesome, and after just a few minutes you might totally forget that you're in an EV at all. Except that even in this mode, with shifts that come with an authentic cut in torque, the GT's acceleration still gives the tunnel-vision feeling you'll only get from an EV. Engineers tell me that the GT is just as quick to accelerate and fast around a track with the shifts on as it would be with them turned off, and in fact the aural and physical feedback will probably help you post even better lap times than you otherwise would.

Crack off shifts with the paddles, and you'd swear there's a real DCT transmission in here. There's an urgency and gut punch quality to the upshifts, and downshifts can be quite forceful too. Using the vibrators in the seats, the car mimics the mechanical clunks and thumps you'd feel in an ICE car's drivetrain. When in S+ you can't mess with the regen, which is set to the minimum level, but downshifting does aid in deceleration, like strong engine braking. It will "rev" to 7,200 rpm, and the car will hold gears for pretty long and upshift right at redline.

You can hear the sounds outside the car as well, and it's loud enough that we can hear GTs driving on track from pretty far away. If you really want to be obnoxious you can park the car, put it in neutral and rev it, putting any nearby Dodge Charger Daytonas to shame. Keep your foot pinned to the floor and it will bounce off the redline seemingly indefinitely – we wouldn't know, because after about ten seconds of doing it for a TikTok, a few Germans walked over to ask us to please shut the hell up.

The GT's Boost function is also at its best in S+, though it's available in all drive modes. Pull both paddles at once and it instantly activates, providing maximum power for a long time. Like, a really long time. Most cars that offer a Boost do so for 10, 15, maybe 20 seconds, and sometimes you have to wait before activating it again. That's one of the big benefits of the GT's powertrain – in the GT55 the boost lasts for 55 seconds while in the GT63 it'll stay on for 63 seconds, and as soon as it finishes or you manually turn it off, you can immediately activate it again. One AMG representative told me the boost could theoretically last forever, and they needed to come up with an arbitrary time limit. It is actually nice to have it last so long, like if you're coming up on a particularly complex and lengthy set of switchbacks. Turning on the Boost always comes with louder sounds and a countdown timer in the gauge cluster, but with the V8 activated it's a lot more dramatic. The engine noises sound like they get 25% louder, and the shifts feel even more aggressive. It rules.

OK, sure, you might try the AMGFORCE mode and still come out hating the sounds and shifts, even though I can't imagine not loving it. At least you can just switch into one of the other drive modes and it all turns off. It is really nice to be able to go back into Comfort once I'm on the freeway or get stuck behind slow-moving traffic on a twisty road, the car immediately settling back down into a silent, swift electric cruiser. Our group of journalists was unanimous about one thing during dinner conversations with AMG execs and engineers, though: We want to be able to turn on all the AMGFORCE stuff in all modes from Comfort to Race, not just be restricted to Sport+. It really is that compelling, and that's something Mercedes could easily change with an over-the-air software update.

Good as the mountain roads are, AMG's real reason for bringing us to this part of Spain is so we can drive the GT around Circuito Ascari, a track you certainly recognize from "Top Gear" and racing games. Luckily I've been here once before, for the launch of the 992.2 Carrera GTS, so the track is a little less daunting. For this part of the day we swap into GT63s, which have the tri-motor setup boosted to produce a peak of 1,153 hp (711 hp continuous) and 1,475 lb-ft of torque. When accelerating to 60 mph the GT63 is 0.4 second quicker than the GT55, both when including or excluding that rollout, and it'll hit 124 mph a much more noticeable 2.3 seconds quicker than the GT55.

We complete our first two laps in Comfort mode (and at quite a fast pace immediately), with the car feeling fast and fun enough that you won't hate your sighting laps when first starting a track day, and on the second lap we play around with the responsiveness dial. For laps three and four AMGForce Sport+ gets activated, and our instructors talk about how nice the feedback is for executing cleaner and faster laps, and how much more fun it is to rip around a track with the V8 soundtrack turned on. After doing one lap with stability control on, we switch it to ESC Sport for the next lap to experience the difference in grip and slip that it allows.

Then we switch into Race mode to try out the other two Race Engineer dials. In the middle of the array is the agility control, which messes with the torque vectoring and chassis systems to simulate shortening the wheelbase for, well, increased agility, and to change the car's handling behavior from favoring a bit of understeer to a lot of oversteer. At the bottom is the traction dial, which adjusts the level of traction control intervention – at the loosest 9 setting, up to 90% of torque can be sent to the rear wheels. These two are only available to change yourself when stability control has been fully turned off; with it on, in Sport+ mode the car defaults to agility at 5 and traction at 2.

For the first lap, we crank all three dials down to 1, which means the powertrain is the least responsive, it's the least agile, and the traction control intervenes the most. Even set up like this the car is great to drive fast, but it's clear that the GT is being held back – traction control is butting in more prominently, turn in isn't super sharp, it favors a bit of understeer on corner entry and exit, and torque is obviously being deployed more sensitively.

The next lap we crank the responsiveness up to 9 and turn the agility and traction dials to 5, and the car's demeanor completely changes. It eagerly dives into corners and rotates like a sports car should, the rear end kicking out quite a bit as I accelerate out of the apex while giving it a dab of oppo, as so many British dudes have probably said to camera on this track. The GT feels so much more lively now, and still totally controllable. On the following lap we turn agility to 7, and the car feels even more precise. Sadly we don't get to try it out with everything turned all the way to 9, going back into Comfort for a cool-down lap, but if we were to do so, things would get quite lairy. There is a lot of video evidence out there of just how effortlessly the GT will drift, or pull off instant burnouts that would shame any muscle car.

Will buyers of this car take them to track days and actually mess with this stuff? Probably not, but they really should. It's fast as hell but fun too, an extremely competent car on track that can be perfectly adapted to anyone's driving skills and desires, allowing you to unlock more capability when you want.

Mercedes hasn't released U.S. range estimates for the GT yet, and neither has the EPA, but I do have some real-world data to give you. Depending on wheel and tire setup Mercedes says the GT55 has a range of between 371 miles and 435 miles on the European WLTP cycle; with a 91% charge at the start of our drive day, the GT55 shows an estimated range of about 300 miles, and that's with it calculating based on how the cars have been driven along the same routes in the hands of other journalists. At the start of my track session the GT63 has 80% charge, and after the eight laps the charge sits at 60%, which ain't bad. The range estimate only dropped by 30 miles, too.

In order to keep the baker's dozen of GTs juiced up for all our track sessions and routes to and from the hotel, AMG brought a bunch of fast chargers to Ascari, including one massive Alpitronic rig that is part of the charging company's partnership with Mercedes. This machine can dole out more than 1,000 kW of power, which the GTXX concept was able to handle and is a goal for future models. The production GT can "only" handle 600 kW of charging power, according to Mercedes. That's still more than nearly any station in the U.S. can provide, and it can take the GT from 10% to 80% in only 11 minutes. In a demo using that Alpitronic monster, we see a GT charge from 17% to 80% in exactly 10 minutes, reaching a peak of 647 kW, and that's with the charger experiencing infrastructure problems and not operating as well as it could.

Thanks to AMG's thermal management system, the harder you drive the more primed the car is for fast-charging. In one of the gauge cluster's many display options you can see the temperature of the battery and motors, and it'll show how much fast-charging power the car is ready for. Doing spirited driving in the canyons it easily shows well over 400 kW available, and when that demo GT63 came right off a track session to charge, it was receiving more than 560 kW in less than a second after being plugged in. As soon as a fast-charging session is finished, the car is ready to go right back on track and keep driving at maximum attack. There's no loss or delay in power delivery, energy and thermal efficiency, or anything else that could hold the car back.

Now I'm not going to try and change anyone's opinions when it comes to how the new AMG GT looks, but I do think it could really grow on you, especially once you start seeing them in the dozens and dozens of different paint colors that Mercedes offers through the Manufaktur program. The turbine wheel design you see above looks fantastic, and gets you more range. The interior is also offered in some great leather schemes too, like a classy full beige or bright blue or yellow, and you don't have to get carbon-fiber trim inside.

At the very least, the new GT's proportions and silhouette are a big improvement over the outgoing car. It's lower and longer, with a longer wheelbase and dash-to-axle ratio plus a more rakish, shark-like nose. With the 21-inch wheels its stance is pretty killer, and I genuinely do love a lot of the details, like the execution of the AMG grille and the trio of afterburner-style taillights set into that black panel. It's pretty practical too, with the liftback rear end opening to reveal 14.7 cubic feet of cargo space.

This may be the rare car where a passenger screen is something you'll actually want to use, at least if you're a bit of a data nerd like me and Roberto. When each of us sit shotgun while the other drives, we can't stop swiping through all of the performance data pages available on the 14-inch passenger touchscreen. (You can pull them up in the center screen, too, and there are a bunch of different designs for the gauge cluster that can also tell you performance info.)

These show the current rpm, torque and electrical output of the motors; pedal inputs, steering angle, suspension compression and g-forces; temperature of the motors and battery pack; the position of the various active dynamic elements such as the deploying rear diffuser; and an IWC stopwatch, among other things. The animations and graphics are all excellent, and looking through it all while the driver is really putting the hammer down must feel like how cats do when they watch birds at a feeder.

Somehow, this performance comes at a reasonable price, at least as far as this class of car is concerned. The GT55 will start at $145,000 before a $1,550 destination charge, putting it right between the Taycan 4S and GTS in terms of price. That also makes it about ten grand cheaper than the outgoing GT63 4-Door, the cheapest V8-powered one that made do with a meager 575 horsepower. For that price you get tons of standard active-safety features, a Burmester 3D surround sound system, fancy digital headlights, three-stage adjustable air springs, and the trio of screens. (A dual-motor GT53 with less power and equipment is coming later in 2027, but we don't have pricing details yet.)

The GT63 will start at $175,000 before a destination fee that Mercedes hasn't announced yet, making it about $25,000 cheaper than the old GT63 S E Performance PHEV and ten grand cheaper than a Taycan Turbo. That also undercuts a Lucid Air Sapphire by almost $100,000. It gets the Active Ride Control suspension, Race Engineer dials, Race mode, the Drift mode and AMG Track Pace features, all of which are available in the GT55's Dynamic Plus package. Other standard features on the GT63 include ventilated and massaging front seats, Nappa leather, a head-up display, and Magic Vision Control wipers.

Available on all GT 4-Door Coupes are a Sky Control panoramic sunroof with dimmable glass and illuminated AMG logos, soft-close doors, a three-person rear seat instead of the standard two, an even fancier Burmester sound system, and all sorts of styling options. An exterior lighting package adds a lightbar between the headlights, a light strip along the trunk edge, and illumination to the grille bars and surround; I would definitely get this, but lots of people prefer the front end without the light bar.

As awesome as it is, at the end of the day this is a niche sort of vehicle regardless of what's powering it. The GT 4-Door is never going to be a major seller for AMG, though the brand is very optimistic about its prospects around the globe, including here in the States. What makes the electric GT truly exciting is what it means for the future of AMG, and performance cars as a whole. Its axial-flux motors, advanced battery solutions, dynamic focus and spectacular sounds will spread across the AMG lineup – we've already seen its AMG GT SUV twin and the "entry-level" 671-hp CLA45, and models like the GLC and C-Class are coming with over 900 hp – and just think what other brands might dream up once they've driven one.

Like all the best magic acts and wonders of the world, the 2027 Mercedes-AMG GT 4-Door Coupe must be seen — or rather, heard and experienced — to be believed. This is a car that could truly win over even the most extreme internal combustion enthusiasts, as long as they'll open their minds and let it.

#5785. The US Midterm Election Results Remind Us To Focus On The “Foundation Of Our Democracy”

The country feels different as the US midterm election results come rolling in. When all of the votes are counted, Democrats are set to win the national popular vote in all House races by about 13 percentage points. The voters have spoken in huge numbers: they want leaders who make everyday life a little bit easier.

Now the politicians must answer, and, essentially, they need to design policies that lower costs for ordinary people in the US. It’s not about revenge: revenge is too distinct from US consumers’ lived experiences.

Paul Krugman, the Nobel Prize-winning economist, says that one of the main things “Democrats need to do now is hold not just Trump’s officials, but also his enablers, accountable.” Representative Robert Garcia of California posted on social media in the same vein: “Lawyer Up” to the MAGA losers.

No, that’s playground antics. Instead, the next two years must demonstrate a willingness to listen to US citizens as each family struggles to make ends meet with groceries, household utilities, healthcare, and other daily costs.

Roy Cooper’s Senate campaign in North Carolina had a mantra that permeated every stop on his statewide tour: Make Stuff Cost Less.

James Talarico, Jon Ossoff, Roy Cooper, among others, grounded their campaigns in economic populist agendas. They harangued against President Trump’s economic record and called out corruption for what it is — self-promotion over the interest of US citizens.

Georgia’s Senator-elect Jon Ossoff made Trump’s assault on the rule of law practical. In everyday terms he pointed out to voters how democracy and economic distress depend on each other. He argued that “corruption is why nothing works for ordinary people.”

We are ready as a nation to reintroduce the importance of science to accomplish these goals.

In a recent commentary in the journal, Nature, a group of authors argue that “science is an essential backbone of democracy.” Science as a source of discovery, expertise, and innovation is intertwined, they continue, with democracy as a set of institutions, rules, and procedures. They put forward three pillars through which science enables democracy:

Citizens have legitimate concerns, the scientists agree, “about economic restructuring, regional inequities, how migration is governed, technological changes, ageing populations, climate risks and geopolitical instability.” Then again, “expertise strengthens democratic legitimacy when it is transparent, diverse, independent and open to scrutiny — as prescribed by the rules of science.”

Democracy — when it lives up to its responsibility as the rule of law — contributes to a decrease in CO2 emissions. Climate change, like voting in a democracy, is a collective action problem — a conflict between the individual interest and the group interest.

Congress can look ahead and back science-based clean energy in a variety of ways, from policies to investment, all of which will address the affordability crisis, improve public health, and protect the environment, says Gretchen Colman, president of the Union of Concerned Scientists. That will include renewables and clean transportation, modernizing the power grid, protecting our air and water, and more.

Science-based legislating, Colman continues, must be based “on facts and evidence, not political whims. We’ll petition Congress to restore funding for science, protect federal science agencies, and re-establish safeguards for federal scientists to carry out their work and communicate about it free from political interference.”

A national return to scientific reasoning is underway. Gina Hinojosa, elected as the first Texas Democratic governor, has called for an immediate moratorium on data center development. She has accused developers of “raising our utility rates and depleting our water.” As reported on Bloomberg Green, she’s vowed to halt approvals until “a legislative session that puts Texans first so that we don’t have any more communities in Texas who are powerless against these global corporations.”

Indeed, the output of data centers must also be questioned. Few AI-generated findings are peer-reviewed or tested in laboratories. Talk about mixed messages! We return to a nostalgic past by foregrounding large language models that are programmed to present particular, narrow, and self-serving points of view — generally by billionaires seeking to protect their wealth. It’s time to tax these rare individuals accordingly, eliminate any incentives for their built structures, and require them to provide major renewable energy contributions to communities that house them.

Yes, energy plays an increasingly important role in today’s world. Instead of treating electrification as an option to move us toward decarbonization, it’s time to see it is essential — we need to electrify everything quickly. In the US, more than 60 state and local governments are similarly in court to seek damages from oil companies. These are the lawsuits that Democrats in Congress should be pursuing as complements to transitioning virtually all building, transportation, and industrial systems to electric. This means subsidizing heat pumps and EVs and solar panels and home battery storage — subsidies are far cheaper than funding wars for oil.

The ground-breaking election events have hoisted non-traditional candidates to levels of power and influence, as the New York Times chronicles:

Political analyst Chris Armitrage suggests another important positive trend. “The thing that lasts past January 2027 is the voter who had never voted in a midterm, did it in 2026, and now has the habit,” he concludes.

The urgency of climate change has brought democracy to a critical moment in time. Whether longtime, non-traditional, or new voters, we must continue to argue for inclusive and participatory democracy to address climate change. Shaped by democratic governance, a robust civil society, and innovative advocacy strategies, collective climate action can be a force for the right to a healthy planet.

#5783. At $11,995, Could This 2013 Cadillac Escalade Hybrid Make You 'Slade In The Shade?

Today's Nice Price or No Dice Cadillac Escalade is a rare Hybrid model with two electric motors sandwiched between its V8 engine and 4WD. Once very expensive, we'll have to see if this Champagne-hued luxury SUV now fits our beer budget.

For the longest time, Bentley Motors and Rolls-Royce were intrinsically intertwined, with Bentley often getting the short shrift in the partnership. In the latter half of the 20th Century, Bentley's cars often felt like an afterthought compared to the Parthenon-grilled Rolls models. That's an unfortunate bit of history, as before Rolls-Royce bought Bentley in 1931, the latter company built entire automobiles, rather than just foisting chassis and drivetrains on commissioned coachbuilders like the former.

The 1997 Bentley Brooklands we looked at on Wednesday attempted to distinguish itself from its near-twin Rolls-Royce Silver Spirit by offering a tauter suspension, toned-down body-color grille surround, and a turbocharger bolted to its 6.75-liter V8 engine. Presented in excellent condition and, in the seller's words, "needing nothing," our Bentley came with an $18,000 asking price. That's about a tenth of what the car cost new, but when it came down to brass tacks, opinions were fairly evenly split on its value. Ultimately, the brave—or foolhardy—among us lost out, as the Bentley bombed in an excruciatingly narrow 52% 'No Dice' loss.

Regardless of outcome, yesterday's Bentley was a textbook example of what the ultimate in luxury travel was intended to be in the later decades of the 20th Century. By comparison, the 2013 Cadillac Escalade Hybrid that comes our way today is a more modern execution of that concept.

The Escalade, of course, is the vehicle that arguably saved Cadillac. Originally a slapped-together version of the GMC Denali SUV, the Escalade proved so successful that GM added additional versions and further differentiation with later iterations. Those included the comically weird ESV version based on the Chevy Avalanche pickup. One of the rarest Escalade models is the initial 4WD Hybrid, produced between 2009 and 2013.

The Hybrid Escalade's party piece is the dual-mode AHS2 (Advanced Hybrid System 2, Electric Boogaloo), which replaces the traditional automatic transmission. Co-developed by GM, Daimler-Chrysler, and BMW, the hybrid drive unit was used in several of GM's SUVs, along with Mercedes and BMW sedans and the Dodge Durango. Dual mode means the truck can be powered by the gasoline engine, one of the 60-kilowatt electric motors, or a combination of both. Another 60kW electric motor serves as a generator for the nickel-metal hydride battery pack. This was technology that all three partners in the development consortium thought would pave the way to greater efficiency, but ultimately proved overly complicated for the task. All three dropped the hybrid drive unit by the end of the 2013 model year.

This Escalade Hybrid is one result of that short-lived partnership and product run. According to the ad, it has done 140,000 miles (132k according to the text, 140k according to the sidebar listing details — we'll go with the larger number) and, aside from some minor wear and tear, presents admirably for those miles.

Naturally, the most expensive component of any hybrid or fully electric vehicle is the battery, and it's reassuring to know the pack in this Escalade was replaced at 90K. That gives it at least another 40K if the current pack has the same lifespan as the first.

On the gas side of things, there's a 6.0-liter LZ1 V8, making 332 horsepower and 367 pound-feet of torque all by its lonesome. Specs indicate that, combined with the electric motors, power jumps to 379 horses.

Per the seller, the HV battery isn't the only new component this truck has seen. It also has new shocks and suspension components, refreshed brake pads, and new tires on its chrome-plated factory alloys. The ad claims no issues with the truck's performance, saying that it has been "extremely well maintained with all the expensive items already taken care of."

Overall, the truck looks as nice as its description lets on. The exterior, appropriately enough in metallic Champagne, is said to only suffer from some minor scuffs on the rear bumper cap. Other than that, it still shines and is matched with clear, unclouded headlamp covers.

The interior looks lived-in but far from abused. The leather on the driver's seat has the patina of many posteriors, but seems fully intact. So too does the leather on the steering wheel, albeit with a glossy sheen expected given the truck's age and miles. The only other really glaring issue is the wear evident on the fan control in the automatic climate control panel. Geez, people, just leave it on auto!

This is a three-row SUV with room for seven, so it should be as versatile as it is efficient. It comes with built-in navigation, a tow package, and, most importantly, a clean title.

When new, this Escalade commanded over $77,000 to drive it off the dealership lot. That was a long time ago, with many miles and a long-expired warranty in the rearview, so naturally it should cost less now. We need to decide whether the seller's set price of $11,995 is low enough.

Since this one comes with the promise that all the expensive work is already done, it may be a safe bet to take ownership. Also, I think the less said about the AI-generated image of the truck in the ad as some sort of weird road boat, the better.

What do you say? Is this rare hybrid Escalade worth $11,995 as it sits? Or is that too much for a truck with an abandoned technology, no matter how fancy it is?

Seattle, Washington, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5782. Kimi Antonelli Won The F1 Driver's Championship On A Cracked Oil Sump

Twenty-year-old Kimi Antonelli made history on Sunday becoming the youngest Formula 1 Drivers' Champion in the history of the sport and the first Italian driver to win it since Alberto Ascari in 1953. He clinched it finishing third at last weekend's Mexico Grand Prix, sharing the podium with fellow champs and legends in their own rights, Max Verstappen and Lewis Hamilton, who lifted Antonelli up onto their shoulders for an F1 photo op to end all F1 photo ops.

Mathematically, Antonelli's championship win was almost a foregone conclusion at that point, but his picture-perfect victory almost didn't happen that way because it's now been revealed that his final laps in Mexico City were done in a car that was very much on its last legs. Mercedes F1 deputy team principal Bradley Lord appeared on the High Performance Racing podcast after Sunday's race and said Antonelli won it all on a cracked oil sump.

"We got the car back after the race, took the floor off the bottom — covered in oil," Lord recalled. "Sump cracked, and Kimi said he thinks about four laps from the end, he could start to feel the engine just starting to struggle. So, right on the edge of retirement of that race and yet it held together." For what it's worth, non-catastrophic engine issues were a bit of a theme for Mercedes in Mexico as Antonelli's teammate George Russell, who qualified P2, suffered a power unit "deployment problem" right at the start of the race. He lost multiple places quickly and eventually finished sixth.

Cracked sump or not, it's unlikely Antonelli would've been going ham in those final stages of his race anyway as he apparently struggled to even see through his own tears before crossing the finish line as World Champion, according to Sky Sports. In the end, he finished a little over four seconds away from second-place Hamilton, who was eight-tenths off of race winner Verstappen. The Italian driver picked up 15 points, more than enough to secure the biggest trophy in motorsport earlier in life than anybody else who'd ever done it before.

#5780. This mysterious mushroom makes people see hundreds of tiny humans

People living thousands of miles apart have reported an extraordinarily strange experience after eating certain wild mushrooms. They begin seeing tiny human figures that appear to move through the real world, walking around objects, climbing over surfaces, and interacting with their surroundings.

These visions are known as Lilliputian hallucinations, named after the miniature inhabitants of "Gulliver's Travels," who stand just 6 inches tall. For decades, researchers suspected that cultural beliefs and folklore might explain why people in different regions described such similar experiences.

Now, a new study provides evidence that the phenomenon has a biological explanation.

Researchers at the University of Utah (U) used DNA sequencing to identify Lanmaoa asiatica as the mushroom responsible for these unusual hallucinations in Southwest China and the northern Philippines. Surprisingly, their investigation found no evidence of any known psychoactive mushroom compounds, including psilocybin.

The findings suggest that the mushroom may produce an entirely unfamiliar hallucinogenic substance. Identifying that chemical could help scientists better understand neurological disorders, unusual visual experiences, and the processes through which the brain creates our perception of reality.

The research was conducted by Colin Domnauer, a doctoral student at the U, and Bryn Dentinger, a mycologist affiliated with the university and the Natural History Museum of Utah. Their findings were published in the journal Mycologia.

In an interview with @theU, Domnauer explained how researchers traced the mushroom's identity, what makes its effects so unusual, and why the discovery could have implications far beyond the study of fungi.

A Century-Old Mystery of Tiny Human Hallucinations

The scientific mystery dates back to 1934, when Western researchers learned that people in Papua New Guinea sometimes experienced vivid visions of miniature humans after eating wild mushrooms. The bizarre effect became known as "mushroom madness."

During the 1950s and 1960s, anthropologists and mushroom specialists traveled to Papua New Guinea to investigate. Their work pointed toward several possible species belonging to the Boletaceae (bolete) family.

However, chemical testing failed to reveal any substances that could explain the hallucinations. Samples were even examined by Albert Hofmann, the scientist who first isolated psilocybin and discovered LSD. He, too, was unable to identify an active ingredient.

With no chemical explanation available, some researchers began questioning whether the mushrooms were responsible at all. They suggested that the visions might instead arise from cultural expectations or traditional beliefs. By the end of the 1960s, scientific investigation of the phenomenon had largely stopped.

The mystery resurfaced during the 1990s, when people in Yunnan, Southwest China, reported similar hallucinations after eating bolete mushrooms. Although the precise species remained uncertain, researchers suspected a recently described mushroom called Lanmaoa asiatica.

Scientists Identify the Mushroom Behind the Strange Visions

Another important clue appeared in 2024, when Domnauer learned of comparable experiences among an Indigenous community in the remote Northern Cordillera region of the Philippines.

Community members described a wild edible mushroom called "Sedesdem" that could sometimes cause people to see miniature human figures. Similar mushrooms were known locally as "Nonda" in Papua New Guinea and "Jian shou qing" in Yunnan.

These mushrooms have an important place in local food traditions, but consuming them without thoroughly cooking them can trigger the unusual hallucinations.

The Philippine accounts represented a third independent report of wild mushrooms causing remarkably similar visions of tiny people.

To investigate, Domnauer traveled to Yunnan and the Philippines, interviewed local residents, and collected specimens of L. asiatica along with other mushrooms belonging to the Lanmaoa genus.

The fieldwork included the first scientific survey of fungi in the northern Philippines. Researchers then analyzed DNA from the collected mushrooms to establish their identities and evolutionary relationships.

The results revealed that the mushrooms called Jian shou qing in China and Sedesdem in the Philippines were both L. asiatica.

This was an unexpected finding because scientists had previously believed the species occurred only in China.

Dentinger also visited Papua New Guinea in hopes of collecting specimens from the location where the mystery originally began. Unfortunately, unusually dry weather made it impossible to obtain samples for genetic comparison.

Although the Papua New Guinea mushroom remains unverified, the evidence from China and the Philippines provides a compelling connection. Three geographically separated cultures have described the same highly specific hallucinations, and DNA testing has linked two of those cases to a single mushroom species.

That consistency strongly suggests a shared chemical and neurological explanation rather than unrelated cultural influences.

Hallucinations of Tiny Humans That Seem Remarkably Real

What makes these hallucinations especially unusual is their striking similarity across different accounts.

People typically describe seeing dozens or even hundreds of miniature figures, generally measuring between 3 and 30 centimeters tall. The figures appear with extraordinary clarity, often seeming as solid and convincing as actual people.

According to Domnauer, approximately 90% of those experiencing the visions describe figures resembling elves, clowns, or other fairy-like characters, often wearing brightly colored clothing.

Even more surprising is the way these imaginary figures seem to behave.

Rather than floating through walls or ignoring their surroundings, they appear to follow familiar physical rules. They walk around obstacles, move across ordinary objects, and sometimes tumble from the edges of tables.

Domnauer recalled one particularly unusual account involving someone eating soup. The person reported seeing tiny humans leap from a spoon into the bowl, where they began swimming. When the person took another spoonful, the miniature figures reportedly remained visible inside their mouth.

Such detailed interactions distinguish these visions from many other forms of drug-induced hallucination.

A Mysterious Psychedelic Compound Unlike Any Known Drug

Scientists also investigated whether L. asiatica contains any of the chemicals already known to cause psychedelic experiences.

Using both chemical testing and genetic analysis, the researchers searched for established psychoactive compounds found in other mushroom species.

The absence of familiar hallucinogens was particularly intriguing because the mushroom's effects differ so dramatically from those associated with known psychedelic drugs.

Researchers now suspect that L. asiatica produces an unidentified psychoactive substance responsible for the tiny human hallucinations.

The compound has not yet been isolated or chemically identified, so its exact nature and mechanism remain unknown.

Discovering it could open an entirely new avenue of research into how certain chemicals alter visual perception.

What the Mushroom Could Reveal About the Human Brain

Although L. asiatica provides an unusually consistent trigger for Lilliputian hallucinations, the phenomenon is not limited to mushroom consumption.

Stories involving miniature humanlike beings appear throughout folklore around the world. Similar hallucinations have also been documented in people experiencing alcohol withdrawal, dementia, macular degeneration, and other neurological conditions.

These connections suggest that the visions may arise from a fundamental feature of the way the human brain processes visual information.

However, scientists still do not fully understand the neurological mechanisms responsible for these experiences or how best to treat them when they occur in medical settings.

This is where the mysterious mushroom could become especially valuable.

Unlike the neurological and medical conditions in which Lilliputian hallucinations occur relatively rarely, L. asiatica can produce the phenomenon in a more consistent and recognizable way.

If researchers can isolate the chemical responsible, they may gain a useful tool for investigating the brain circuits and processes that create these unusually detailed visual experiences.

Such research could eventually improve scientific understanding of hallucinations associated with neurological diseases.

The research team's next major objective is to isolate and identify the suspected psychedelic compound.

DNA Sequencing Reveals Four Previously Unknown Mushroom Species

The investigation also produced important discoveries about fungal biodiversity.

Rather than studying L. asiatica alone, Domnauer and Dentinger conducted the first comprehensive genomic analysis of the Lanmaoa genus.

They examined 53 mushroom specimens collected from natural habitats and preserved in fungarium collections around the world.

Genetic sequencing revealed 17 species within the group, including four species previously unknown to science.

Remarkably, two of those newly identified species had already been preserved in existing collections in the United States. Their distinct identities had gone unrecognized until researchers examined their DNA.

The analysis also indicated that L. asiatica is the only species in the genus currently known to produce psychoactive effects.

Scientists are now working to reconstruct the group's evolutionary history. They hope to determine how the unusual chemical trait developed and whether closely related mushrooms might produce comparable substances.

The findings also demonstrate the scientific value of museum collections. Specimens preserved for years can contain biological discoveries that become apparent only when newer analytical techniques are applied.

For Domnauer, the hidden diversity found in these collections is a reminder that many species and potentially important biological characteristics remain undiscovered, even among organisms that scientists have already collected.

Exploring Fungi Could Lead to New Medicines and Discoveries

The broader implications extend well beyond unusual hallucinations.

Fungi remain one of the least thoroughly explored groups of organisms, and researchers believe their chemical diversity could provide valuable clues about biology, medicine, and the nervous system.

One of the most famous examples is penicillin, the antibiotic originally obtained from a fungus that transformed the treatment of bacterial infections and became one of the most consequential medical discoveries of the last century.

Scientists hope continued exploration of fungal biodiversity could reveal other medically useful compounds and previously unknown biological processes.

The mysterious effects of L. asiatica illustrate just how much remains to be learned about the fungal kingdom. A mushroom long consumed by communities in Asia may contain a substance capable of revealing new information about the human brain and how it creates conscious experience.

The research received funding from the National Science Foundation (award #DEB-2114785).

#5778. What's The Worst Road Trip You've Ever Taken?

It might be getting colder outside, but road trips aren't over for the year yet. The busiest driving days are the days around Thanksgiving and Christmas, as people travel to visit loved ones. Some might be just heading across town. Others are driving around the state, while others are traveling across the country. And while the old adage that "it's not about the destination, it's about the journey" might be true, the journey can be crummy. What's the worst road trip you've ever taken?

Plenty of things can spoil a long drive. Snow is on the way in the next few months, and that's always something to look out for, but with hurricane season winding down, you can still get caught in a torrential downpour or an early winter storm with lots of non-frozen water. Never underestimate how dangerous precipitation can be out on the road. I learned that lesson the hard way while driving across Pennsylvania before the start of a fall semester in college. It was raining so hard that I couldn't see beyond my hood. The curtain of water made visibility so poor that I had to pull over beneath an underpass for shelter.

A big part of driving for hours at a time is staying comfortable behind the wheel. When it's scorching hot outside, a good climate control system is the only thing that can keep you cool inside a car because rolling down the windows will literally just blow hot air into your face. If an issue crops up with your car's air conditioning unit, it's never an easy fix. A road trip, especially a summer one, where your air conditioning breaks can be particularly brutal. You either have to suffer through oppressive temperatures, or stop somewhere unfamiliar to get a fix — potentially delaying your trip quite a bit.

A bad road trip could be well outside the bounds of one that's got expensive repairs or uncomfortable conditions. It could be an ultra-long trip that goes exclusively past boring, endless, flat terrain with no music to keep you entertained.

#5777. Electric Buses Need a Plan for the Next Operating Contract

Greater Manchester is entering a new stage of bus franchising. In September 2026, Go-Ahead announced that Go North West had won two Bee Network operating contracts covering 38 routes and approximately 60 buses, with service beginning in March 2027. Eighteen of those routes will operate from a new depot in Leigh. The award is a reminder that operating companies can change while the depots, charging infrastructure and grid connections supporting the passenger network remain in place for decades.

Electric-bus procurement adds a long-lived physical commitment to contracts that are often much shorter. An operating agreement may run for five to seven years. A depot building, substation or multi-megawatt electricity connection can remain useful through several such contracts. Buses can sometimes be transferred between operators, but land, cable routes, electrical capacity and installed charging equipment are tied to particular sites. Those assets need to support the passenger service even when the company responsible for driving the buses changes.

Consider an authority retendering electric-bus routes. The incumbent has used a convenient depot and financed chargers and a substantial grid connection. A competing operator submits an attractive price and a credible operating plan, but has no assured right to use the depot and electricity infrastructure. It might need to negotiate with the incumbent, find alternative premises or secure another grid connection, potentially adding years of work and substantial costs. A nominally competitive tender can become much less contestable once those obligations are included.

South Yorkshire has taken a deliberate approach to this problem. In March 2026, the South Yorkshire Mayoral Combined Authority announced the purchase of five major depots, adding them to the publicly owned Leger Way depot in Doncaster. First Bus and Stagecoach will lease the acquired premises back during the transition. The authority intends to use the estate for franchised operations and electric-bus infrastructure, with its first franchised services scheduled for September 2027. Six operators reached the next procurement stage for the initial packages at Olive Grove and Leger Way.

This does not establish that every city should own every depot. London has long maintained a publicly specified, competitively operated bus network while many garages remain outside direct Transport for London ownership. Secure leases, access rights, assignable contracts and specialist infrastructure providers may preserve the same continuity, depending on the local market. The practical test is whether a qualified successor operator can obtain the depot, buses, charging capacity and operational information required to take over on the contracted date.

Remaining asset value is central to that test. If an operator finances twenty-year charging infrastructure under a five-year contract, it needs to know what happens to the investment if another operator wins the routes. The authority must also avoid paying for a useful electrical connection and then finding it cannot make that capacity available to the next contractor. Depreciation, compensation, asset transfer, warranties, software access and grid-connection permissions need to be settled before the first contract ends, ideally before it starts.

England’s January 2026 bus-franchising guidance recognizes the importance of depots, charging infrastructure, transfer arrangements and competition in subsequent procurements. The engineering of electric buses has advanced considerably. Public authorities now need procurement structures that keep the fleet and its supporting infrastructure useful as routes, contractors and technologies evolve.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article examines the second-contract test through Greater Manchester and South Yorkshire, including asset access, residual values and operating continuity. It draws on my open report, The Bus Is Not the System, comparing metropolitan bus governance and electrification across Britain and Ireland.

Michael Barnard has 1543 posts and counting. See all posts by Michael Barnard

#5776. Here Are All The Cool Cars Elon Musk Could Have Bought Instead Of Spending $474,000,000 On Political Ads

The midterm elections are over. If you live in a swing state like I do, you've probably been inundated with political attack ads, texts, and phone calls for the last several months. This has been the most expensive election in U.S. history, and much of the blame for that can be laid on the shoulders of the richest man in the world, and one-time Jalopnik contributor, Elon Musk. After Joe Biden spurned him in 2021, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO has become the single largest political donor in history, spending hundreds of millions of dollars in support of Republican political campaigns. According to recent reports Musk spent an incredible 474 million American greenbacks nationwide, with some $200 million of that spent supporting Ken Paxton's campaign for U.S. Senate in his adopted home state of Texas.

Megadonor Musk's efforts were all in vain as every one of the political campaigns he supported in swing states failed. With economic inequality, an incredibly unpopular war, and AI data centers among the top concerns for American voters in this election, it certainly feels like these results are a referendum on Musk himself. Musk spent the weeks leading up to the election using his social media platform Twitter to call Texas Senator Elect James Talarico a "far left extremist" when he wasn't using it to promote his AI chatbot or retweet the thoughts of vile bigots.

So, after all that, it seems the world's only trillionaire decided to throw away half a billion dollars on unelectable candidates and an extremely unpopular agenda. $474 million is a small fraction of Elon's net worth, just under half a tenth of a percent, in fact. He probably won't even notice the money is gone, but the fact remains that he could have spent that money instead on something much cooler. He could have bought 6,423 brand new base model Corvettes, for example. He could have given each of the 500 people living in SpaceX company town Starbase, Texas, 12 Corvettes as a gift.

What if he had grander ambitions than Corvettes? Let's look into it.

Back in August, if Musk had found himself at Monterey Car Week instead of pumping money into his super pacs, he could have really cleaned house and built himself a really cool collection of some of the most important cars in history. As the CEO of the world's most valuable car company, Musk must have at least some interest in cars, right? He did own a McLaren F1 at one point, for example, so maybe there is still a car guy hiding in there. Auction house RM Sotheby's recently set a record for collector car auctions in Monterey, with more money changing hands that weekend than ever had in history. The wealthy car collectors are still flush with cash and willing to spend, it seems. The sale was led by the controversial $40 million Ferrari Luce charity auction, a rare $34.655 million McLaren F1 GTR sale, and an $18.7 million 1963 Corvette Grand Sport. In total the auction pushed 199 lots across the dais and nearly half of them were million-plus sales. What was that record breaking number? $380 million, meaning Musk could have used his political contribution slush fund to buy every single one of them and still have money left over for transportation, taxes, and registration.

If Musk really wants to be as popular as it seems he is desperate to be, he could have spent the summer driving around in his new Aston Martin Valkyrie Spider, Talbot-Lago T26 Record Cabriolet, Mercer Raceabout, or Lamborghini Miura P400. The point is, he could have bought whatever he wanted, but instead of something cool or useful, what he wanted was Ken Paxton.

What if he wanted to own the most expensive collector cars of all time? For just the money he spent in Texas he could easily have bought the one-of-two Mercedes-Benz 300 SLR "Uhlenhaut Coupe" which sold in 2022 for $142,000,000. In fact, for his 2026 political campaign contributions, Musk could have also purchased the Mercedes-Benz W 196 R Stromlinienwagen ($52,960,000), a Ferrari 330 LM/250 GTO ($51,705,000), another 250 GTO ($48,405,000), a Shelby Cobra Daytona Coupe ($42,905,000), the aforementioned Ferrari Luce, and yet two more Ferrari 250 GTOs ($38,500,000 and $38,115,000 respectively). That's the top eight most expensive cars ever sold in history. Or, you know, one feckless midterm election media buy blitz. Which would you rather have?

Let's say Elon woke up one day and felt like he wanted to do something magnanimous in an effort to repair his reputation with the American people. What could he have done with his $474,000,000 political contributions that would have instead helped the average American feel better about their country and place in it? Auto loan debt is pretty bad right now, reaching some $1.7 trillion. Obviously he couldn't clear the table on every car loan, and it would be absurd to expect him to. That said, Texas has been hit harder by auto loan debt than any other state with around 10% of borrowers in the state more than 30 days behind on their payments. How much goodwill could he garner for himself and Tesla if he were to forgive all Tesla loans in the state? That might be a good start.

Half a billion dollars buys a whole lot of goodwill for a man. Maybe he could start subsidizing grocery stores in poor Texas neighborhoods. Or pushing to fund the state's unreliable and undermaintained power grid. He could set up a fund to install EV chargers in apartment complexes or on the street, further growing Tesla's potential customer base in the state. Man, there are just so many better uses of that money than attack ads claiming James Talarico isn't manly enough to represent Texas.

My biggest takeaway from this election cycle is that America's wealthiest donor is spending his tremendous wealth trying to buy politicians. Thankfully, this time anyway, it seems that the voters have rebuked that effort. It certainly should be one of the priorities of this new crop of elected representatives to remove the ability for one man to wield this much potential political power, regardless of whether it was successful or not. It seems Americans are desperate for some kind of financial relief, whether that's reducing gas prices by ending the war in Iran, combating inflation, or making healthcare affordable, rather than fighting ridiculous culture wars. While those should be major priorities for the Democrats, campaign finance reform should probably be on the docket somewhere.

Elon seems to have made himself incredibly unpopular across the last half decade simply by aligning himself with MAGA Republicans. He's going to have a long uphill battle to revamp his image. Maybe he should just disappear from public life for a decade or so. Or buy a few hundred vintage collector cars at auction and simply give them to random American car enthusiasts. Either way, he should probably stop tweeting.

#5775. Low-dose aspirin linked to 70% lower dementia risk, but there's a catch

A new analysis from Monash University suggests that low-dose aspirin may be linked to a dramatically lower risk of dementia in older adults with certain genetic characteristics. Researchers found that people with a specific genetic profile who took aspirin had a 70 percent lower relative risk of developing dementia, raising the possibility that genetic testing could eventually help identify people who might benefit from preventive treatments.

The findings, published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association, were based on genetic data from more than 13,500 participants in the landmark ASPREE Trial (Aspirin in Reducing Events in the Elderly). Scientists examined whether differences in participants' genes could help explain why aspirin might affect dementia risk differently from one person to another.

Aspirin Linked to 70% Lower Dementia Risk in Certain People

The most striking results involved genes associated with platelet count. Platelets are small components of the blood that help form clots, and researchers wanted to determine whether genetic variations related to platelet levels could influence aspirin's potential effects on dementia.

After assigning participants genetic scores associated with platelet count, the team compared dementia rates among those who received aspirin and those given a placebo.

Among people in the highest 20 percent of platelet count genetic scores, just 1 percent of aspirin users developed dementia, compared with 3.3 percent of participants taking a placebo. That difference translates to approximately a 70 percent lower relative risk of dementia among those receiving aspirin.

However, the apparent benefit was accompanied by a significant safety concern. In the same genetic group, 4.4 percent of aspirin users experienced major bleeding, compared with 2.1 percent of those receiving a placebo.

A Genetic Clue Could Change Dementia Prevention

Lead author Dr. Peter Fransquet, a Research Fellow at Monash's School of Public Health and Preventive Medicine, said the results suggest that a person's genetic makeup could eventually play an important role in determining which dementia prevention strategies are most appropriate.

"Previous trials found aspirin didn't prevent dementia when everyone was considered together," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Our findings suggest there may be more to the story.

"In people with this particular genetic profile, we saw substantially fewer cases of dementia among those taking aspirin.

"It raises the possibility that genetics could one day help us identify who may benefit from a preventive treatment, rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach."

The possibility of more targeted prevention is especially important as dementia becomes increasingly common. Dementia Australia estimates that approximately 446,500 people in Australia are living with the condition in 2026. That number is expected to exceed one million by 2065.

Blood Platelets Reveal an Unexpected Dementia Connection

Previous research has connected dementia with platelet activation and aggregation, the processes through which platelets become active and stick together. However, scientists have had a less complete understanding of how a person's overall platelet count might relate to dementia.

The new findings point toward platelet biology as a potentially important area of investigation. Notably, the genetic association identified by researchers did not involve the well-established genetic risk factors typically linked to Alzheimer's disease and other forms of dementia.

"What's particularly interesting is that the signal wasn't linked to the established genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's disease and dementia," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Instead, it's pointing us towards platelet biology and gives us a new avenue to investigate."

Why More Research Is Needed Before Taking Aspirin

Although the findings suggest that aspirin could have different effects depending on a person's genes, researchers emphasize that the results must be confirmed before they can guide medical recommendations.

The next step is to investigate whether the association appears in other populations. Scientists would then need to conduct a clinical trial specifically involving people with the genetic profile identified in the analysis.

"We now need to confirm the finding in other studies and ultimately test it in a trial designed specifically for people with this genetic profile.

"People shouldn't start taking aspirin to prevent dementia on the basis of this study without consulting with their doctor, particularly given the increased risk of serious bleeding.

"If it holds up, the fact that aspirin is already cheap and widely available could make personalized dementia prevention a real possibility."
