#5774. Recurrent IgA Nephropathy Tied to Allograft Failure After Transplant

— Rising proteinuria in years before recurrence diagnosis pointed to high-risk allograft loss

As one of the most common autoimmune kidney diseases worldwide, immunoglobulin A (IgA) nephropathy is diagnosed in an estimated 2.5 per 100,000 adults each year.

Roughly 20% to 40% of individuals with IgA nephropathy will develop end-stage kidney disease within 20 years of diagnosis and require dialysis or a kidney transplant. But even after a transplant, disease can recur, threatening allograft survival.

To better understand post-transplant disease trajectory, researchers analyzed data from the U.K. National Registry of Rare Kidney Diseases (RaDaR).

"In an evolving therapeutic landscape, detailed characterization of recurrent IgA and its impact on graft outcomes is needed," noted a research team led by Sherry Masoud, MBChB, of University College London.

The team evaluated long-term outcomes among 1,416 first-time kidney transplant recipients with biopsy-proven native IgA nephropathy treated across 37 centers between 2000 and 2025. Over a median follow-up of 9 years, recurrent IgA nephropathy was diagnosed in 244 recipients, corresponding to a cumulative recurrence incidence of 24% and a median time to recurrence of 4.8 years.

Patients with recurrent disease experienced significantly shortened graft longevity, with median death-censored graft survival dropping to just over 4 years following recurrence.

"Recurrent IgA nephropathy was consistently associated with a 2.5- to 3.5-fold higher risk of graft failure," said co-author Vivienne Ralph, PhD, of King's College London, at the American Society of Nephrology Kidney Week annual meeting.

The magnitude of the association between recurrent disease and graft loss suggests an important unmet need in this population, the researchers underscored.

At baseline, the transplant cohort had a median age of 45 years, 84% were white, 27% were female, and 46% received a living-donor allograft.

The risk of recurrence appeared closely tied to how aggressive the recipient's primary disease was. Prior to transplant, patients who would go on to develop recurrence experienced a significantly steeper loss in native-kidney estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) than those whose disease did not recur (slope difference -4.15 mL/min/1.73 m² per year, 95% CI -0.98 to -7.31, P for interaction=0.011).

This finding suggests "that something about their disease phenotype or the aggressiveness of it perhaps predisposes them to subsequently developing recurrent disease," said Masoud.

In multivariable landmark modeling at 1-year post-transplant, older age at kidney failure appeared to be protective against recurrence (adjusted subdistribution HR [sHR] 0.74 per 10-year increase, 95% CI 0.65-0.84), whereas recipients under age 30 exhibited the highest cumulative incidence.

"The older you were, the less likely you were to experience recurrence, and similarly, those who were on prednisolone therapy at 1 year were also much less likely to experience recurrence," Masoud explained.

Maintenance prednisolone therapy at 1 year was associated with a 40% reduction in recurrence risk (adjusted sHR 0.60, 95% CI 0.45-0.81).

Native biopsy MEST-C scores were not associated with recurrence risk.

Longitudinal models revealed that urine protein-creatinine ratio (UPCR) trajectories began separating years ahead of clinical diagnosis.

"Proteinuria trajectories are rising many years before we're actually making the [biopsy] diagnosis," Masoud highlighted. "As you might expect, it's continued to rise from the time of diagnosis." This pre-diagnostic escalation suggests a critical "missed opportunity" for earlier therapeutic intervention, she noted.

In 1-year post-transplant landmark analyses, patients whose disease eventually recurred began with higher proteinuria levels that rose steadily over time, while non-recurrent recipients maintained low, stable levels. By year 6, predicted UPCR reached 0.89 g/g in patients who developed recurrence compared with 0.17 g/g in non-recurrent controls. By year 11, predicted UPCR escalated to 4.56 g/g versus 0.25 g/g, respectively.

Notably, a doubling of UPCR prior to recurrence diagnosis was linked to a 2.2-fold increase in the risk of graft failure.

Even modest proteinuria at the time of recurrence signaled poor long-term outcomes. Patients with a UPCR of 0.5 to 1.0 g/g at recurrence faced an estimated 50% cumulative incidence of graft failure at 5 years. This jumped to 70% among those with UPCR over 1.0 g/g.

On allograft biopsy, tubular atrophy and interstitial fibrosis (T-lesions) independently predicted graft failure (sHR 1.56, 95% CI 1.05–2.31, P=0.027). Additionally, higher UPCR at recurrence was associated with increased risk (sHR 1.29 per unit increase, 95% CI 1.17–1.42, P<0.0001), whereas higher eGFR at recurrence was protective (sHR 0.94 per unit increase, 95% CI 0.913–0.964, P<0.0001).

As a recent Sierra Club report makes clear, the ACC wields a tremendous amount of power over the future of Arizona’s energy landscape, public health, and utility bill increases, especially as the state experiences a fossil gas rush to power data centers. Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country and with Arizona’s largest utility, APS, opting to run the dirty Four Corners coal plant for more than another decade. Yet, up until now, the Commission has made a habit of increasing ratepayer bills, opting for expensive fossil fuels and cutting programs that require utilities to utilize solar energy to generate electricity and to conserve energy, lower electric bills, and invest in energy efficiency.

“These election results should act as a wakeup call to the rest of the Arizona Corporation Commission. Arizonans are tired of being put on the backburner, bearing the burden for expensive fossil fuels that harm our neighbors and contribute to climate change. We share in the optimism of our fellow voters and will continue to carefully monitor the ACC as our work to promote clean air, clean water, and healthy communities and a healthy climate enters a new chapter.”

In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5771. Zocdoc opens infrastructure to AI agents, launches partnership with Instinct

Zocdoc opens infrastructure to AI agents, launches partnership with Instinct" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

As more patients turn to artificial intelligence agents to manage their healthcare, Zocdoc is opening its healthcare access infrastructure to streamline the process.

Beginning Oct. 28, agents can connect patients to care through a variety of channels—including MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, deep links and more.

Zocdoc Chief Strategy Officer Alex Doyne told Fierce Healthcare that AI agents are “becoming a new starting point for patients seeking healthcare.”

Doyne said traffic from AI agents on the platform has “at least doubled roughly every three weeks since July.”

“AI agents can help patients navigate their healthcare options and take action on their behalf, but they're only as reliable as the information and systems they can access,” Doyne said. “By connecting agents to Zocdoc's infrastructure, we can ensure they are booking the right appointments for patients.”

Zocdoc has over 175 scheduling integrations, with more than 10,000 insurance plans and 200,000 verified providers in its network, according to Doyne.

“Opening that infrastructure to agents means they now have access to a reliable booking experience,” Doyne said.

Doyne added that healthcare is “particularly challenging,” for AI agents since “so much of the information available online is fragmented, outdated, or inaccurate,” which can have “real consequences for patients who may already be struggling to access care.”

“That's why it's critical that agents have access to reliable provider and insurance information, accurate availability and infrastructure that ensures the booking is integrated with a provider’s calendar and will hold on the day,” Doyne said.

“We've established a two-way promise that sets clear expectations for Zocdoc and the agents connecting to our infrastructure,” Doyne said. “Zocdoc commits to welcoming agents that register and identify themselves, while agents are expected to obtain explicit patient consent before booking appointments and protect patient and provider data.”

The company also announced a partnership with AI personal assistant Instinct to let agents use Zocdoc’s healthcare access infrastructure to find vetted providers, verify insurance benefits and book appointments.

“Instinct is built to genuinely improve your life and handle important everyday tasks,” said Ben Schwerin, Instinct chief business officer, in a statement. “Since healthcare is more complicated than ordering groceries or booking travel, the stakes are higher. By partnering with Zocdoc, Instinct can navigate that complexity—so getting an appointment can be as simple as sending a text.”

Instinct will join Zocdoc’s growing Care Access Network, which also includes Google Gemini, Amazon Health AI, Yelp, Healthgrades and more.

Last month, Zocdoc acquired Sohar Health in its push to evolve beyond an appointment-booking marketplace into broader healthcare access infrastructure.

Sohar Health helps healthcare organizations simplify insurance workflows, and its APIs automate insurance discovery, eligibility and benefits verification, network status checks and cost estimates.

It marked the company's first acquisition in its nearly 20-year history, executives said.

#5770. Scientists reveal the hidden danger of missing blood pressure medication

Missing doses of blood pressure medication could significantly reduce its ability to protect against heart attacks, strokes, and other serious cardiovascular problems, according to findings presented at ESC Congress 2026.

An estimated 1.4 billion adults worldwide between the ages of 30 and 79 have high blood pressure (hypertension), a major contributor to illness and death. Although medications that lower blood pressure are proven to reduce the risk of cardiovascular complications, approximately half of patients do not take their prescriptions as directed.

"We have evidence that poor adherence to antihypertensive medication is associated with worse outcomes," explained presenter, Miss Qianqian Yang from the University of Oxford, UK. "However, previous data has come from observational studies and could be confounded by other factors. We undertook an analysis of randomized trial data to assess the true impact of adherence on the benefits of antihypertensive treatment."

Major Study Examines the Impact of Missed Blood Pressure Medication

To better understand how consistently taking medication affects its benefits, researchers analyzed individual patient data from 91,339 participants across nine clinical trials. Each trial compared a blood pressure treatment (intervention group) with an alternative approach (comparator group: either placebo or a less-intensive regimen).

The researchers divided participants into two categories based on how closely they followed their prescribed treatment. Those who took at least 80% of their assigned medication were considered to have higher adherence, while those who took less than 80% were placed in the lower adherence group.

Even with the regular monitoring and structured conditions of clinical trials, patients became less consistent about taking their medication as time passed.

"A decline in adherence over time was apparent, even in the structured setting of trials," noted Miss Yang. Overall, 88% of participants met the higher adherence threshold during the first year, but that figure had dropped to just 79% by the fifth year.

Skipping Blood Pressure Pills Reduces Treatment Benefits

The difference in medication adherence was reflected in how much patients' blood pressure improved.

Among participants with higher adherence, systolic blood pressure, the top number in a blood pressure reading, was 5.2 mmHg lower in the intervention group than in the comparator group. For those with lower adherence, the difference was considerably smaller at just 3.0 mmHg.

Researchers then examined whether these differences translated into protection against major cardiovascular problems. The outcomes included strokes, heart attacks (myocardial infarction), ischemic heart disease, and heart failure that resulted in hospitalization or death.

The findings revealed a striking contrast. Among patients who consistently followed their treatment plans, the intervention group experienced an 11% lower incidence of major cardiovascular events compared with the comparator group. However, among patients with lower adherence, there was no statistically significant reduction in these events.

"Notably, our results reinforce the saying that 'drugs don't work in patients who don't take them.' Patients with lower adherence derived little or no cardiovascular benefit, whereas those with higher adherence had greater reductions in blood pressure and prevention of cardiovascular events," said Miss Yang.

Helping Patients Get Lifesaving Heart Protection

The researchers believe that helping people take their medication consistently should become a greater priority in the treatment of high blood pressure. They recommend more routine assessments of medication adherence, along with better patient education and practical strategies to make treatment easier to follow.

Possible approaches include simplifying medication schedules, combining multiple treatments into a single pill, and prescribing longer-acting drugs that can help limit the consequences of an occasional missed dose.

Professor Felix Mahfoud, Chair of the ESC Communication Committee, emphasized that missed medication has received too little attention as a contributor to poorly controlled blood pressure.

"For years, we have neglected non-adherence to medication as a cause for uncontrolled hypertension. Assessment of adherence should become a routine part of hypertension care so patients do not miss out on life-saving benefits. As healthcare providers, we are here to have open, non-judgmental conversations with our patients about any barriers they may have to taking medication, to help improve adherence."

Materials provided by European Society of Cardiology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#5768. FTC issues warning letters over deceptive marketing as ACA enrollment opens

FTC issues warning letters over deceptive marketing as ACA enrollment opens" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Open enrollment season is getting underway, and the Federal Trade Commission sent warning letters to 25 companies that conduct marketing or generate sales leads for insurers.

The commission said in an announcement that the letters aim to put these companies on notice about deceptive or unfair marketing tactics that are likely unlawful. Activities in question include misrepresenting the benefits or limitations of a plan, or portraying a plan as comprehensive when it isn't.

In addition, FTC also warned against misrepresenting any costs associated with a plan or falsely telling consumers that a plan offers free rewards, cash offers or other incentives.

"Making healthcare more transparent and affordable is a top priority for the FTC," said Christopher Mufarrige, Director of the FTC’s Bureau of Consumer Protection, in the announcement.

"Americans experiencing rising healthcare costs should not also have to worry about whether a healthcare plan or program is what it claims to be," Mufarrige continued. "The FTC will remain focused on monitoring this market and will not hesitate to hold accountable companies that unlawfully market or advertise healthcare plans to consumers."

The FTC letters were largely sent to firms that market plans on the Affordable Care Act's exchanges, where open enrollment opened on Sunday, Nov. 1. Medicare is also in its annual enrollment period, which continues through Dec. 7, and late fall is when most employers begin plan selections for workers.

The agency does not allege wrongdoing in the warning letters, but instead suggests that the companies "conduct a thorough review of their advertisements to ensure they are complying with applicable laws and rules."

The letters include examples of previous enforcement actions against marketers in the ACA space, such as Innovative Partners and Top Healthcare Options Insurance Agency.

In the letters, the agency emphasizes that its staff will be closely watching for potential violations.

"FTC staff will continue to actively monitor this market for potentially deceptive or unfair acts or practices and will take enforcement action as warranted," the FTC wrote in the letter (PDF).

#5767. Low-Grade Proteinuria Linked to Poor Outcomes in IgA Nephropathy

— Study findings align with updated clinical practice guidance

In immunoglobulin A (IgA) nephropathy, proteinuria levels below 1.0 g/day have long been viewed as relatively low risk for kidney disease progression.

Recent studies have challenged this assumption, however, with data suggesting that even low-grade proteinuria -- defined as 0.5 to 1.0 g/day -- carries significant clinical implications.

Such data prompted the 2025 update to Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) guidelines, which lowered the target proteinuria threshold from less than 1.0 g/day to less than 0.5 g/day.

A recent systematic review and meta-analysis published in the Clinical Journal of the American Society of Nephrology reinforced that shift, linking low-grade proteinuria to a significantly increased risk of adverse kidney outcomes.

"Low-grade proteinuria should not be dismissed as reassuring in IgA nephropathy," study author Takaya Sasaki, MD, PhD, of the Jikei University School of Medicine in Tokyo, told MedPage Today.

"Even when proteinuria is below 1 g/day, persistent levels of 0.5 g/day or higher are associated with an increased risk of disease progression," he said.

IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis globally. Up to 50% of patients ultimately progress to kidney failure within 10 to 20 years.

The 2025 KDIGO guideline update "represents a more stringent target than in previous guidelines and underscores the recognition of proteinuria reduction as a surrogate marker of improved kidney outcomes," the researchers noted. However, current recommendations were based primarily on individual observational studies and narrative reviews.

"No prior systematic review and meta-analysis has specifically focused on the risk for kidney outcomes associated with low-grade proteinuria in IgA nephropathy," they wrote.

To fill that gap, the team synthesized 23 studies published through July 2025 encompassing 15,289 patients across Asia, Europe, and North America, with follow-up periods ranging from 3.1 to more than 9 years.

Across nine studies evaluating baseline proteinuria, low-grade proteinuria (0.5 to 1.0 g/day) was associated with a 73% higher risk of adverse kidney outcomes -- defined as a doubling of serum creatinine, a ≥30% to 50% decline in estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), kidney failure, or kidney replacement therapy -- compared with proteinuria below 0.5 g/day (pooled HR 1.73, 95% CI 1.36-2.20).

In seven studies evaluating time-averaged proteinuria (TAP), low-grade levels were associated with nearly triple the risk of adverse kidney outcomes (pooled HR 2.87, 95% CI 1.48-5.56). Low-grade TAP was also associated with a significantly faster annual loss of kidney function across four studies, with a mean difference in eGFR slope of -1.02 mL/min/1.73 m² per year (95% CI -1.60 to -0.45).

"These findings strengthen the evidence supporting the KDIGO treatment target of proteinuria below 0.5 g/day," said Sasaki.

Dose-response analyses showed that each incremental category increase in baseline proteinuria was linked with a 1.88-fold increased risk of adverse outcomes, and each category increase in TAP with a 3.06-fold increased risk. These associations remained robust across geographic regions and sensitivity analyses.

"These findings shed light on the importance of achieving a reduction in proteinuria and sustaining suppression of proteinuria throughout the disease course," the researchers highlighted, adding that "therapeutic interventions aimed at maintaining lower proteinuria levels long-term are critical for modifying disease trajectory."

In an accompanying editorial, Erin Vaughan, MBBS, of the University of Sydney, and Vlado Perkovic, MBBS, PhD, of the University of New South Wales in Sydney, emphasized that the findings carry "meaningful prognostic significance."

"Individuals with low-grade proteinuria have traditionally been considered low risk of progression in IgA nephropathy, a view that has shaped both clinical decision making and trial design," Vaughan and Perkovic wrote. "The present findings add important granularity to available evidence by demonstrating a graded, continuous association between proteinuria burden and subsequent kidney risk, challenging the assumption that proteinuria goals below conventional thresholds of 1 g/day maintain long-term kidney protection."

Over follow-up periods ranging from 46 to 84 months, 18% of patients with baseline low-grade proteinuria and 12% with time-averaged low-grade proteinuria experienced adverse kidney outcomes.

"When considered across the life course of IgA nephropathy, these event rates translate into a substantial cumulative burden of clinically meaningful kidney function decline that may now be potentially modifiable," Vaughan and Perkovic observed.

"Although our findings support a proteinuria target below 0.5 g/day, they are based on observational studies and do not directly establish that treatment to achieve this target improves kidney outcomes," Sasaki underscored. "An important next step is to determine which treatments, and at what stage of disease, provide long-term kidney benefits for patients with low-grade proteinuria."

A major barrier remains trial eligibility. Recent clinical trials have almost exclusively enrolled patients with proteinuria greater than 1.0 g/day. Because guideline indications and regulatory approvals frequently track trial inclusion criteria, patients with proteinuria between 0.5 and 1.0 g/day may be excluded from newer disease-modifying therapies.

Both the study authors and editorialists stressed that future interventional trials must recruit patients with low-grade proteinuria to confirm whether therapeutic suppression below 0.5 g/day alters their long-term clinical trajectory.

Vaughan and Perkovic also pointed out that more data are still needed on whether very low-grade proteinuria (0.3 to 0.5 g/day) confers residual progressive risk.

#5766. Hyundai Motor Group Outlines Future Mobility Technology Roadmap at FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026

#5765. Just two drinks a day may be far riskier than you think

Even alcohol consumption that many Americans would describe as moderate may carry significant health risks, including a greater likelihood of premature death, disability, cancer, and heart disease. That is the conclusion of a new study published in the Journal of Studies on Alcohol and Drugs, a journal published at Rutgers University.

The research, known as the Alcohol Intake and Health Study, was originally commissioned by the U.S. federal government to help shape updated national dietary recommendations. Researchers estimated that people who consume an average of 14 alcoholic drinks per week face a 1 in 25 lifetime risk of dying from alcohol-related causes. By comparison, consuming up to seven drinks weekly was linked to only small increases in risk for most health conditions.

"Even low levels of alcohol use come with health risks," says lead study author Kevin Shield, Ph.D., an associate professor at the University of Toronto and a senior scientist who leads the World Health Organization (WHO)/Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) Collaborating Centre in Addiction and Mental Health. "And that risk continues to increase the more someone drinks."

How Scientists Measured the Health Risks of Alcohol

To better understand the long-term consequences of drinking, Shield and his colleagues in the United States and Canada investigated how alcohol consumption over a lifetime contributes to disease and premature death among Americans.

Medical experts first examined more than 7,200 scientific publications covering illnesses and injuries associated with alcohol. Using this evidence, they estimated the risks for individual health conditions and combined those findings with large national health data sets. Statistical models then allowed the team to calculate how different levels of alcohol consumption affect the likelihood of developing serious health problems or dying prematurely.

The findings provide specific estimates that are missing from the latest U.S. Dietary Guidelines. The updated recommendations simply encourage Americans to "limit alcoholic beverages" without identifying a particular consumption threshold. Earlier versions advised men to drink no more than two alcoholic beverages per day and women no more than one.

"While the new U.S. Dietary Guidelines contain a useful 'less-is-best' message, they provide no quantitative framework. Our study was designed to do just that across the drinking spectrum," says study co-author Timothy Naimi, M.D., M.P.H., director of the University of Victoria's Canadian Institute for Substance Use Research and an adjunct professor at Boston University.

"It turns out that two drinks per day, which might be considered 'moderate' from a social standpoint, is associated with a substantially elevated risk of a premature death caused by alcohol," explains Naimi.

Moderate Drinking Offers No Significant Overall Health Benefits

The researchers looked beyond the risk of premature death to examine alcohol's connection to a wide range of immediate and long-term health problems. These included cancers (e.g., esophageal, oral, and breast), cardiovascular disease, liver disease, and injuries.

One of the study's most important findings challenges the longstanding belief that consuming small amounts of alcohol can improve health, particularly heart health.

"We did not observe a significant protective effect of alcohol on health at any level of consumption," says Shield. "At low levels, alcohol may be associated with a reduced risk of ischemic heart disease and stroke. But when you look across the full range of health outcomes, including cancer and other chronic diseases, those potential benefits are outweighed by the risks even at 7 drinks per week."

In other words, while some studies have suggested that light drinking may benefit certain aspects of cardiovascular health, those possible advantages do not translate into an overall health benefit once alcohol's links to cancer and other diseases are taken into account.

Although the analysis drew on extensive scientific evidence, the researchers emphasize that its estimates describe risks across populations rather than predicting precisely what will happen to any particular person.

Shield says the statistical calculations relied on "the best possible data." However, he cautions that individual circumstances can substantially influence the results.

"However, we can't assume that means one person's individual health risk is the same as what is reported here -- that depends on other factors like lifestyle, genetics, drinking patterns, and other choices that differ person to person."

To estimate alcohol's overall impact, the researchers calculated risks for health conditions already known to be caused by alcohol and combined them into a broader assessment. However, scientists are continuing to investigate additional possible consequences of drinking, including emerging evidence connecting alcohol consumption to pancreatic cancer.

"Understanding those relationships, and how much alcohol contributes to those risks, is an area that still needs further work," says Shield.

What the Findings Mean for U.S. Drinking Guidelines

The results suggest that alcohol-related health risks become more pronounced when consumption exceeds one drink per day, regardless of whether the person is male or female. This provides a more specific reference point for people trying to understand how their drinking habits might affect their long-term health.

Rather than suggesting that any particular amount is completely safe, the findings show how risks increase with greater consumption and why drinking less generally reduces exposure to alcohol-related harm.

"Having a clearer threshold helps people better understand what level of drinking is associated with increased risk and make more informed decisions when drinking," says Shield.

The study has also raised questions about how scientific evidence was incorporated into federal dietary recommendations.

In an accompanying editorial, Robert M. Vincent, a former Associate Administrator for the U.S. Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration, offers his perspective on the circumstances surrounding the research and its role in the development of national guidance.

"The Alcohol Intake and Health report was explicitly invited to inform alcohol guidance during development of the Dietary Guidelines for Americans, 2025-2030," he writes. "Despite the study's adherence to its mandate, its findings were sidelined."

Materials provided by Journal of Studies on Alcohol and Drugs. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#5764. 1968 Ford Mustang For Sale Is A Clean, Green V8 Cruiser Begging To Be Driven Daily

When you hear "green 1968 Ford Mustang," you likely picture one very specific car. You know the one: a verdant, tire-killing fastback driven by mercurial automotive and Hollywood legend Steve McQueen in the 1968 film "Bullitt." Today, a first-generation Ford Mustang Fastback similar to the one from the 58-year-old action-crime thriller could demand six figures, especially in that oh-so coveted color: Highland Green. For first-gen Mustang fans who can't afford to spend fixer-upper-home money on a car, there are alternatives. Alternatives like this green 1968 Ford Mustang.

At a glance and on paper, it ticks boxes. A V8 under the hood? Of course. That classic facelifted first-generation Mustang look? Thankfully, it's intact. Mostly. See, someone thought this one was deserving of a cool restomod treatment, and it got just that. Instead of being a numbers-matching carbureted survivor, this one benefits from a myriad of modern and aftermarket updates, like electronic fuel injection, electric fans, power locks, keyless entry, and Vintage Air air-conditioning and heat. This is a daily-driver-ready take on a classic. It's up for grabs, too. As of this writing, the restomod-ed Mustang is for sale just north of Dallas. Purists need not apply.

No, it's not finished in Highland Green. Not even close, really. The 1968 Ford Mustang restomod currently for sale by PC Classic Cars in Sherman, Texas is a much lighter hue. The headlights have been replaced with an integrated LED lamp-and-signal set and the tail lights are sequential like an aftermarket set you'd find on a much newer Mustang. It also rides on a set of staggered two-tone Halibrand-style alloy wheels that complement the paintwork, wrapped up in Nitto rubber. The builder also swapped in a Mustang II front end, complete with a sway bar and tubular control arms.

Under the hood, the '68 Mustang gets a familiar engine option: a 289-cubic-inch V8. It's far from stock, though. The parts list continues with a set of aluminum AFR cylinder heads, a Holley Sniper EFI setup, a Fi-Tech electronic ignition, an aftermarket serpentine system, an aluminum radiator in place of the tired old stock unit, and dual electric fans, to name a few things. The whole thing hooks up to a four-speed automatic gearbox with overdrive. Sorry, no manual here.

There are more modern touches beyond the engine bay, including power locks with keyless entry via an aftermarket fob, a stereo system with an amplifier and subwoofers, and that Vintage Air climate control. The restomod treatment continues inside the classic Mustang's cabin. It's a full brown leather TMI interior with black accents on the folding bucket seats and dash paneling. The brown leather even extends into the trunk.

It's not perfect. A glance at the driver's side door reveals a little misalignment. The car was once red. There are blemishes here and there. In short, this is more of a driver, less of a concours condition car meant to be kept out of the rain or prolonged direct sunlight. That doesn't mean the modified pony car loses the aesthetic charm that landed the 1968 model on the list of your favorite Ford Mustangs. Well, the fastback did, at least.

So just how much does PC Classic Cars want for this one? It's currently on sale for $46,950. That's quite a bit steeper than the average $25,310 price of 1968 Ford Mustang coupé sales over the last year. Then again, not every '68 Mustang on the market has had this much work done.

Get this: Its clock shows just 119 miles. Though, as you might expect, that's not exactly accurate. It's likely that this build's underpinnings have a much higher mileage. Frankly, a 1968 Ford Mustang with a 289-cubic-inch V8 and an true odometer reading anywhere near 119 would demand a much, much higher price. Still, with the modern tweaks already on this 58-year-old 'Stang, it seriously needs a driver to take it on as a daily and run that odometer up. After all, what would McQueen do? He'd almost certainly drive it — and so should the next owner.

#5763. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology! Part 1

Your car is getting “long in tooth,” or maybe your daughter is going away to college and you are giving her your car. Whatever the reason, you are looking for another car. It could be new, it could be used: don’t even look at gas cars, electric cars are superior technology!

The wheels on every railroad locomotive in the world are driven by electric motors. All the busiest rail routes in Europe and China are pure electric, including high-speed rail. 98% of new cars in Norway are electric, over 80% of new cars in Denmark are electric. In China, the country with the largest sales of new cars in the world, the percentage of new electric cars in September 2026 was 67%. 82% of the operating bus fleet in China is electric. Even ships and airplanes are going electric. The data is unequivocal: electric propulsion is superior to internal combustion propulsion. Only the huge political contributions to Trump by the fossil fuel industry and his resulting anti-EV policies have slowed down adoption in this country.

In places we’ve been like California; Portland, Oregon; and northern Utah, you are seeing a lot of Teslas, so you know that electric cars are working for a lot of people these days. It’s time for you to do some research on electric cars.

You do pay a premium for new electric cars, because the batteries are still quite expensive. If price is the driving factor for you, buy a used electric car. Hardly any used EVs are high mileage, and EV batteries still have plenty of life even after they go off warranty at 8 years and 120,000 miles. There are thousands of lightly used EVs sitting at used car lots at reasonable prices all over the country these days. Some used car dealers will give you an estimate of the battery life. If not, get a friend who is an experienced EV driver to help you evaluate the battery life of the EV you are considering.

If you just do local driving or have two cars and have a garage or off-street parking, an EV is a no brainer. EVs are hands-down more convenient than gas cars for local driving. Your EV will have plenty of range for local driving, it will get a complete charge overnight in your home garage refueling station and it will never need an oil change.

EVs are as quiet as mice, as smooth as kittens, and as quick as jackrabbits. They have simple electric motors, not complex internal combustion engines. They have simple one-speed transmissions, not complex 7-speed automatic transmissions. They don’t have exhaust systems with mufflers that will rust and need replacement. They don’t have emission control systems with limited life. They don’t have complex cooling systems with big radiators. You seldom use your brakes with an EV, so your brakes may last the life of the car. They don’t have a big lead-acid battery for starting, with a maximum life of 5 years. New EVs have only a small lithium-ion battery, which lasts 10+ years. Bottom line: Routine maintenance for EVs is almost nil. I think the only real maintenance item on a Tesla over the first 3 years is the cabin air filter. You can expect an EV to go 500,000 miles without major repair or replacement in most cases.

Perhaps you can stomach buying a car from Tesla whose CEO has become one of the most despicable persons on the planet lately. (Example: Musk recently suspended access to his Starlink communications system to the Ukrainians who are valiantly defending their country from the Russian aggressors.) However, Tesla has sold almost 10 million automobiles worldwide that don’t emit CO2 and particulate pollution while operating. Tesla also has by far the most ubiquitous charging system, which allows cross-country driving on major US roads with little planning. Consider buying a Tesla.

So, what about cross-country trips? Everyone knows that long-distance trips in an electric car are inconvenient or even impossible, because there aren’t enough charging stations and it takes an hour if you can find one. Wrong: Every 100 miles along all the major Interstate highways, Tesla has built Superchargers with 8 to 32 and a few with over 100 charging stalls. By the time you have gone to the bathroom, walked the dog, and chatted with a fellow EV owner for a few minutes, you probably have more than enough charge to continue your journey. Usually, charging takes about 20 minutes. If you need a longer charge, you may need to watch a bit of Octopus Teacher or Back to the Future II on the big screen in the car for another 10 minutes.

News flash! Since February 28, 2023, most Tesla Superchargers (now North American Charging Standard/NACS chargers) are usable by all EV brands. Unfortunately, older Tesla Superchargers can’t be used by other brands, so I would still advise buying a Tesla if you want carefree cross-country driving. CCS chargers which are native to most other brands have sprung up by the thousands in the US, which together with the newer Tesla Superchargers make it possible to travel cross-country with non-Teslas on many routes with some planning. Also, Teslas can use non-Tesla chargers. Non-Tesla chargers often come with Tesla cables now, so Tesla owners don’t even need an adapter in most cases. However, even Teslas require careful planning if you are driving far from major highways.

My wife and I have done cross-country trips from Utah to Wisconsin for 7 years, on to North Carolina (once), and back to Utah. We have also traveled from Utah to Southern California at the same 500 miles per day rate that we used to do with our gas cars … and with no worries, mate.

I’m going to try to pass on some things I’ve learned from driving five different electric vehicles over 12 years and some 200,000 miles. See part two, coming soon.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Arthur Frederick (Fritz) Hasler, PhD, former leader of NASA Goddard Space Flight Center Scientific Visualization & Analysis Laboratory (creator of this iconic image), and avid CleanTechnica reader. Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.

#5762. LeanTaaS expands iQueue into ambient intelligence with LookDeep acquisition

LeanTaaS expands iQueue into ambient intelligence with LookDeep acquisition" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Healthcare technology company LeanTaaS is expanding its iQueue platform beyond traditional hospital operations and capacity management into ambient intelligence, using computer vision to monitor patient rooms and turn signals into actions.

As part of this strategy, the company plans to launch iQueue for Virtual Care, using AI-powered patient-room monitoring to detect events such as fall risks, patient movement and other environmental signals, then route those insights into operational workflow.

The system is designed to turn events like an attempted bed exit into prioritized tasks rather than additional alarms. AI monitors rooms and surfaces events to a virtual observation team, which can assess the situation, remotely engage the patient or escalate to bedside staff when needed, Mohan Giridharadas, founder and CEO of LeanTaaS, told Fierce Healthcare in an interview.

Urgency and routing are based on the patient’s context and the health system’s established protocols. Human clinicians remain responsible for clinical decisions, Giridharadas said, while iQueue provides context, prioritizes events and helps route information to the appropriate care team.

The company’s software and services are being used by nearly 200 health systems in more than 1,200 hospitals, centers and clinics in the U.S.

To accelerate this strategy, LeanTaaS acquired LookDeep Health, its second acquisition in about two months following its purchase of care transition platform Aidin. The deal brings computer vision technology, an open platform approach and virtual care deployment experience into LeanTaaS.

LeanTaaS provided Fierce Healthcare with a first look at the acquisition news. Financial details of the deal were not disclosed.

Giridharadas said the company is building the "central nervous system of the hospital," and computer vision will be a key data source for this broader hospital operations platform.

"It is a move beyond retrospective dashboards toward continuous operational awareness and execution," Giridharadas told Fierce Healthcare.

"A great operational brain needs more signal[s], so [that] it can decide faster and decide better. Health systems generate hundreds of thousands of data points each day—but the vast majority of them are discarded after they have served their immediate purpose. The next frontier is building a robust and scalable approach to capture high-value signals from the physical care environment that may never reach the EHR, or may reach it too late to be useful. That includes whether a patient is alone, trying to stand or hasn't been out of bed in days, when a nurse was last in the room, and whether a room is ready for the next patient, from compatible cameras and in-room devices, and potentially other device-generated or workflow signals," Giridharadas said.

"The test is whether a signal is sufficiently accurate and timely, and whether it can be converted into a clear action for the care team. We intend to continuously expand the sources and uses of data from the entire end-to-end patient journey," he added.

Narinder Singh, former CEO and co-founder of LookDeep who joined LeanTaaS as senior vice president of ambient intelligence, said the technology can support stretched care teams by monitoring patients when clinicians can’t be present. "Nurses and physicians cannot be in every room at every moment, and too often technology has added to their work instead of taking it away. Ambient intelligence keeps watch when they can't be there, so staff finally feel the technology works for them, not the other way around," he said in a statement.

LeanTaaS acquired LookDeep rather than partner with a computer vision or virtual care company to integrate ambient intelligence technology deeply into the iQueue platform, executives said.

"We are building the central nervous system of healthcare operations—a highly functional nervous system must have audio and video sensory capabilities in order to be effective. This is why we needed to acquire the capability rather than rely on an arms-length partnership. We already had the intelligence that drives the car, 15 years of it. What we wanted was the best perception ever built for hospitals. Nobody confuses the cameras with the car, but there is no self-driving car without them," Giridharadas said.

LookDeep brings three capabilities to LeanTaaS' platform: peer-reviewed vision AI, an open technology platform that works with hospitals’ existing infrastructure and experience deploying virtual care. Its approach also focuses on using AI to ease care team capacity constraints rather than adding to clinicians’ workloads.

Health systems can build on compatible cameras and in-room devices already in place or deploy supported hardware as needed. This open approach helps preserve existing investments while creating a foundation for new capabilities, according to the company.

"The acquisition gives us the opportunity to make ambient intelligence a native part of iQueue and extend the technology across our broader portfolio. We also share a conviction that in something as complex as a hospital, before you can be smart, you must be open. Vendor-specific camera silos will not last as health systems demand greater interoperability," Giridharadas said, noting that a camera alone "does not generate an ROI." "The value comes when what it sees changes what happens next—earlier, with the right person acting. Nobody expects the barcode scanner to redefine the supply chain, yet no supply chain runs well without one."

The new virtual care offering includes three services: virtual observation for AI-assisted patient monitoring and escalation, virtual nursing to support admissions, rounding, patient education and discharge preparation, and virtual medicine to connect patients with remote physicians and specialists.

These offerings build on LookDeep’s experience deploying the technology in health systems. Across different deployments and operating models, reported results include a more than 70% reduction in fall rates, twice as much nursing contact, an 80% reduction in medication errors and threefold cost savings compared with traditional virtual sitting solutions, according to the companies.

Beyond virtual care, LeanTaaS plans to incorporate ambient intelligence into additional iQueue products and workflows to support capacity, patient flow and care coordination.

"We will bring the vision capability into all of our products beginning with inpatient flow and operating rooms and then moving on to infusion centers and other areas of the hospital," Giridharadas said.

The company aims to find areas in hospital patient care where important signals are either missed or received too late by current systems. "We will prioritize use cases where the signal is reliable, the response is well defined and the impact can be measured," he noted.

As adoption of AI in healthcare grows, LeanTaaS says its competitive advantage comes from a proprietary knowledge base built from more than 15 years of health system deployments. "We have accumulated an enormous knowledge base of thousands of corner cases that make operational improvement and change management difficult to accomplish. We have built a multi-layered knowledge architecture of tens of thousands of 'knowledge nodes' that enables us to deliver an enterprise-grade AI capability where the data and knowledge is tightly curated to the unique needs of each site at each customer rather than relying on generic recommendations from foundation LLM models," he said.

#5761. Ayrton Senna's Honda NSX Sells For Record $1.1 Million At Auction

One of the original NSX's biggest selling points was that Honda recruited three-time Formula 1 champion Ayrton Senna to help develop the sports car. While the legendary Brazilian wasn't impressed with the NSX at first, it evolved into a mass-produced machine he eventually praised. Senna's personal 1991 Honda NSX sold for $1.17 million at RM Sotheby's auction in London last week. The sale made the late champion's car the most expensive first-generation NSX ever sold at auction, surpassing a $1.06 million mark set for a 2003 NSX-R last year.

Honda was contractually obligated to provide Senna with an NSX for his personal use. The Japanese automaker supplied three examples of the sports car, a pair in black and one in Formula Red. The red NSX, the auctioned car, was stored in Portugal and delivered to Senna when he visited the country. It's the same car that the Brazilian was photographed washing with a garden hose. The 1992 documentary "Ayrton Senna: Racing Is In My Blood" also features Senna spinning the wheels on this NSX. After Senna's death in 1994, the sports car stayed with the Portuguese dealership that owned it until 1997.

Senna's most successful period in F1 came with McLaren from 1988 until 1993. The Brazilian won three F1 world championships and 35 races over the six seasons with the British team. Honda supplied engines to McLaren, ending the partnership after the 1992 season. If by fate, McLaren conducted a preseason test at Suzuka Circuit in February 1989, just as Honda's R&D department was wrapping up its month-long NSX testing program. The engineers let Senna run laps in the sports car. According to Road & Track, Senna wasn't pleased, stating, "I'm not sure I can really give you appropriate advice on a mass-production car, but I feel it's a little fragile."

Honda went back to the drawing board and increased the NSX's chassis stiffness by 50%. The automaker even invited Senna back to test the sports car at the Nürburgring and help tune the suspension. The expert input played a significant role in the NSX's success, from marketing to the sports car's critical success. While most of us didn't have the budget to bid on Senna's NSX, Honda is doing its part to help other NSX owners keep their cars just as pristine. The Japanese automaker's heritage program now produces over 3,000 official reproduction parts to keep first-generation NSXs on the road and running smoothly.

#5760. AI detects “invisible” signs of pancreatic cancer years before diagnosis

An artificial intelligence model called REDMOD can identify extremely subtle changes in pancreatic tissue that may signal cancer long before the disease becomes visible to doctors or conventional imaging technology. According to research published online in the journal Gut, the system can detect early signs of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, the most common type of pancreatic cancer.

Researchers believe this technology could help transform pancreatic cancer detection by identifying the disease at a much earlier stage (stage 0), when treatment is more likely to succeed. Instead of receiving a diagnosis after the cancer has progressed to an advanced, potentially terminal stage, patients might have an opportunity to begin treatment while the disease is still treatable.

Although REDMOD outperformed experienced radiologists in the study, more research is necessary before it can be introduced into routine medical care. In particular, the scientists emphasize the need to test the system in patients at high risk, including those experiencing unexplained weight loss or recently diagnosed diabetes.

Why Pancreatic Cancer Is So Difficult to Detect Early

Pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma has a low survival rate, largely because it is often discovered only after the disease has advanced. Early pancreatic cancer typically produces few noticeable symptoms, and the changes it causes in pancreatic tissue can be too subtle to detect through standard medical imaging. The cancer can also progress rapidly, making timely diagnosis especially important.

To address these challenges, researchers created an artificial intelligence framework known as Radiomics-based Early Detection MODel (REDMOD). The system analyzes extremely fine patterns in the texture of pancreatic tissue, known as radiomics (radiomics), that are not readily visible on conventional computed tomography (CT) scans.

REDMOD also automatically identifies and outlines the pancreas, separating it from nearby organs and tissues. This process eliminates the need for specialists to manually trace the organ's boundaries, reducing the inconsistencies that can arise when different people perform the task.

AI Finds Hidden Cancer Signals Years Before Diagnosis

To evaluate the system, the researchers examined abdominal CT scans from 219 patients treated at multiple hospitals. At the time of their scans, experienced radiologists had found no evidence of pancreatic cancer. However, every patient in this group was subsequently diagnosed with the disease.

The scans had been taken at different intervals before diagnosis. For 87 patients (40%), the images were obtained 3 to 12 months earlier. Another 76 patients (35%) had been scanned 12 to 24 months before diagnosis, while 56 (25%) had undergone imaging more than 24 months earlier (up to around 3 years). In nearly two thirds (64%) of the patients, the cancer developed in the head of the pancreas.

The researchers compared these images with CT scans from 1243 people who remained free of pancreatic cancer for up to three years afterward. The comparison group was matched according to age, sex, and the dates of their scans.

Patients who eventually developed pancreatic cancer had an average age of 69, with ages ranging from 34 to 88. Those in the comparison group were 64 years old on average, with the same age range of 34 to 88.

REDMOD successfully identified subtle imaging signatures associated with pancreatic cancer an average of 475 days before patients received their clinical diagnoses. These early warning signs had previously gone undetected during routine radiological assessments.

"This temporal window holds profound significance, as attaining such early detection would substantially augment the probability of cure and improved survival," highlight the researchers.

"In fact, modelling studies indicate that increasing the proportion of localized [pancreatic ductal carcinomas] from 10% to 50% would more than double survival rates, thereby underscoring that the timing of diagnosis is the single most critical determinant of survival outcomes," they add.

The results also showed that REDMOD was substantially better than experienced radiologists at recognizing early cancer signals.

The AI achieved a sensitivity of 73%, compared with 39% for radiologists. Sensitivity measures how effectively a diagnostic method identifies people who actually have the disease, rather than overlooking genuine cases.

The difference was even more pronounced when researchers examined scans taken more than two years before the patients were diagnosed. In these cases, REDMOD achieved a detection rate of 68%, nearly three times the 23% recorded by radiologists.

The researchers also assessed how well the system could recognize scans from people without pancreatic cancer. In an independent group of 539 patients from several hospitals, REDMOD correctly classified just over 81% of scans as cancer free. It performed even better on the publicly available US National Institutes of Health NIH-PCT dataset (80 patients), correctly identifying 87.5% as free of pancreatic cancer.

Additional testing suggested that the early warning signs detected by REDMOD were consistent over time. When the researchers analyzed scans taken from the same patients several months apart, the system produced matching results in 90 to 92% of cases. This consistency strengthened the evidence that the AI was identifying meaningful changes associated with the disease.

Could AI Transform Early Pancreatic Cancer Detection?

Despite the promising findings, the researchers caution that several questions remain unanswered. One limitation is that the study population did not include a sufficiently diverse range of ethnic backgrounds, which could affect how well the technology performs across different populations.

The system must also undergo prospective testing, particularly among people at increased risk of pancreatic cancer, before researchers can determine whether it will be effective in everyday clinical practice.

Even with these limitations, the findings suggest that AI could eventually help doctors identify pancreatic cancer while it is still at a much more treatable stage, potentially improving survival for patients who would otherwise receive a late diagnosis.

The researchers conclude: "This study validates REDMOD as a fully automated AI framework capable of identifying the imaging signatures of stage 0 [pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma] in normal pancreas, achieving this with substantial lead times and performance superior to expert radiologists."

They add: "While prospective validation is paramount to confirm clinical utility, the REDMOD framework represents a significant advance towards shifting the paradigm for sporadic [pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma] from a late-stage symptomatic diagnosis to proactive pre-clinical interception, offering tangible hope for improving outcomes in this challenging disease."

Materials provided by BMJ Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#5759. Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger

Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

After months of securing the necessary regulatory approvals, Samaritan Health Services officially joined up with MultiCare Health System at the top of November to create an 18-hospital, $7.5 billion nonprofit health system.

The Northwestern organizations' deal, announced last year, directs $700 million from MultiCare into care services, workforce development, facility improvements and technology investments across Samaritan facilities over the coming decade. Those investments and other "longer-term" decisions will be informed by local community needs and perspectives, with decisions headed by "a locally led regional strategic planning process," they said in this week's announcement.

“MultiCare brings expertise and resources that will help us meet the changing healthcare needs of our region and support our clinicians and employees in their work every day," Marty Cahill, Samaritan's president and CEO, who has also taken on the roles of senior vice president and chief executive of MultiCare's Oregon Coast to Cascades Region, said in the announcement.

Samaritan comprises five hospitals in western Oregon, and will also remain locally led under its new parent organization, officials have said throughout the merger process. Its addition represented a geographic market expansion for the 13-hospital MultiCare.

Samaritan Health Services and MultiCare Health System have cleared all the regulatory bars necessary to complete their merger, which will cross the finish line on Nov. 1.

The nonprofits' affiliation would create an 18-hospital system with more than $7.5 billion in revenue—and give MultiCare an acute-care foothold in Oregon. Announced in 2025, it also includes a $700 million, 10-year commitment from MultiCare to invest in Samaritan's facilities, workforce and care access points.

On Monday, the pair said that all public agencies that were reviewing their deal "have either approved the transaction or announced their intent to do so." Some of these, such as the approval from the Oregon Health Authority (see below), would bring additional conditions on areas such as the reinvestment of operating margins into local care.

“We look forward to investing in the future of healthcare across Samaritan’s service area and advancing our vision to be the Pacific Northwest’s highest-value system of health,” Bill Robertson, CEO of MultiCare, said in a statement. “Together, we can build on the strengths of both organizations and invest in the future of healthcare for the people who depend on us.”

A phased integration process will begin on Nov. 1, with the organizations reassuring patients and health plan members that the process wouldn't interrupt services or coverage. Samaritan is also slated to retain its name, local leadership and community-based governance, they reaffirmed.

The Oregon Health Authority (OHA) approved a deal on Monday that would merge the nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services.

State regulators said the OHA's Health Care Market Oversight (HCMO) program reviewed how the proposed merger would affect Samaritan's facilities and its impact on Oregon citizens' access to affordable and equitable healthcare.

HCMO approved the deal provided it meets established conditions (PDF), including requiring MultiCare to follow its financial commitment of investing $700 million towards Samaritan and posting a public-facing version of the commitment on Samaritan's website. Other conditions involve reinvestment of Samaritan's operating margins into local communities and a requirement to maintain existing services.

The proposal to form a $7.5 billion, 18-hospital system still has to face review from other state programs prior to moving forward.

OHA's Coordinated Care Organization (CCO) Form A review is analyzing the deal's potential impact on InterCommunity Health Network's financial stability and ability to serve state Medicaid recipients. Moreover, the state Department of Consumer and Business Services (DCBS) is reviewing the financial and overall impact of changing control of Samaritan commercial health plans.

Samaritan President and CEO Marty Cahill said in a statement that the approval is "an important step forward for the communities we serve."

"Our affiliation with MultiCare will provide additional resources to invest in our workforce, facilities, technology and services—and help make it easier for people to receive high-quality care close to home," Cahill said.

The nonprofits will continue to operate as independent organizations until the transaction closes, and executives say they are working toward an Oct. 1 closing.

The boards of nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services have approved an affiliation they expect to consummate in mid-2026.

The arrangement would bring Samaritan, which runs five community hospitals as well as health plans and clinics in Oregon’s Benton, Lincoln and Linn counties, under the 13-hospital MultiCare—a new geographic foothold for the Tacoma, Washington-based organization.

“MultiCare and Samaritan are closely aligned in our mission, vision and values, making us ideal partners as we strengthen our networks across our service regions,” MultiCare CEO Bill Robertson said in the announcement. “It will be our privilege to be a part of the communities Samaritan serves, and we look forward to working together to support and advance health care in Oregon.”

The systems said there are no immediate changes for Samaritans’ patients, plan members or employees as the two sides move through the affiliation process.

Once the deal is finalized, Samaritan will remain “locally governed and community-based” but receive new resources from MultiCare that will expand services, update its operations and ensure sustainable care in its Oregon communities, according to the announcement.

More specifically, the deal includes a 10-year investment commitment focused on modernizing Samaritan’s facilities, expanding the flagship Good Samaritan Regional Medical Center’s inpatient capacity, adopting new technologies including telehealth capabilities and growing primary and specialty care. MultiCare’s behavioral health will also expand into Samaritan’s service area along the mid-Willamette Valley and central coast.

These changes will together increase care quality and access while helping to lower the cost of care, the organizations said.

“This affiliation represents an important step in securing the future of nonprofit health care in our region,” Marty Cahill, president and CEO of Samaritan, said in the announcement. “Our commitment has always been to remain a community-based, mission-driven organization, and this partnership strengthens that promise for decades to come. […] We are excited and honored to become MultiCare’s first health system in Oregon and to bring the benefits of MultiCare’s expertise home to the communities we serve.”

The parties said they will sign a formal affiliation agreement “in the coming weeks.”

MultiCare Health System employs more than 28,000 people across its 13 hospitals. It has 300-plus care locations in Washington, Idaho and Oregon, though no hospitals in the latter. The system reported $5.9 billion of total operating revenue but a $116.5 million operating loss (-2% operating margin) in 2024, a nearly $70 million improvement over the prior year.

Samaritan Health Services operates more than 80 physician clinics and multiple health insurance plans, serving 110,000 people, as well as its five community hospitals. In 2024, the nonprofit reported more than $1.7 billion of revenue but a $93.6 million operating loss (-0.5% operating loss).

#5758. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology. Part 2

Some reasons why you shouldn’t be worried about buying an electric vehicle.

Everyone knows that electric cars are much more inconvenient than gas cars. After all, there is a gas station on every corner and it only takes 5 minutes to fill up. Electric chargers are rare, and it takes at least an hour to charge.

Wrong: If you have access to 220 volt electricity or a L2 charger at home or work, an electric vehicle is much more convenient than a gas car for 300 miles per day of local driving.

In principal: In the evening, you drive into your garage, carport or driveway and take 10 seconds to plug in, the next morning, you have a full charge and take 10 seconds to unplug.

Compare that with a gas car. You probably have to make a couple of special trips per week to the gas station, put in your credit card, grab the smelly nozzle, put it in your car, and wait five minutes to fill. Then you return the smelly nozzle to the pump.

With the gas car, every 3,000 miles, you need to go to a Jiffy Lube and sit in their smelly waiting room for an hour while they change your oil.

With the gas car, every year, you need to go to an inspection station and wait an hour in their smelly waiting room while they do an emissions check.

These three tasks are all eliminated with an electric vehicle, which makes it much more convenient for local driving.

How do you charge at home? Most electric cars come with an EVSE (Electric Vehicle Service Equipment) cable. If not, you can buy one for ~$200. On one end of the cable is the plug that goes into your car, and on the other end is an interchangeable plug. The one that comes with most cables is a standard 110V plug, but for $40 you can buy a 220V Nema 14-50 plug. Almost all RV parks have NEMA 14–50 sockets. If you have 220V service in your garage, you can have one installed for about $200. Northern Lights Electric installed one in my garage in Three Lakes Wisconsin for $130. An electrician in Saint George, Utah, installed one in our garage there for $250. It cost about $500 to have 220V service run to my garage in Lindon, UT. I had a L2 charger installed there for another $700, which I needed for my first Nissan LEAF in 2014. The L2 charger is not necessary if you have an EVSE, you only need a NEMA 14–50 outlet.

L2 charging gives you about 28 miles of range in one hour. Most EVs these days have more than 300 miles of range — so, in principle, you will charge from dead empty to full in ~11 hours. However, I am rarely at dead empty and I normally only charge to 80%, so usually I am only charging from one to five hours. Note: If you want your battery to last as long as possible, don’t charge over 80% or let the charge go below 20%. This is easier to do if you have a bigger battery.

L1 (110V) charging only gives you about 4 miles of range per hour. In principal, if you plug in next to your shaver in the bathroom, it would take three days for a full charge. However, if you drive fewer than 100 miles/day, this works.  We have friends who would need to upgrade the electrical service to the whole house to get 220V service to the garage, and they have gotten along fine with L1 charging. This works especially well since they have a second car. They also have a clothes dryer near the garage. They are able to use its 220V service or nearby Superchargers in special cases.

What if you live in a condo or other place where you can’t install your own charger? With considerable effort, my brother was able to get 220V service for L2 charging in the garage of his condo. If you have a L2 charger at work, it solves the problem. Also, there are many Superchargers in cities, and if you live near one, you can fill up there just as you would with a gas station.

The lowest price Tesla is the Model 3 RWD with seating for five, a range of 321 miles, at a price of $37,000 (see mine in the lead photo). That is plenty of range for both local and cross-country driving. However, if you want to up the range to 363 miles, you need to get the Model 3 Premium for $47,500. For only $3,000 more, you can get the base Model Y. It costs only $40,000. (See the photos below.) With the Model Y, you get a lot more room for baggage, you ride a little higher, you get a much bigger power liftgate, and you have the option of a factory-installed receiver/tow hitch, which is the one upgrade I chose because I often carry my ebikes on a rack in back. The standard Model Y seats five, but you can get the optional YL which seats 6 in three rows of captain chairs. I upgraded from my 2019 Model 3 to a base 2026 Model Y recently. You can get the premium Model Y with better external and internal styling and 363 miles of range for the RWD Model. It also comes with numerous other features, like faster charging, better audio speakers, and a small video screen for the rear seat passengers for another $5,000, as shown below. Four-wheel drive is a $2,000 option on all models and does reduce your range a little.

Every Tesla comes equipped with smart cruise control. Unfortunately, autosteer was eliminated recently unless you pay for Full Self Driving. If there is a car in front of you, you essentially have 100% self-driving until you need to make a turn. You have full automation in a traffic jam, where the car will move forward, automatically following the car in front of you.

I’m currently driving a base 2026 Model Y with FSD V14, which now costs $99/month. I can get into my car in my garage or a big parking lot or a Supercharger and without touching anything say, Hey Grok, then verbally say: Navigate me to [nearly any business, my home, or any address I dictate to the navigation]. Then FSD will drive me automatically out of my garage or the parking lot to the location requested and park my car in a parking lot or on the street (but not yet into my garage). I sit back and “supervise,” but seldom must intervene or complain about its actions as it drives.

“Tesla has introduced a new feature called Automatic Collision Evasion, which can autonomously activate Full Self-Driving (FSD) to avoid imminent collisions, even if the driver has not engaged the FSD system. This feature aims to enhance safety by allowing the vehicle to take evasive actions during critical situations.”

Note: In my opinion, Tesla customers that don’t opt in for the $99/month FSD subscription are missing a very important, possibly live saving safety feature.

There are literally 161 different new EV models to choose from in the US today. There are also several EV models that have been discontinued that you can buy used. If you can’t stomach buying a Tesla from Elon Musk’s company, you can choose one of those.

I will present two good alternatives to buying a Tesla: The top selling EVs in the US in 2026 to date right now are as follows. You can see that the Teslas are by far the most sold, but the Hyundai IONIQ 5 and the Toyota bz are reasonable alternatives for Musk haters.

Toyota led the movement towards automobiles that are more friendly to the environment with the Prius over 20 years ago. However, they were very slow to offer a compelling pure battery electric vehicle. That has now changed, Toyota’s bz EV has received excellent reviews. I had been a big customer of Toyota gas cars until recently, so I dropped down to the nearest Toyota dealership to see the status of EVs there, as shown in the photos below. I was pleased that the dealer had four bzs in stock and I took one for a quick spin. It had the usual excellent Toyota workmanship, with spectacular acceleration like all EVs. However, the driving automation, like in the Corolla I had rented in the spring, is lame. The lane assist will only work on gentle turns.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.
